Sharp local smoothing estimates for curve averages
Shengwen Gan, Dominique Maldague, Changkeun Oh

Abstract
. We prove sharp local smoothing estimates for curve averages in all dimensions. As a corollary, we prove the sharp L p L^{p} boundedness of the helical maximal operator in ℝ 4 \mathbb{R}^{4} , which was previously known only for ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} and ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} . We also improve previously known results in higher dimensions. There are new ingredients in the proof: Fourier decay estimates and wave envelope estimates for nondegenerate curves in ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} . As a byproduct, we prove Bochner-Riesz estimates for nondegenerate curves in all dimensions.



1.
Introduction

Consider a
C^{\infty}
nondegenerate curve
\gamma_{n}:I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the average operator

(1.1)

A_{t}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t\gamma_{n}(s))\chi(s)ds.

Here,
\chi(s)
is a smooth bump function supported on a finite interval
I
. We say a curve
\gamma_{n}
is nondegenerate if

(1.2)

\det{(\gamma_{n}^{\prime}(s),\gamma_{n}^{\prime\prime}(s),\ldots,\gamma_{n}^{(n)}(s))}\neq 0

for all
s\in I
.
Our main results are sharp local smoothing estimates for curve averages in all dimensions.

Theorem 1.1
.

For
2\leq p<\infty
and
\sigma<\sigma(p,n):=\min\{\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p}),\frac{2}{p}\}
, we have

(1.3)

\Big(\int_{I}\|A_{t}f\|_{L_{\sigma}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\,dt\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C_{p,\gamma_{n},\chi}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for all functions
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

The sharp local smoothing estimate for
n=2
is proved in the celebrated work by
[
GWZ20
]
1
1

1

More precisely, a square function estimate for a cone in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is proved by
[
GWZ20
]
, and the sharp local smoothing estimate is a corollary of their square function estimate. We refer to
[
MSS92
]
for the implication.
, and has been open for
n\geq 3
. Prior to our work,
the best known bound for
n\geq 3
was obtained by
[
KLO23
]
, where they proved the estimate (
1.3
) for
p\geq 4n-2
and
\sigma<2/p
. Our theorem gives sharp local smoothing estimates for all ranges of
p
in all dimensions.

The local smoothing estimate (
1.3
) is conjectured by
[
KLO23
]
with
\sigma<\min\{\frac{1}{n},\frac{2}{p}\}
. An additional necessary example is found in
[
BH25
]
by modifying an example in
[
BGHS21
]
, and it led to the conjecture of Theorem
1.1
. Our theorem confirms their conjecture.

The local smoothing estimate can be understood better by comparing a fixed time estimate; for
2\leq p<\infty
and
\sigma<\min\{\frac{1}{p},\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p})\}
,

(1.4)

\|A_{1}f\|_{L^{p}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{p,\gamma_{n},\chi}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

This estimate is called the
L^{p}
Sobolev regularity estimate.
For
n=2
, this was proved by
[
Chr95
]
, and it has been open for a while in higher dimensions. For
n=3
, this was proved by
[
PS07
]
using the decoupling inequality of
[
BD15
]
. For
n=4
, it is obtained by
[
BGHS21
]
, and for
n\geq 5
, it is finally obtained by
[
KLO23
]
using decoupling theorems of
[
BGHS21
]
and
[
BDG16
]
. In Theorem
1.1
, we have an extra integration over
t
, and this gives an additional smoothing effect. This is why Theorem
1.1
is called the local smoothing estimate. The local smoothing estimate is first observed by
[
Sog91
]
, where he noticed Bourgain’s circular maximal function estimate follows from a local smoothing estimate.

As mentioned earlier, the main application of the local smoothing estimate is as follows.
Define the helical maximal operator

(1.5)

M_{\gamma_{n}}f(x):=\sup_{t>0}|A_{t}f(x)|.

Here is a corollary of the local smoothing estimate.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
n\geq 2
.

M_{\gamma_{n}}f
maps
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
bounded for

(1.6)

p>\begin{cases}n,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\mskip 2.0mun=2,3,4\\[2.0pt]
2(n-2),\;\;\;\;\,n\geq 5\end{cases}

It is conjectured that the
L^{p}
boundedness of
M_{\gamma_{n}}f
holds true if and only if
p>n
. The case
n=2
is proved by
[
Bou86
]
, and later by
[
MSS92
]
. For
n=3
, after the partial progress by
[
PS07
]
, it is completely proved independently by
[
KLO22
]
and
[
BGHS25
]
. Our theorem proves the conjecture for
n=4
. For
n\geq 5
, the best bound was obtained by
[
KLO23
]
, and our theorem improves theirs. We note that the range of ours is the best possible range for the
L^{p}
boundedness of the helical maximal operator if we use the local smoothing method. In particular, the sharp local smoothing can prove the sharp
L^{p}
boundedness of the helical maximal operator in dimension four but not in higher dimensions, as observed in
[
BH25
]
.

Our approach is Fourier analytic. There is a geometric approach to proving a boundedness of maximal functions; see
[
Sch98
,
HZ25
,
Zah25
]
and references there.
There has been a lot of work generalizing maximal functions to a multi-parameter setting. See
[
Zah25
,
LLO25
,
CGY25
]
for references. We also refer to
[
Sch97
,
BDH24
]
for the
L^{p}\rightarrow L^{q}
mapping properties of maximal operators.

It is well known that Theorem
1.1
implies Theorem
1.2
. We refer to Section 2 of
[
BGHS25
]
for details.

We introduce a new ingredient in a proof: a wave envelope estimate for a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. To do that, we need to introduce some notations.
For each
R\geq 1
, define the anisotropic neighborhood
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of a curve
\gamma_{n}
by

(1.7)

\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R):=\Big\{\gamma_{n}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n}^{(j)}(t):t\in[0,1],\;\;\;|\lambda_{j}|\leq R^{-\frac{j}{n}}\Big\}.

Define
\Theta^{n}(R)
to be a collection of canonical moment curve blocks at scale
R
which is a finitely overlapping cover of
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
:

(1.8)

\bigcup_{l=0}^{R^{\frac{1}{n}}-1}\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)\cap\Big\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:lR^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq\xi_{1}<(l+2)R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\Big\}.

Note that the cardinality of the collection
\Theta^{n}(R)
is comparable to
R^{\frac{1}{n}}
.
Let
\psi_{\theta}
be a smooth partition of unity subordinate to the covering
\{\theta\}
, and define
\hat{f}_{\theta}:=\hat{f}\psi_{\theta}
, and
\mathrm{BR}_{R}(f):=\sum_{\theta}f_{\theta}
.

For each
\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})
, denote the dual rectangular box of
\tau
by
\tau^{*}
. Then
\tau^{*}
has dimension
s^{-1}\times s^{-2}\times\cdots\times s^{-n}
. Define
U_{\tau,R}
to be a scaled version of
\tau^{*}
with dimension
s^{n-1}R\times s^{n-2}R\times\cdots\times R
, which roughly contains
\theta^{*}
for each
\theta\subset\tau
. Let
U\|U_{\tau,R}
be a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by translates of
U_{\tau,R}
and call each
U
a wave envelope. Define the
l^{q}
-function corresponding to the wave envelope
U
by

(1.9)

S_{U,q}^{curve}f(x):=\Big(\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}|f_{\theta}(x)|^{q}w_{U}(x)\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

The function
w_{U}
is essentially supported on
U
, and
L^{1}
-normalized in the sense that
\|w_{U}\|_{1}\sim 1
.
We refer to Subsection
4.1
for the definition of
w_{U}
. Here is a wave envelope estimate for a nondegenerate curve
\gamma_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
n\geq 2
. For
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
,
R\geq 1
, and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U,{\frac{p}{2}}}^{curve}f\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

for any Schwartz functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose Fourier supports are in
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
.

Note that Theorem
1.3
was previously known for
n=2
by
[
GWZ20
]
. Our theorem can be thought of as a generalization of a wave envelope estimate to higher dimensions. One may see that the range of
p
is sharp by using a bush example. We refer to Section
10
for the example.

As a byproduct, we prove sharp Bochner-Riesz type estimates for nondegenerate curves in all dimensions. Recall that
\mathrm{BR}_{R}(f):=\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}f_{\theta}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
n\geq 2
.
For
2\leq p<\infty
and
\epsilon>0
, we have

(1.10)

\|\mathrm{BR}_{R}(f)\|_{L^{p}}\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}\big(1+R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}+R^{\frac{1}{n}}R^{-\frac{n+1}{2p}-\frac{1}{np}}\big)\|f\|_{L^{p}}

for any Schwartz functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

The Bochner-Riesz estimate for a nondegnerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is proved by
[
Fef73
]
.
To the best of our knowledge, the Bochner-Riesz estimate for a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is first studied by
[
Pre84a
,
Pre84b
]
. We refer to Section
10
for the sharpness of Theorem
1.4
. Note that there are two critical exponents:
p=4
and
p=n^{2}+n-2
. These are precisely the critical exponents of Theorem
1.3
.

We introduce a wave envelope estimate for a cone in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
generated by a nondegenerate curve. This estimate is an ingredient in the proof of Theorem
1.3
.
Let
\gamma_{n}
be a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Define the cone
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma_{n};R)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
to be

(1.11)

\Gamma_{n}(\gamma_{n};R):=\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n}^{(j)}(t):t\in[0,1],\;\;|\lambda_{i}|\leq R^{-\frac{i-1}{n}},\;\;|\lambda_{1}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\Big\}.

Write
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma_{n};R)\subset\bigcup_{\theta\in\Xi_{n}(R)}\theta
where
\Xi_{n}(R)
is the collection of subsets
\theta
of the form

(1.12)

\theta:=\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n}^{(j)}(t):\,[t_{\theta},t_{\theta}+2R^{-\frac{1}{n}}],\;\;|\lambda_{i}|\leq R^{-\frac{i-1}{n}},\;\;|\lambda_{1}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\Big\}

and
t_{\theta}\in R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\mathbb{Z}
. Note that the cardinality of
\Xi_{n}(R)
is comparable to
R^{\frac{1}{n}}
.

For each
\tau\in\Xi_{n}(s^{-n})
, denote by the dual rectangular box
\tau^{*}
. Then
\tau^{*}
has dimension
1\times s^{-1}\times s^{-2}\times\cdots\times s^{-(n-1)}
. Define
U_{\tau,R}
to be a scaled version of
\tau^{*}
with dimension
s^{n-1}R\times s^{n-2}R\times\cdots\times R
, which roughly contains
\theta^{*}
for each
\theta\subset\tau
. Let
U\|U_{\tau,R}
be a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by translates of
U_{\tau,R}
and call each
U
a wave envelope. Define the
l^{q}
-function corresponding to the wave envelope
U
by

(1.13)

S_{U,q}^{cone}f(x):=\Big(\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}|f_{\theta}(x)|^{q}w_{U}(x)\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

Let us now state the wave envelope estimate for the cone in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
n\geq 3
. For
4\leq p\leq n^{2}-n-2
,
R\geq 1
, and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Xi_{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U,{\frac{p}{2}}}^{cone}f\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

for any Schwartz functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
.

Note that Theorem
1.5
was previously known for
n=3
by
[
GWZ20
]
. As we mentioned before, Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.5
are closely related. More precisely, Theorem
1.3
for
n-1
implies Theorem
1.5
for
n
, and Theorem
1.5
for
n
is an ingredient in the proof for Theorem
1.3
for
n
. We refer to Section
6
for the discussion.

1.1.
New ingredients in the paper

A straightforward computation shows that

(1.14)

A_{t}f(x)=(f\delta_{t=0})*\mu_{\Gamma}(x,t),

where
\mu_{\Gamma}
is a smooth surface measure on
\Gamma=\{t(\gamma_{n}(s),1):t\in[1,2]\}
. For heuristic purposes, one may essentially regard
\mu_{\Gamma}
as
1_{\Gamma}
.
Taking the Fourier transform in the
(x,t)
variables yields

(1.15)

\widehat{A_{t}f}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})=\widehat{f}(\xi)\widehat{\mu_{\Gamma}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1}).

Thus, the key task becomes understanding the behavior of the multiplier
\widehat{\mu_{\Gamma}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})
.
A classical approach is to express
\widehat{\mu_{\Gamma}}
as an oscillatory integral (see (
2.1
)) and analyze it using the Van der Corput lemma. For fixed
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})
, one studies the zeros of the
s
-derivatives (up to order
n
) of the phase function to determine the type of the point
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})
. By grouping together points of the same type, the frequency space can be decomposed into rectangular regions where
\widehat{\mu_{\Gamma}}
exhibits similar behavior.

As previously discussed, determining the type of
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})
requires solving a system of equations corresponding to the vanishing of certain functions. This analysis has been carried out explicitly in dimensions
n\leq 4
in prior work (see, for example,
[
BGHS21
,
BGHS25
]
), but it is difficult for us to extend the method to higher dimensions.

A new ingredient in this paper is a geometric framework for describing the behavior of
\widehat{\mu_{\Gamma}}
. Rather than analyzing the zeros of the derivatives of the phase function, we adopt a wave packet approach. Specifically, we first express
\widehat{\mu_{\Gamma}}
as a sum of large wave packets with dimensions

R\times\dots\times R\times R^{1/2}\times 1.

These large wave packets are then further decomposed into smaller wave packets, whose support lies in explicitly determined rectangular regions. We refer to these regions as
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-planks, denoted by
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
. This provides a complete geometric characterization of the support of
\widehat{\mu_{\Gamma}}
. The details of this construction are presented in Section
2
.

Using the structural lemmas developed in Section
2
, we establish a High/Low frequency decomposition for the planks
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
, as detailed in Section
3
. Building on the method of Guth-Wang-Zhang
[
GWZ20
]
, we further obtain an
L^{4}
wave envelope estimate associated with these planks. In
[
GWZ20
]
, the authors proved a square function estimate and, via the Nikodym maximal function estimate, ultimately derived the
L^{4}
local smoothing estimate.

However, it is not hard to see that the
L^{4}
local smoothing estimate can also be deduced directly from the wave envelope estimate, without relying on the Nikodym maximal function estimate. By applying our wave envelope estimate for the
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-planks, we obtain the
L^{4}
local smoothing estimate for curve averages.

To extend our results to
L^{p}
local smoothing estimates for
p>4
, a new ingredient is needed: an
L^{p}
wave envelope estimate for
p>4
. As mentioned earlier, although some technical challenges arise, the
L^{4}
wave envelope estimate is conceptually not difficult to obtain.
Before proceeding, let us briefly review previous results on wave envelope estimates.

The
L^{4}
wave envelope estimates are known only for the parabola and the cone in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, as established in
[
GWZ20
]
. Subsequently,
[
MG22
]
proved an
L^{4}
amplitude-dependent wave envelope estimate for the cone in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which was used to derive a small cap decoupling inequality for the cone. This, in turn, implies a small cap decoupling result for the moment curve in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
via the work of
[
GM22
]
. As a side remark, we note that a small cap decoupling for the cone in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
has applications in projection theory and the Gauss circle problem; see
[
GGM24
,
LY23
]
for details.

In the cases of the parabola in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the cone in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the wave envelope estimates are equivalent to square function estimates. However, it remains unclear whether such an equivalence holds for general nondegenerate curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n\geq 3
.

Square function estimates for nondegenerate curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
have been obtained in
[
Mal24
,
GM23
]
, but in their work, these do not follow from any known
L^{p}
wave envelope estimates for such curves. In fact, neither our Theorem
1.3
nor the square function estimates for nondegenerate curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
seem to imply each other, as can be seen from the difference in their sharp exponent ranges
2
2

2

In their work, square function estimates were established for
2\leq p\leq n(n+1)/2+1
, and the inequality fails for
p>n(n+1)/2+1
.
for
p
.

Our
L^{p}
wave envelope estimates (Theorem
1.3
) imply sharp Bochner-Riesz and local smoothing estimates for the full range of
p
. The proof of the
L^{p}
wave envelope estimates relies on ideas developed in
[
Mal24
]
, which, in turn, build on earlier work by
[
GWZ20
,
GMW24
]
.

Finally, we comment on the difference between the local smoothing estimate considered in this paper and the classical local smoothing estimate for wave equations.

Guth-Wang-Zhang
[
GWZ20
]
proved the following local smoothing estimate for wave equations:

\Big\|\sum_{\theta}F_{\theta}\Big\|_{L^{4}(B_{R}^{3})}\lessapprox R^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big\|\sum_{\theta}F_{\theta}(\cdot,0)\Big\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{2}})},

where
\textup{supp}\widehat{F}_{\theta}\subset\theta
and
\theta
are
1\times R^{-1/2}\times R^{-1}
-planks of the cone.
In this paper, we need to prove a shifted version:

\Big\|\sum_{\theta}a_{\theta}F_{\theta}(\cdot-\mathbf{n}_{\theta})\Big\|_{L^{4}(B_{R}^{3})}\lessapprox R^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big\|\sum_{\theta}F_{\theta}(\cdot,0)\Big\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{2}})},

where
|a_{\theta}|\lesssim 1,\mathbf{n}_{\theta}\in\theta^{*}
.

We emphasize that this shift does not introduce any essential difficulties. One may tile
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by rectangular boxes
\mathbb{T}_{\theta}={T}
, which are translates of
\theta^{*}
. The wave packet decomposition then takes the form

F_{\theta}=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{\theta}}F_{T},

where
F_{T}
is essentially supported in
T
.
After the shift,

F_{\theta}(\cdot-\mathbf{n}_{\theta})=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{\theta}}F_{T}(\cdot-\mathbf{n}_{\theta}).

The key observation is that
F_{T}(\cdot-\mathbf{n}_{\theta})
is now essentially supported in
2T
, which is only a constant enlargement of the original support. Hence, the wave envelope technique developed by Guth-Wang-Zhang remains applicable in the shifted setting.

1.3.
Notations.

\bullet
For a sequence
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{j}
we introduce

(1.16)

\mathop{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\displaystyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=11.00012pt}\cr$\displaystyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\textstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=11.00012pt}\cr$\textstyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=8.00009pt}\cr$\scriptstyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=6.00006pt}\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\prod$\cr}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{j}a_{i}:=\prod_{i=1}^{j}|a_{i}|^{\frac{1}{j}}.

\bullet
We use
{\rm RapDec}(R)
to denote a quantity of order
O_{M}(R^{-M})
for any
M
.

\bullet
We use
A\lesssim B
to mean that
A\leq CB
for some universal constant
C
. We use
A\lesssim_{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}}B
to mean that
A\leq C_{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}}B
for some universal constant
C_{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}}
depending on parameters
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}
.

\bullet
For a fixed scale
R>1
, we use
A\lessapprox B
to means that
A\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}B
for any
\epsilon>0
.

\bullet
We use
A\ll B
to mean that
A\leq cB
for some small enough constant
c>0
.

\bullet
For a rectangular box
P
, we use
W_{P}
to denote a weight function essentially
\sim 1
on
P
and with rapidly decaying tail outside
P
; we use
w_{P}
to denote
|P|^{-1}W_{P}
, which is the
L^{1}
normalization of
W_{P}
. The precise definition is in Section
4
. We may use
\phi_{P}
or
\chi_{P}
to denote a smooth bump function of
P
(with compact support).

\bullet
For a nonnegative function
G
, define

(1.17)

\|f\|_{L^{p}(G)}:=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(x)|^{p}G(x)\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

2.
Reduction to a local smoothing estimate

In this section, our primary goal is to reduce Theorem
1.1
to the main estimate (Theorem
2.30
). Since the main estimate requires some notation, let us postpone the statement to the end of the section.
Recall that
\gamma_{n}:I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a smooth nondegenerate curve.
We fix
n
and denote
\gamma(s)=\gamma_{n}(s)
to simplify the notation. By applying a linear transformation, we may assume that the range of
s
is
[0,1]
.
By abusing the notation, we use the notation

\gamma:=\{\gamma(s):0\leq s\leq 1\}

to denote the nondegenerate curve,
and use

\Gamma:=\{t(\gamma(s),1):1\leq t\leq 2,\;0\leq s\leq 1\}

to denote the two-dimensional cone in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
generated by
\gamma
. We remark that
s
plays the role of angular parameter, while
t
plays the role of radial parameter.

Let
\chi(s,t)
be a smooth bump function supported in
[0,1]\times[1,2]
.
We can modify the averaging operator as

Af(x,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t\gamma(s))\chi(s,t)\mathrm{d}s=(f\delta_{t=0})*\mu_{\Gamma}(x,t).

One can think of
f\delta_{t=0}
as a distribution supported in the
t=0
slice. Also,
\mu_{\Gamma}
is a measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
satisfying

(2.1)

\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-it(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\chi(s,t)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t.

One can compute

(2.2)

\begin{split}\mu_{\Gamma}(z)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-i(t(\gamma(s),1)-z)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\chi(s,t)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\xi_{n+1}\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta_{z=t(\gamma(s),1)}\chi(s,t)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

If
\chi(s,t)=1_{[0,1]\times[1,2]}(s,t)
, then the above expression is
1_{\Gamma}
.
So morally speaking, we can think of the measure
\mu_{\Gamma}
as a “smooth” surface measure supported on the two-dimensional cone
\Gamma
.

Let us recall Theorem
1.1
.

\Big(\int_{1}^{2}\|Af(x,t)\|_{L^{p}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\,dt\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

By a standard Littlewood-Paley decomposition and localization argument, it is reduced to proving the following: there exists
\epsilon>0
such that

(2.3)

\Big(\int_{B_{1}^{n}\times[1,2]}|Af(x,t)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim R^{-\sigma-\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n}})},

for every
f
satisfying
\textup{supp}\widehat{f}\subset B^{n}_{R}\setminus B^{n}_{R/2}
and
\sigma<\sigma(p,n)
.

The main goal of this section is to compute
\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})
for
|\xi|\sim R
.
We will explicitly compute
\widehat{\mu_{\Gamma}}
(see Theorem
2.29
). This is one of the crucial steps in the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Based on these decay rates, we partition the frequency space into conical regions and we estimate each region separately.

Introduce the notation
\mathbf{v}_{1}(s):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma(s)^{2}+1}}(\gamma(s),1)
for the flat direction of
\Gamma
. Since
\gamma(s)
is a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1}(s),\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\prime}(s),\dots,\mathbf{v}_{1}^{(n)}(s)
are linearly independent in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. The Gram-Schmidt process gives the Frenet basis which we denote by
\mathbf{v}_{1}(s),\dots,\mathbf{v}_{n+1}(s)
.
For convenience, we also denote
{\bf e}_{i}(s)=\mathbf{v}_{n+2-i}(s)
. It is an easy exercise to show that
{\bf e}_{1}(s),{\bf e}_{1}^{\prime}(s),\dots,{\bf e}_{1}^{(n)}(s)
are linearly independent and the Gram-Schmidt process yields the Frenet basis
{\bf e}_{1}(s),{\bf e}_{2}(s),\dots,{\bf e}_{n+1}(s)
for the curve
{\bf e}_{1}(s)
.

We actually call
\{{\bf e}_{1}(s):s\in[0,1]\}
the
dual curve
of
\{\mathbf{v}_{1}(s):s\in[0,1]\}
, and view
{\bf e}_{1}(s)
as a curve in the frequency space.
Later, we will use
{\bf e}_{1}(s),\dots,{\bf e}_{n+1}(s)
to decompose the frequency space. For the setup of the scales, we let
R\gg 1
be the largest scale. We will also use
0<\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n-1}\leq 1
to define the intermediate scales. We will discuss a dyadic decomposition at scale
R
.

2.1.
Bump functions adapted to a rectangular box

We discuss a notion that is frequently used in the paper. Let
P=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[-a_{i},a_{i}]
be a rectangular box in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We say
\phi_{P}
is a bump function adapted to
P
(or simply
\phi_{P}
is adapted to
P
), if

(1)

\phi_{P}
is supported in
C
-dilation of
P
. Here,
C
is a fixed constant.

(2)

|\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{P}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})|\lesssim_{\alpha}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+\frac{|x_{i}|}{a_{i}})^{\alpha_{i}}
holds for any multi-indices
\alpha
.

For any other rectangular box
P
, we can define a function adapted to
P
by doing rigid motion for
P
so that it is of form
\prod_{i=1}^{n}[-a_{i},a_{i}]
, and then use the above definition.

It is easy to see the following facts.

(1)

If
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}
are bump functions adapted to
P
, then
\prod_{i=1}^{m}\phi_{i}
are bump functions adapted to
P
.

(2)

If
P\subset Q
, and
\phi_{P},\phi_{Q}
are adapted to
P,Q
(respectively), then
\phi_{P}\phi_{Q}
is adapted to
P
.

If
X=\cup P
is a finite union of rectangular boxes, we say
\phi_{X}
is adapted to
X
if it can be written as
\phi_{X}=\sum_{P\subset X}\phi_{P}
where each
\phi_{P}
is adapted to
P
.

2.2.
Dyadic decomposition for a hollow rectangular box

The goal of this subsection is to introduce a way to decompose the hollow rectangular box

(2.4)

S^{(0)}:=[-1,1]^{n-1}\setminus[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{n-1}

into sub-rectangular boxes. The decomposition is through an iterative algorithm. This kind of decomposition also appeared in the study of projection theory (see, for example, in
[
GGG+22
,
GGW24
]
). We call it a hollow rectangular box because the portion near the origin is removed.

Let us discuss the heuristic. Since we use an iterative argument, let us fix
1\leq k\leq n
. Suppose
0<b_{i}<a_{i}

(1\leq i\leq k)
and we have a
k
-dimensional hollow rectangular box

\prod_{i=1}^{k}[-a_{i},a_{i}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=1}^{k}[-b_{i},b_{i}].

We partition it into three parts:

(1)

[-a_{1},a_{1}]\times\Big(\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-a_{i},a_{i}]\Big\backslash[-b_{i},b_{i}]\Big)
,

(2)

[b_{1},a_{1}]\times\Big(\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-b_{i},b_{i}]\Big)
,

(3)

[-a_{1},-b_{1}]\times\Big(\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-b_{i},b_{i}]\Big)
.

For these three parts, the first components of them are determined. We proceed to partition the remaining
(k-1)
components.

For scenario
(1)
, the remaining component is a
(k-1)
-dimensional hollow rectangular box. It is reduced to the
(k-1)
-dimensional case.

For both scenario
(2)
and
(3)
, we will partition
\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-b_{i},b_{i}]
into hollow rectangular boxes. We introduce the scales

1=d_{1}>d_{2}>\dots>d_{m}>0.

Partition

\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-b_{i},b_{i}]=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m-1}\Big(\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-d_{j}^{i-1}b_{i},d_{j}^{i-1}b_{i}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-d_{j+1}^{i-1}b_{i},d_{j+1}^{i-1}b_{i}]\Big)\bigsqcup\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-d_{m}^{i-1}b_{i},d_{m}^{i-1}b_{i}].

For
\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-d_{j}^{i-1}b_{i},d_{j}^{i-1}b_{i}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-d_{j+1}^{i-1}b_{i},d_{j+1}^{i-1}b_{i}]
which is a
(k-1)
-dimensional hollow rectangular box, we iterate the partitioning process. For
\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-d_{m}^{i-1}a_{i},d_{m}^{i-1}a_{i}]
, we stop and obtain

[b_{1},a_{1}]\times\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-d_{m}^{i-1}a_{i},d_{m}^{i-1}a_{i}]\textup{~or~}[-a_{i},-b_{i}]\times\prod_{i=2}^{k}[-d_{m}^{i-1}a_{i},d_{m}^{i-1}a_{i}].

We make the above argument precise and define the partition at scale
R
for
R\gg 1
.
Let us first introduce some notations.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
2\leq k\leq n
.
For
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k-1}
, we define the function

D_{k}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k-1})=(R\delta_{1}^{n}\delta_{2}^{n-1}\cdots\delta_{k-1}^{n+2-k})^{-\frac{1}{n+1-k}}.

Remark 2.2
.

The motivation for this definition is that if
\delta_{k}=D_{k}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k-1})
, then

\delta_{1}^{n}\cdots\delta_{k-1}^{n+2-k}\delta_{k}^{n+1-k}=R^{-1}.

Definition 2.3

(Admissible
\vec{\delta}
)
.

We consider the sequence of scales:

\mathcal{D}_{1}=\{1,2^{-1},\dots,2^{-m_{1}},R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\}=\{\delta_{1,1},\dots,\delta_{1,m_{1}+2}\},

where
m_{1}
is the largest integer such that
2^{-m_{1}}>R^{-\frac{1}{n}}
. Choose
\delta_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
.

Let
2\leq k\leq n-2
.
Suppose we have chosen
\delta_{1},\dots\delta_{k-1}
satisfying
\delta_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{i}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{i-1})
for
2\leq i\leq k
. Define

\mathcal{D}_{k}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k-1}):=\{1,2^{-1},\dots,2^{-m_{k}},D_{k}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k-1})\}=\{\delta_{k,1},\dots,\delta_{k,m_{k}+2}\},

where
m_{k}
is the largest integer such that
2^{-m_{k}}>D_{k}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k-1})
.

Finally, we let
\delta_{n-1}:=D_{n-1}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n-2})
.

The
(n-1)
-tuples
\vec{\delta}=(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n-1})
obtained from the above algorithm are called
admissible
tuples (at scale
R
).

For
\delta_{k}=\delta_{k,m}\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k-1})
(
m\leq m_{k}
), define the next scale of
\delta_{k}
to be

\delta_{k,\downarrow}:=\delta_{k,m+1}.

Remark 2.4
.

Note that when
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n-2}
are fixed, then
\delta_{n-1}
is also fixed. It is also convenient to always set
\delta_{n}=1
.
If
\delta_{k}=D_{k}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k-1})
, then
\delta_{k+1}=\dots=\delta_{n-1}=1
. One may essentially treat
\delta_{k,\downarrow}
as
\delta_{k}/2
.

Definition 2.5

(Admissible
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
)
.

Let
\vec{\delta}=(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n-1})
and
\vec{\nu}=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{n-1})\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n-1}
. We say
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
is admissible (at scale
R
) if
\vec{\delta}
is admissible (at scale
R
), and
\nu_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
when
\delta_{i}<1
.

It is also convenient to set
\nu_{n}=\nu_{n+1}=0
, which are always fixed.

We introduce an algorithm to decompose the hollow rectangular box
S^{(0)}
in (
2.4
).

Algorithm
.
Suppose
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
is admissible at scale
R
.

Step 0
.
For
1\leq i\leq n-1
, define

\rho_{i}^{(0)}:=1,\;\;\;\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(0)}:=\frac{1}{2}.

One sees that
S^{(0)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i}^{(0)},\rho_{i}^{(0)}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(0)},\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(0)}]
.

Step 1
.

Case 1
:
\nu_{1}=0
.

We let
I_{1}:=[-\rho_{1}^{(0)},\rho_{1}^{(0)}]
, and
\rho_{i}^{(1)}:=\rho_{i}^{(0)}
,
\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(1)}:=\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(0)}
for
2\leq i\leq n-1
. Let

S^{(1)}:=\bigg(\prod_{i=2}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i}^{(1)},\rho_{i}^{(1)}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=2}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(1)},\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(1)}]\bigg).

Case 2
:
\nu_{1}=1
.

We let
I_{1}:=[\rho_{1,\downarrow}^{(0)},\rho_{1}^{(0)}]
, and
\rho_{i}^{(1)}:=\delta_{1}^{i-1}\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(0)}
,
\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(1)}:=\delta_{i,\downarrow}^{i-1}\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(0)}
for
2\leq i\leq n-1
. Let

S^{(1)}:=\bigg(\prod_{i=2}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i}^{(1)},\rho_{i}^{(1)}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=2}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(1)},\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(1)}]\bigg).

Case 3
:
\nu_{1}=-1
. Everything is the same as in Case 2, except we let
I_{1}:=[-\rho_{1}^{(0)},-\rho_{1,\downarrow}^{(0)}]
.

Step
k
. Suppose we obtained

S^{(k-1)}=\bigg(\prod_{i=k}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i}^{(k-1)},\rho_{i}^{(k-1)}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=k}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k-1)},\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k-1)}]\bigg)

in Step
(k-1)
.

Case 1
:
\nu_{1}=0
.

We let
I_{k}:=[-\rho_{k}^{(k-1)},\rho_{k}^{(k-1)}]
, and
\rho_{i}^{(k)}:=\rho_{i}^{(k-1)}
,
\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k)}:=\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k-1)}
for
k+1\leq i\leq n-1
. Let

S^{(k)}:=\bigg(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i}^{(k)},\rho_{i}^{(k)}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=k+1}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k)},\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k)}]\bigg).

Case 2
:
\nu_{1}=1
.

We let
I_{k}:=[\rho_{k,\downarrow}^{(k-1)},\rho_{k}^{(k-1)}]
, and
\rho_{i}^{(k)}:=\delta_{k}^{i-k}\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k-1)}
,
\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k)}:=\delta_{k,\downarrow}^{i-1}\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k-1)}
for
k+1\leq i\leq n-1
. Let

S^{(k)}:=\bigg(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i}^{(k)},\rho_{i}^{(k)}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=k+1}^{n-1}[-\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k)},\rho_{i,\downarrow}^{(k)}]\bigg).

Case 3
:
\nu_{1}=-1
. Everything is the same as Case 2, except we let
I_{k}:=[-\rho_{k+1}^{(k)},-\rho_{k,\downarrow}^{(k)}]
.

We run the algorithm for
1\leq k\leq n-1
then stop.

Definition 2.6

(
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-planks at sclae
R
)
.

Let
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
be admissible at scale
R
. Let
I_{1},\dots,I_{n-1}
be the intervals obtained from the above algorithm.
For
1\leq i\leq n-1
,
I^{\prime}_{i}=RI_{i}
is the
R
-dilation of
I_{i}
with respect to the center
0
.

(2.5)

\begin{split}I^{\prime}_{n}&:=[-R\delta_{1}^{n-1}\delta_{2}^{n-2}\dots\delta_{n-1},R\delta_{1}^{n-1}\delta_{2}^{n-2}\dots\delta_{n-1}]\\
&=[-(\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n-1})^{-1},(\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n-1})^{-1}]\\
I^{\prime}_{n+1}&:=[-1,1].\end{split}

Define the rectangular box

(2.6)

P[\vec{\delta};\vec{\nu}]:=I_{1}^{\prime}\times\dots\times I_{n+1}^{\prime}.

The definition of
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}]
is somewhat involved. Here is a simple way to view it, which we will adopt in the paper. Let
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
an admissible pair.
Define the corresponding
\rho
-sequence as:

(2.7)

\rho_{k}:=\delta_{1}^{k}\delta_{2}^{k-1}\cdots\delta_{k}

for
1\leq k\leq n-1
.
And let
\rho_{0}:=1
,
\rho_{n}:=\delta_{1}^{n}\delta_{2}^{n-1}\cdots\delta_{n-1}^{2}=R^{-1}
. Then
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}]
is roughly the collection of
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n+1})
such that for
1\leq i\leq n-1
,

(2.8)

\begin{split}\begin{cases}|a_{i}|\lesssim R\rho_{i-1}&\textup{if~}\nu_{i}=0,\\
a_{i}\sim R\rho_{i-1}&\textup{if~}\nu_{i}=1,\\
a_{i}\sim-R\rho_{i-1}&\textup{if~}\nu_{i}=-1;\end{cases}\\
\textup{and~}|a_{n}|\lesssim R\rho_{n-1};|a_{n+1}|\lesssim R\rho_{n}=1.\end{split}

Definition 2.7
.

Let
s\in[0,1]
. Suppose
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We define

X(s):=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s):(a_{1},\dots,a_{n+1})\in X\Big\}.

In most cases,
X
is a rectangular box. For example,
if
{\bf I}=I_{1}\times\dots\times I_{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, where each
I_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval. Then

{\bf I}(s):=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s):a_{i}\in I_{i}\Big\}.

By changing coordinates, we obtain
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-plank
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
that is associated to Frenet coordinate. More precisely, we define

(2.9)

P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s):=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s):(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\in P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}]\Big\}.

Lemma 2.8

(Frenet geometry)
.

Recall the Frenet coordinate
{\bf e}_{1}(s),\dots,{\bf e}_{n+1}(s)
. Define
the nonzero functions

\kappa_{j}(s):=\langle{\bf e}_{j}^{\prime}(s),{\bf e}_{j+1}(s)\rangle\ \textup{for~}j=1,\dots,n.

Then one has the formula

(2.10)

\begin{split}&{\bf e}_{1}^{\prime}(s)=\kappa_{1}(s){\bf e}_{2}(s),\\
&{\bf e}_{i}^{\prime}(s)=-\kappa_{i-1}(s){\bf e}_{i-1}(s)+\kappa_{i}(s){\bf e}_{i+1}(s),\ i=2,\dots,n,\\
&{\bf e}^{\prime}_{n+1}(s)=-\kappa_{n}(s){\bf e}_{n}(s).\end{split}

Since the curve is fixed, we assume
|\kappa_{i}(s)|\sim 1
throughout the paper.

Lemma
2.8
is well-known, so we omit the proof here.

Remark 2.9
.

Recall that the geometric objects we want to study are boxes of form (
1.12
). They are expressed using basis
\{\gamma^{\prime}_{n},\dots,\gamma_{n}^{(n)}\}
. We may replace the basis by
\{{\bf e}_{1},\dots,{\bf e}_{n}\}
, the Frenet coordinate for the curve
\gamma_{n}
. Noting that the coefficients
\lambda_{j}
are decreasing, one can show that the old box is comparable to the new box, which is some kind of
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-plank. This type of argument appeared in the previous work (see, for example,
[
BGHS25
, Lemma 8.2]
).

Lemma 2.10

(Taylor expansion)
.

For
|\Delta|\ll 1
and
1\leq i<k\leq n+1
, then

(2.11)

\langle{\bf e}_{i}(s+\Delta),{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle=\frac{\Delta^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\kappa_{i}(s)\cdots\kappa_{k-1}(s)+O(|\Delta|^{k-i+1}).

Similarly, for
1\leq k<i\leq n+1
, then

(2.12)

\langle{\bf e}_{i}(s+\Delta),{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle=\frac{(-\Delta)^{i-k}}{(k-i)!}\kappa_{k}(s)\cdots\kappa_{i-1}(s)+O(|\Delta|^{i-k+1}).

Also,

(2.13)

\langle{\bf e}_{k}(s+\Delta),{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle=1+O(|\Delta|^{2}).

Proof.

Let us prove (
2.11
).
We use the Taylor’s theorem

{\bf e}_{i}(s+\Delta)=\sum_{j=1}^{k-i}\frac{\Delta^{j}}{j!}{\bf e}_{i}^{(j)}(s)+O(|\Delta|^{k-i+1}).

Using (
2.10
), we have

\langle\sum_{j=1}^{k-i-1}\frac{\Delta^{j}}{j!}{\bf e}_{i}^{(j)}(s),{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle=0,

and

\langle\frac{\Delta^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}{\bf e}_{i}^{(k-i)}(s),{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle=\frac{\Delta^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\kappa_{i}(s)\cdots\kappa_{k-1}(s).

This finishes the proof of (
2.11
). The proof of the other two are similar.
∎

As a next step,
we will exploit the geometric positions of the
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-planks.
We will show that if
s,s^{\prime}
are close, then they are
comparable
. Here, we say a rectangular box
U
is comparable to
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
if
U
contains and is contained in rectangular boxes of form
\{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s)\}
, where
a_{i}
satisfy (
2.8
) but with slightly different implicit constants. We make the following definition.

Definition 2.11

(Anisotropic dilation of
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-planks)
.

Let
0<C<\infty
. Recall
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}]
in (
2.8
).
We define the
C
-dilation of
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
, denoted by
CP[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
to be a plank consisting of points

\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s)
,
where

(2.14)

\begin{split}\begin{cases}|a_{i}|\leq CR\rho_{i-1}&\textup{if~}\nu_{i}=0,\\
a_{i}\in(\frac{1}{C+1}R\rho_{i-1},(1+C)R\rho_{i-1}]&\textup{if~}\nu_{i}=1,\\
a_{i}\in[-(1+C)R\rho_{i-1},-\frac{1}{C+1}R\rho_{i-1})&\textup{if~}\nu_{i}=-1;\end{cases}\\
\textup{and~}|a_{n}|\leq CR\rho_{n-1};|a_{n+1}|\leq C.\end{split}

Remark 2.12
.

For such dilation, we see that if
\nu_{i}\neq 0
, then
a_{i}
do not take
0
. This dilation should be distinguished from the isotropic dilation
\textup{Dil}_{C}(U)=CU
, which is the dilation with respect to the center of
U
by factor
C
. In the paper, unless specified, when we write
C
in front of a
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-plank or some similar things, it is the anisotropic dilation in the sense of Definition
2.11
; otherwise, it is the isotropic dilation.

Definition 2.13
.

We say a set
U
is comparable to
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
if

C^{-1}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)\subset U\subset CP[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s).

Here,
C
is a universal constant.

The following lemma tells us that if we slightly change the basis, the resulting
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-planks are morally the same.

Lemma 2.14
.

There exists a constant
K\gg 1
independent of the parameter
R
so that the following holds.
Let
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
be admissible at scale
R
. Let
s,s^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. If
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq K^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}
, then
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
and
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s^{\prime})
are comparable.

Proof.

We let
\vec{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{n-1})
be the
\rho
-sequence determined by
\vec{\delta}
as in (
2.7
), and also let
\rho_{0}=1,\rho_{n}=R^{-1}
.

Write
s^{\prime}=s+\Delta
with
0\leq\Delta\leq K^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}
. The case
\Delta<0
can be handled similarly. Let us first fix
z=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s+\Delta)\in P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s^{\prime})
. Then we write
z=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}^{\prime}{\bf e}_{i}(s)
with
a_{i}^{\prime}=\langle z,{\bf e}_{i}(s)\rangle
. To prove the lemma,
we need to show

(2.15)

\begin{split}&|\langle z,{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle-a_{k}|\ll R\rho_{k-1}\textup{~for~}1\leq k\leq n-1\,\\
&|\langle z,{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle|\lesssim R\rho_{k-1}\textup{~for~}k=n,n+1.\end{split}

If such inequalities were true, then we get the desired result. This is because by the first inequality if
a_{k}\sim R\rho_{k-1}
then
a_{k}^{\prime}\sim R\rho_{k-1}
, and if
|a_{k}|\lesssim R\rho_{k-1}
, then we have
|a_{k}^{\prime}|\lesssim R\rho_{k-1}
.

Let us prove (
2.15
). We compute

(2.16)

\begin{split}\langle z,{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle=&\langle\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s+\Delta),{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle\\
&+\langle a_{k}{\bf e}_{k}(s+\Delta),{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle+\langle\sum_{i=k+1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s+\Delta),{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle.\end{split}

Recall that
|a_{l}|\lesssim R\rho_{l}\lesssim R\rho_{k}
for
l\geq k+1
.
By Lemma
2.10
, (
2.16
) is equal to

\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\bigg(a_{i}\frac{\Delta^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\kappa_{i}(s)\cdots\kappa_{k-1}(s)+O(a_{i}\Delta^{k-i+1})\bigg)+a_{k}+O(a_{k}|\Delta|^{2})+O(\Delta R\rho_{k}).

We first deal with the
O
-term. We choose
K
sufficiently large so that if
\Delta\leq K^{-1}
then
O(\Delta R\rho_{k})\ll R\rho_{k}\leq R\rho_{k-1}
and
a_{i}O(\Delta^{k-i+1})
can be absorbed into
O(a_{i}\frac{\Delta^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\kappa_{i}(s)\cdots\kappa_{k-1}(s))
.
The main term is
a_{k}
.
The other terms are of order
O(a_{i}\Delta^{k-i})
. For
1\leq k\leq n-1
, we just need to verify for
1\leq i\leq k-1
that

a_{i}\Delta^{k-i}\ll R\rho_{k-1}.

Actually, using that
1\leq i\leq k-1\leq n-2
, we get

a_{i}\Delta^{k-i}\ll R\delta_{1}^{i-1}\cdots\delta_{i-1}(\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n-1})^{k-i}=R\delta_{1}^{k-1}\cdots\delta_{i-1}^{k-i+1}\delta_{i}^{k-i}\cdots\delta_{n-1}^{k-i}\leq R\rho_{k-1}.

This proves the first inequality of (
2.15
).

When
k=n,n+1
. Same computation yields
|\langle z,{\bf e}_{k}(s)\rangle|\lesssim R\rho_{k-1}
. We do not reproduce it here.
∎

We also prove a finite overlapping lemma. We first show the following lemma.

Lemma 2.15
.

For any
C\geq 1
, there exists
K\geq 1
so that the following holds. Fix an admissible
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
.
For
1\leq l\leq n-1
, and
s,s^{\prime}
such that
K\prod_{i=1}^{l}\delta_{i}\leq|s-s^{\prime}|\leq K^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{l-1}\delta_{i}
, then
CP[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
and
CP[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s^{\prime})
are disjoint.

Proof.

To simplify the notation, we may assume
C=1
and assume
\nu_{i}\geq 0
. Our proof works for general
C
and
\nu_{i}
. We may further assume that
\delta_{l}<1
. Otherwise, there is nothing to prove.
Write
s^{\prime}=s+\Delta
with
K\prod_{i=1}^{l}\delta_{i}\leq\Delta\leq K^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{l-1}\delta_{i}
, and
z\in P[\vec{\delta}](s^{\prime})
as

z=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s+\Delta).

Since
\delta_{l}<1
, we have
\nu_{i}
is either one or negative one, and
a_{l}\sim R\rho_{l-1}
.

We compute

(2.17)

\langle z,{\bf e}_{l+1}(s)\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\bigg(a_{i}\frac{\Delta^{l+1-i}}{(l+1-i)!}\kappa_{i}(s)\cdots\kappa_{l}(s)+a_{i}O(\Delta^{l+2-i})\bigg)+O(R\rho_{l}).

It suffices to show that if
\Delta\leq K^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{l-1}\delta_{i}
and
z\in P[\vec{\delta}](s)
, then
\Delta<K\prod_{i=1}^{l}\delta_{i}
, which is a contradiction. Since
z\in P[\vec{\delta}](s)
, we have

(2.18)

\langle z,{\bf e}_{l+1}(s)\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\bigg(a_{i}\frac{\Delta^{l+1-i}}{(l+1-i)!}\kappa_{i}(s)\cdots\kappa_{l}(s)+a_{i}O(\Delta^{l+2-i})\bigg)\lesssim R\rho_{l}.

Note that
|\kappa_{i}(s)|\sim 1
and
\Delta\leq K^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{l-1}\delta_{i}
. We claim that for
1\leq i\leq l-1
, we have

a_{i}\Delta^{l+1-i}\ll a_{l}\Delta.

This is true since

a_{i}\Delta^{l-i}\leq K^{-(l-i)}(\delta_{1}^{i-1}\cdots\delta_{i-1})(\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{l-1})^{l-i}\ll\delta_{1}^{l-1}\cdots\delta_{l-1}\sim a_{l}.

As a result, we obtain

a_{l}\Delta\lesssim R\rho_{l},

which implies

\Delta\lesssim\frac{R\rho_{l}}{a_{l}}\sim\prod_{i=1}^{l}\delta_{i}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
C\geq 1
.
Let
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
be admissible at scale
R
. Let
\delta=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}
, and
\mathbb{I}_{\delta}
be a set of
\delta
-separated points in
[0,1]
. Then
\{CP[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)\}_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}
are
O_{C}(1)
-overlapping. In other words,

\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}1_{CP[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{C}1.

Proof.

For simplicity, let us assume that
C=1
.
The proof relies on Lemma
2.15
.
For each number
0<\beta\leq 1
, let
\mathcal{J}_{\beta}
be a collection of intervals of length
\beta
that form a finitely overlapping cover of
[0,1]
. If
J\subset[0,1]
is an interval, we denote
\mathbb{I}_{\delta}(J):=\mathbb{I}_{\delta}\cap J
.

Define
\beta_{1}:=1
. For
2\leq l\leq n
, let
\beta_{l}:=\prod_{i=1}^{l-1}\delta_{i}
.We have

(2.19)

\begin{split}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}1_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}&\lesssim K\sup_{J_{1}\in\mathcal{J}_{K^{-1}\beta_{1}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}(J_{1})}1_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\\
&\lesssim K\sup_{J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}_{K\beta_{2}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}(J_{2})}1_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}.\end{split}

Here, the first inequality is by the triangle inequality; in the second inequality, we used Lemma
2.15
with
l=1
. Similarly, we have

(2.20)

\begin{split}\sup_{J_{l}\in\mathcal{J}_{K\beta_{l}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}(J_{l})}1_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}&\lesssim K^{2}\sup_{J_{l}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{K^{-1}\beta_{l}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}(J_{l}^{\prime})}1_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\\
&\lesssim K^{2}\sup_{J_{l+1}\in\mathcal{J}_{K\beta_{l+1}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}(J_{l+1})}1_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}.\end{split}

Iterating and noting that
K
is a fixed number independent of
\delta_{i}
, we obtain

\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}1_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sup_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta_{n}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}(J)}1_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\leq 1.

The last inequality is because
\beta_{n}\leq\delta
, and hence
\#\mathbb{I}_{\delta}(J)\leq 1
for
J\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta_{n}}
.
∎

The next lemma is the comparison of
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-planks at different scales. Recall the definition of an isotrophic dilation in Remark
2.12
.

Lemma 2.17
.

There exists
K,C\geq 1
so that the following holds.
Let
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
be admissible at scale
R
, and
\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}\leq\sigma<1
.
Then there exists
(\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime})
admissible at scale
r\leq R
, such that
\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}^{\prime}=\sigma
and

\bigcup_{s^{\prime}:|s^{\prime}-s|\leq K^{-1}\sigma}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)\subset\textup{Dil}_{\frac{R}{r}}\big(CP[\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime}](s)\big)

for any
s\in[0,1]
. Here,
P[\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime}]
is a plank of dimensions
r\times r\rho^{\prime}_{1}\times\dots\times r\rho^{\prime}_{n-1}\times 1
as defined in (
2.8
), where
\rho_{i}^{\prime}=(\delta^{\prime}_{1})^{i}\cdots\delta^{\prime}_{i}
and
r=(\delta_{1}^{\prime})^{-n}\cdots(\delta^{\prime}_{n-1})^{-2}
.

Proof.

We construct
\vec{\delta}^{\prime}
by adjusting
\vec{\delta}
. It is easy to see that there exists
1\leq l\leq n-1
and
\delta_{l}\leq\delta_{l}^{\prime}<1
such that
\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{l-1}\delta_{l}^{\prime}=\sigma
. We let

\vec{\delta}^{\prime}:=(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\delta_{n-1}^{\prime}):=(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{l-1},\delta_{l}^{\prime},1\dots,1),

and let
\vec{\nu}^{\prime}:=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{l},0,\dots,0)
.
It is straightforward to see that
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)\subset\frac{R}{r}P[\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime}](s)
. By Lemma
2.14
, if
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq K^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}^{\prime}=K^{-1}\sigma
, then
P[\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime}](s)
and
P[\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime}](s^{\prime})
are comparable. Hence, we see that

P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s^{\prime})\subset\textup{Dil}_{\frac{R}{r}}\big(CP[\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime}](s)\big)

for
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq K^{-1}\sigma
.
∎

Remark 2.18
.

From Lemma
2.17
and Lemma
2.16
, we see that

\Big\{\textup{conv}(\bigcup_{s^{\prime}:|s^{\prime}-s|\leq\sigma}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s))\Big\}_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}

are
O(1)
-overlapping. Here
\textup{conv}(X)
is the convex hull of
X
.

2.3.
Computing the decay rate

The goal of this subsection is to obtain an expression of
\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})
for
|\xi|\sim R
. Recall

\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})=\int_{[0,1]\times[1,2]}e^{-it(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\chi(s,t)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t,

where
\chi(s,t)
is a smooth bump function supported in
[0,1]\times[1,2]
.
The following algorithm is designed to compute it.

Denote by
\mathcal{D}=\{\vec{\delta}\}
the set of admissible
(n-1)
-tuples at scale
R
.
Fix a sequence
R^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\Delta_{1}<\Delta_{2}<\dots<\Delta_{N+1}=R^{-\frac{1}{n}}
. Here,
\Delta_{k}=R^{-\frac{1}{2}+(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{n})\frac{k-1}{N}}
and
N
is a sufficiently large number, which will be determined later
3
3

3

We use the fact that
N
is sufficiently large several times; we use it in the proof of Lemma
2.28
and also in Subsection
2.4
.

Definition 2.19
.

Define
\Delta(\vec{\delta}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}
.

We set
\vec{\delta}_{0}:=(1,\dots,1,R^{-1/2})
and
\vec{\delta}_{N}:=(R^{-1/n},1,\dots,1)
. We see that

\inf_{\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}}\Delta(\vec{\delta})=\Delta(\vec{\delta}_{0})=R^{-1/2},\ \ \sup_{\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}}\Delta(\vec{\delta})=\Delta(\vec{\delta}_{N})=R^{-1/n}.

Definition 2.20
.

Define

(2.21)

\mathcal{D}_{1}:=\{(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n-1})\in\mathcal{D}:\Delta(\vec{\delta})\in[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}]\}.

For
2\leq k\leq N
, define

\mathcal{D}_{k}:=\{(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n-1})\in\mathcal{D}:\Delta(\vec{\delta})\in(\Delta_{k},\Delta_{k+1}]\}.

Remark 2.21
.

If
\vec{\delta}=(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n-1})
and
\vec{\delta}^{\prime}=(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\delta_{n-1}^{\prime})
with
\delta_{i}\leq\delta_{i}^{\prime}
for
1\leq i\leq n-2
, then
\Delta(\vec{\delta})\geq\Delta(\vec{\delta}^{\prime})
. In other words,

\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\cdots\delta_{n-2}D_{n-1}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n-2})\geq\delta_{1}^{\prime}\delta_{2}^{\prime}\cdots\delta_{n-2}^{\prime}D_{n-1}(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\delta_{n-2}^{\prime}).

We first partition the range of the
s
variable. Let
K
be the number in Lemma
2.14
. For
0\leq k\leq N
, let
{\bf I}_{k}=\{I_{k}\}
be a set of disjoint intervals of length
K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
that form a partition of
[0,1]
. Recall the definition of
P[\vec{\delta};\vec{\nu}](s)
in (
2.8
).

Let us next define the rectangular boxes centered at the origin that will play a role later in a proof.
We use
\textup{Proj}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
to denote the projection onto the first
n-1
coordinates.
By the decomposition for a hollow rectangular box, we have

(2.22)

[-R,R]^{n-1}\Big\backslash[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{n-1}=\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}}\textup{Proj}(P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}]).

Definition 2.22

(Cluster of planks)
.

For
1\leq k\leq N
, define

(2.23)

Q_{k}=\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta}\in\sqcup_{k\leq j\leq N}\mathcal{D}_{j},\vec{\nu}}\textup{Proj}(P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}]).

Here,
\vec{\nu}
under
\bigsqcup
is over those such that
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
is admissible.

The next lemma is to help digesting the geometry of
Q_{k}
.

Lemma 2.23
.

If
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1})\in Q_{k}
, then

\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}[-a_{i},a_{i}]\subset Q_{k}\bigcup[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{n-1}.

Proof.

By symmetry, we may assume
a_{i}\geq 0
for each
i
.
By the definition of
Q_{k}
, we suppose
\vec{a}\in\textup{Proj}(P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}])
for some
\vec{\delta}\in\sqcup_{k\leq j\leq N}\mathcal{D}_{j}
. We just need to show that for each
\vec{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1})
with
0\leq b_{i}\leq a_{i}
, we have
\vec{b}\in Q_{k}\bigcup[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{n-1}
.

If
\vec{b}\notin[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{n-1}
, then by (
2.22
) there exists an admissible pair
(\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime})
such that
\vec{b}\in\textup{Proj}(P[\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime}])
. Since
\vec{b}
is closer to the origin than
\vec{a}
is, we have that
P[\vec{\delta}^{\prime},\vec{\nu}^{\prime}]
is closer to the origin than
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}]
is. Recall the size of
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-planks in (
2.8
), we have
\delta_{i}\geq\delta_{i}^{\prime}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
. By Remark
2.21
,
\Delta(\vec{\delta})\leq\Delta(\vec{\delta}^{\prime})
. Hence,
\vec{\delta}^{\prime}\in\sqcup_{k\leq j\leq N}\mathcal{D}_{j}
. This means that
\vec{b}\in Q_{k}
.
∎

Figure 1.

Remark 2.24
.

We can roughly draw the picture of
Q_{k}
as in Figure
1
.
Q_{1}=[-R,R]^{n-1}\setminus[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{n-1}
. When
k
is bigger,
Q_{k}
becomes smaller.

Definition 2.25

(Thickened planks)
.

We define the cluster of rectangular boxes thickened in the
n,(n+1)
th directions.

Q_{k}[L,J]:=Q_{k}\times[-L,L]\times[-J,J].

Similarly, we define

P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};L,J]:=\textup{Proj}(P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}])\times[-L,L]\times[-J,J].

We see that

(2.24)

Q_{k}[L,J]=\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta}\in\sqcup_{k\leq j\leq N}\mathcal{D}_{j},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};L,J].

We would like to call each sub-rectangular boxes on the RHS a
block
of the LHS. If
X
is a finite union of blocks
X=\sqcup P
, we say
\phi_{X}
is adapted to
X
if
\phi_{X}=\sum_{P\subset X}\phi_{P}
where each
\phi_{P}
is adapted to
P
.

We have

(2.25)

Q_{k}[L,J]\Big\backslash Q_{k+1}[L,J]=\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{k},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};L,J].

We partition the range of the
s
variable. Let
K
be the number in Lemma
2.14
. For
1\leq k\leq N
, let
{\bf I}_{k}=\{I_{k}\}
be a set of disjoint intervals of length
K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
that form a partition of
[0,1]
.

For
1\leq k\leq N
, we let
\{\varphi_{I_{k}}\}_{I_{k}\in{\bf I}_{k}}
be a set of bump functions such that
\varphi_{I_{k}}
is supported in
2I_{k}
and
\sum_{I_{k}}\varphi_{I_{k}}=1
on
[0,1]
. We can also assume the derivative estimates

(2.26)

\|\partial^{m}\varphi_{I_{k}}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{m}(K^{-1}\Delta_{k})^{-m},

for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We have a bump function
\varphi_{I_{k}}
adapted to
I_{k}
.

For
I_{i}\in{\bf I}_{i}
, we define

(2.27)

\varphi_{I_{k},I_{k+1},\dots,I_{N}}=\prod_{i=k}^{N}\varphi_{I_{i}}.

We see that
\varphi_{I_{k},I_{k+1},\dots,I_{N}}
also satisfies the derivative estimate (
2.26
). So it is also a bump function adapted to
I_{k}
.

It is convenient to introduce the following notion. For
I_{i}\in{\bf I}_{i},I_{i+l}\in{\bf I}_{i+l}
(
l\geq 1)
, we write

I_{i}<I_{i+l}
, if
\textup{supp}\varphi_{I_{i}}\cap\textup{supp}\varphi_{I_{i+l}}\neq\emptyset
. we see that (
2.27
) is nonzero only when
I_{k}<\cdots<I_{N}
.

Define

(2.28)

\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1}):=\int_{[0,1]\times[1,2]}e^{-it(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\chi(s,t)\varphi_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t.

We recall that
{\bf e}_{n+1}(s)=\frac{(\gamma(s),1)}{\sqrt{|\gamma(s)|^{2}+1}}
.
Noting that
\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\varphi_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}=1
on
[0,1]
, we have the decomposition

(2.29)

\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}=\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}.

Let
\beta=\frac{1}{N^{1000}}
be a small number to handle with rapidly decreasing tail.

As explained in (
2.2
), one can think of
\mu_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}
as
1_{\Gamma(I_{1})}
. Here for interval
I
,
\Gamma(I)
denotes
\{t(\gamma(s),1):s\in I,t\in[1,2]\}
which is a sector of
\Gamma
. We will also use
s_{I}
to denote the center of
I
. Recall that
I_{1}
is an interval with sidelength
R^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Note that
\Gamma(I_{1})
is contained in a translate of the rectangular box

\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s_{I_{1}}):|a_{i}|\lesssim R^{-1}(1\leq i\leq n-1),|a_{n}|\lesssim R^{-1/2},|a_{n+1}|\lesssim 1\Big\}.

Hence,
\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}1_{|\xi|\sim R}
is essentially supported in its dual rectangular box minus
[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{n+1}
, i.e.,
Q_{1}(s_{I_{1}})
.
The proof can be made rigorous by method of stationary phase. We sketch the idea here but omit the detail.
Recall that

\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1}):=\int_{[0,1]\times[1,2]}e^{-it(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\chi(s,t)\varphi_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t.

We only consider
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\in B_{R}^{n+1}
. Choose a small number
\beta\ll\frac{1}{N}
to deal with the
rapidly decaying tail. (If one wants to ignore technical difficulties, one may view
\beta=0
.)
By integration by parts in variable
s
, we see that
\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}
is essentially supported in

\{(\xi,\xi_{n+1}):|\gamma^{\prime}(s)\cdot\xi|\lesssim R^{1/2+\beta/3}\textup{~for~some~}s\in I_{1}\}.

Since
|I_{1}|\lesssim R^{-1/2}
, the above set is contained in

\{(\xi,\xi_{n+1}):|\gamma^{\prime}(s_{I_{1}})\cdot\xi|\lesssim R^{1/2+\beta/3}\}.

Furthermore, by the integration by parts in the variable
t
, we see that
\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}
is essentially supported in

\{(\xi,\xi_{n+1}):|\gamma^{\prime}(s_{I_{1}})\cdot\xi|\lesssim R^{1/2+\beta/3},\;|(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})|\lesssim R^{\beta/2}\textup{~for~some~}s\in I_{1}\}.

Note that
|\gamma^{\prime}(s_{I_{1}})\cdot\xi|\lesssim R^{1/2+\beta/3}
implies
|\gamma^{\prime}(s)\cdot\xi|\lesssim R^{1/2+\beta/3}
for
s\in I_{1}
, which is equivalent to that
|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})|\lesssim R^{1/2+\beta/3}
. Therefore, the above set is contained in

\{(\xi,\xi_{n+1}):|\gamma^{\prime}(s_{I_{1}})\cdot\xi|\lesssim R^{1/2+\beta/3},\;|(\gamma(s_{I_{1}}),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})|\lesssim R^{\beta/2}\}.

Note that
{\bf e}_{n+1}(s)\|(\gamma(s),1)
, and
{\bf e}_{n}(s)=a_{s}(\gamma^{\prime}(s),0)+b_{s}{\bf e}_{n+1}(s)
for some
|a_{s}|\sim 1,|b_{s}|\lesssim 1
.
We see that
\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}1_{|\xi|\sim R}
is essentially supported in

\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s_{I_{1}}):|a_{i}|\leq R(1\leq i\leq n-1),|a_{n}|\leq R^{1/2+\beta},|a_{n+1}|\leq R^{\beta}\Big\}\big\backslash[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{n+1},

which is contained in
Q_{1}[R^{1/2+\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{1}})
.

Let
\eta(\xi)
be a smooth function so that
\eta=1
on
B^{n}_{1}(0)
and
\eta
is supported in
B^{n}_{2}(0)
. Let
\eta_{R}=\eta(\frac{\cdot}{R})-\eta(\frac{\cdot}{R/2})
be the Littlewood-Paley cutoff. Recall Subsection
2.1
. By (
2.29
), we have

(2.30)

\begin{split}\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\eta_{R}(\xi)=&\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\Big(\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\\
&\times\phi_{Q_{1}[R^{\frac{1}{2}+\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{1}})}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\Big)\eta_{R}(\xi)+{\rm RapDec}(R),\end{split}

where
\phi_{Q_{1}[R^{1/2+\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{1}})}
is adapted to
Q_{1}[R^{1/2+\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{1}})
. Recall that

Q_{1}[R^{1/2+\beta},R^{\beta}]=\Big([-R,R]^{n-1}\setminus[-R/2,R/2]\Big)^{n-1}\times[-R^{1/2+\beta},R^{1/2+\beta}]\times[-R^{\beta},R^{\beta}].

Recall also that
\mathrm{RapDec}(R)
is defined in Subsection
1.3
.

Our goal is to show that
\phi_{Q_{1}[R^{1/2+\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{1}})}
in (
2.30
) can be “essentially ” replaced by the characteristic function of

(2.31)

\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s_{I_{1}})=\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta(\vec{\delta})^{-1},1](s_{I_{1}}).

Compared with

Q_{1}[R^{1/2},1](s_{I_{1}})=\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};R^{1/2},1](s_{I_{1}}),

we have a narrower support in
{\bf e}_{n}
direction (
\Delta(\vec{\delta})^{-1}\leq R^{1/2}
). This is because when we sum over
\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}
, we get cancellation in certain region.

Fix a sequence
I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}
. Let us state a key lemma. For
1\leq k\leq N
, we will construct functions
\phi_{k},\phi_{k}^{\prime},\psi_{k}
so that the following holds. (We remark that
\phi_{k}^{\prime}
is not the derivative of
\phi_{k}
, but another function.)

Lemma 2.26

(Partition of unity)
.

There exist functions
\{\phi_{I_{k}}\}_{1\leq k\leq N,I_{k}\in{\bf I}_{k}}
,
\{\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k}}\}_{2\leq k\leq N,I_{k}\in{\bf I}_{k}}
,
\{\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}\}_{1\leq k\leq N-1,I_{k}\in{\bf I}_{k},I_{k+1}\in{\bf I}_{k+1},I_{k}<I_{k+1}}
, so that the following holds.

(1)

\phi_{I_{k}}
is a bump function adapted to
Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
;

(2)

\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k}}
is a bump function adapted to
Q_{k}[\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
;

(3)

\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}
is a bump function adapted to

\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{k},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}});

(4)

If
I_{k}<I_{k+1}
, then

(2.32)

\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k+1}}+\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}=\phi_{I_{k}}.

(5)

If
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\in\textup{supp}(\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k}}-\phi_{I_{k}})
, then

(2.33)

|(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\cdot{\bf e}_{n}(s)|\gtrsim\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},

for all
|s-s_{I_{k}}|\leq K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
.

Figure 2.

Proof.

We roughly describe the geometry of the support of
\phi_{I_{k}},\phi_{I_{k}}^{\prime},\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}
. See Figure
2
.
\phi_{I_{k}}
is morally a bump function of the big cross minus the central square
[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{n+1}
;
\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k+1}}
is morally a bump function of the small cross minus
[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{n+1}
;
\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}
is morally a bump function of the difference of them;
\phi_{I_{k+1}}
is morally a bump function of the small cross but with thinner thickness in
{\bf e}_{n}
direction.

We first construct
\phi_{I_{1}}
. Note that
Q_{1}[\Delta_{1}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{1}})=Q_{1}[R^{1/2+\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{1}})
. We let

\phi_{I_{1}}:=\phi_{Q_{1}[R^{1/2+\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{1}})}

which is the bump function on the RHS of (
2.30
).

Suppose we have constructed
\phi_{I_{k}}
, supported in
Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
.
4
4

4

To be rigorous, the support is in a dilation of
Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
. But to keep our notation simple, we omit the dilation factor.

Let
I_{k}<I_{k+1}
. To construct
\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k+1}},\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}
so that (
2.32
) holds, we consider partitioning
Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
. Note that by (
2.25
) we have the partition

(2.34)

Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})=Q_{k+1}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{k},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}}).

We want to replace the angular parameter
s_{I_{k}}
of
Q_{k+1}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
by
s_{I_{k+1}}
.
Note that

Q_{k+1}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}]=\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta}\in\sqcup_{k+1\leq j\leq N}\mathcal{D}_{j},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}].

We need the following lemma which is similar to Lemma
2.14
.

Lemma 2.27
.

If
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
and
\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{j}
for
k\leq j\leq N
, then for any
R^{\beta}\leq L\leq\Delta_{k}^{-2}
,
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};L,R^{\beta}](s)
and
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};L,R^{\beta}](s^{\prime})
are comparable. In particular, it holds for
L=\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta}
and
\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta}
.

Proof.

If we replace
(\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta})
by
(\Delta(\vec{\delta})^{-1},1)
, then the rectangular box becomes
P[\vec{\delta};\vec{\nu}](s)
and
P[\vec{\delta};\vec{\nu}](s^{\prime})
. By Lemma
2.14
, they are comparable. Now we thickened their two shortest sides and still want to show they are comparable. Actually, besides the cases checked in the proof of Lemma
2.14
, it remains to check

L\Delta^{n-l}\ll R\rho_{l-1}

for
1\leq l\leq n-1
, where
\Delta=|s-s^{\prime}|
.
By monotonicity, we just need to check for
l=n-1
.
This is true since

R\rho_{n-2}=\Delta(\vec{\delta})^{-1}\geq\Delta_{k}^{-1}\gg L\Delta.

This completes the proof.
∎

Since
|s_{I_{k}}-s_{I_{k+1}}|\leq K^{-1}\Delta_{k+1}
,
by the above lemma (with
k
replaced by
k+1
) we see that each block of
Q_{k+1}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
is comparable to the corresponding block of
Q_{k+1}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k+1}})
. We may deduce from (
2.34
) that

Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})\subset Q_{k+1}[100\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},100R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k+1}})\cup\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{k},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}}).

Choose the bump functions
\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k+1}}
adapted to
Q_{k+1}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k+1}})
and
\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}
adapted to
\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{k},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
so that

\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k+1}}+\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}=\phi_{I_{k}}.

This verifies (
2.32
).

Suppose we have constructed
\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k}}
adapted to
Q_{k}[\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
. Define
\phi_{I_{k}}
by narrowing the support of
\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k}}
in the
n
th direction, so that
\phi_{I_{k}}
is adapted to
Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
. More precisely, we may define

\phi_{I_{k}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1}):=\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\chi((\xi,\xi_{n+1})\cdot{\bf e}_{n}(s_{I_{k}})),

where
\chi
is a bump function adapted to
[-100\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},100\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta}]
. We see that if
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\in\textup{supp}(\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k}}-\phi_{I_{k}})
, then (
2.33
) holds for
s=s_{I_{k}}
. This means

(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\notin Q_{k}[100\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}}).

(Here,
100
should be a bounded constant that is big enough to make (
2.35
) holds. We simply denote it by
100
to avoid introducing more notation.)

By Lemma
2.27
, we see that when
|s-s_{I_{k}}|\leq K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
, then the blocks of
Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s)
are comparable to the corresponding blocks of
Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
. We may assume

(2.35)

Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s)\subset Q_{k}[100\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}}),

and hence

(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\notin Q_{k}[\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s),

which implies that

|(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\cdot{\bf e}_{n}(s)|\gtrsim\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta}

for any
|s-s_{I_{k}}|\leq K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
.
∎

We prove a cancellation lemma. This enables us to replace
\phi_{I_{k}}^{\prime}
with
\phi_{I_{k}}
.

Lemma 2.28
.

For
k=2,\dots,N
, we have

(2.36)

\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\big(\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})-\phi_{I_{k}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\big)={\rm RapDec}(R).

Proof.

In the proof, we will use Lemma
A.1
which was proven in
[
BGHS25
, Appendix D]
.
By the supports of
\phi_{I_{k}}^{\prime}
and
\phi_{I_{k}}
, and (
2.33
), we need to show that for any
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\in Q_{k}[\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
satisfying

|(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\cdot{\bf e}_{n}(s)|\gtrsim\Delta_{k}^{-1}R^{\beta}

for any
s\in I_{k}
, we have
|\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})|={\rm RapDec}(R)
.

Recall the definition in (
2.28
):

\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})=\int_{[0,1]\times[1,2]}e^{it(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\chi(s,t)\varphi_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t,

where
\varphi_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}
is defined in (
2.27
). Hence,
\varphi_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}
is adapted to
I_{k}
. Fix
t\in[1,2]
and denote
\phi(s)=t(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})
and
a(s)=\chi(s,t)\varphi_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}(s)
.
We need to show

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\phi(s)}a(s)\mathrm{d}s\Big|={\rm RapDec}(R).

It suffices to verify the three conditions of Lemma
A.1
. Note that
\textup{supp}\ a\subset 2I_{k}
which is an interval of length
K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
. We choose
r=R^{\beta}
in Lemma
A.1
.
For any
j\geq 0
, easy computation gives

(2.37)

\begin{split}&|\phi^{\prime}(s)|\sim|{\bf e}_{n}(s)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})|\gtrsim\Delta_{k}^{-1}r\\
&|a^{(j)}(s)|\lesssim(K^{-1}\Delta_{k})^{-j}\lesssim r^{-j}|\phi^{\prime}(s)|^{j}.\end{split}

It remains to verify for
|s-s_{I_{k}}|\leq K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
that

(2.38)

|\phi^{(j)}(s)|\lesssim r\Delta_{k}^{-j}\lesssim r^{-(j-1)}|\phi^{\prime}(s)|^{j}\;\;\textup{~for~}j\geq 2.

Since the second inequality is shown, it suffices to prove the first inequality. Suppose that
|s-s_{I_{k}}|\leq K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
.
Writing
\phi(s)=tb(s){\bf e}_{n+1}(s)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})
, where
b(s)=\sqrt{|\gamma(s)|^{2}+1}
is a bounded smooth function,
for any
2\leq j\leq n
, we have

|\phi^{(j)}(s)|\lesssim\sum_{i=n+1-j}^{n+1}|{\bf e}_{i}(s)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})|.

Since
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\in Q_{k}[\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
, there exists
\vec{\delta}\in\bigcup_{k\leq l\leq N}\mathcal{D}_{l}
and
\vec{\nu}
such that

(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\in P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{k}})
. By Lemma
2.27
,
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})
is contained in a dilation of
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s)
since
|s-s_{I_{k}}|\leq K^{-1}\Delta_{k}
. Let
(\rho_{i})
be the
\rho
-sequence of
\vec{\delta}
in (
2.7
). We have

\sum_{i=n+1-j}^{n+1}|{\bf e}_{i}(s)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})|\lesssim\sum_{i=n+1-j}^{n-1}R\rho_{i-1}+\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta}+R^{\beta}.

To show that this is bounded by
\lesssim r\Delta_{k}^{-j}
, we need to show

R\rho_{n-j}+\Delta_{k-1}^{-1}R^{\beta}\lesssim R^{\beta}\Delta_{k}^{-j}.

Since
j\geq 2
, we have
\Delta_{k}^{j}\leq\Delta_{k}^{2}\leq\Delta_{k}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq\Delta_{k-1}
. Note that in the last inequality we used
N\geq n/2
. Also, we have

R\rho_{n-j}=\delta_{1}^{-n}\cdots\delta_{n-1}^{-2}\cdot\delta_{1}^{n-j}\cdots\delta_{n-j}\leq\Delta(\vec{\delta})^{-j}\leq\Delta_{k}^{-j}.

The reason for the last inequality is because
\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{l}
for some
l\geq k
. We finished the proof of (
2.38
) when
2\leq j\leq n
. For
j\geq n
, we have

|\phi^{(j)}(s)|\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}|{\bf e}_{i}\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})|\lesssim r\Delta_{k}^{-n}.

Hence, the three conditions in Lemma
A.1
are satisfied. This finishes the proof.
∎

We recall (
2.30
) as follows:

(2.39)

\eta_{R}\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}=\eta_{R}\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}\phi_{I_{1}}+{\rm RapDec}(R).

We will derive the following result.

Theorem 2.29

(Fourier decay rate of a cone spanned by moment curve)
.

We have

(2.40)

\eta_{R}\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}=\eta_{R}\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}+\phi_{I_{N}}\bigg)+{\rm RapDec}(R).

Proof.

We inductively show

(2.41)

\eta_{R}\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}=\eta_{R}\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{I_{i},I_{i+1}}+\phi_{I_{k+1}}\bigg)+{\rm RapDec}(R),

for
k=0,\dots,N-1
. (
2.39
) gives
k=0
. Suppose it has been proved for
k-1
:

(2.42)

\eta_{R}\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}=\eta_{R}\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\psi_{I_{i},I_{i+1}}+\phi_{I_{k}}\bigg)+{\rm RapDec}(R).

We deal with the term

\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}\phi_{I_{k}}=\sum_{I_{k}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}\phi_{I_{k}}.

Here, we used that
\sum_{(I_{1},\dots,I_{k-1}):I_{1}<\dots<I_{k}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}=\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}
.
By the item 4 of Lemma
2.26
, we write

\sum_{I_{k}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}\phi_{I_{k}}=\sum_{I_{k}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}(\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k+1}}+\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}).

By Lemma
2.28
,

\displaystyle\sum_{I_{k}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}\phi^{\prime}_{I_{k+1}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{I_{k}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{k},\dots,I_{N}}\phi_{I_{k+1}}+{\rm RapDec}(R)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}\phi_{I_{k+1}}+{\rm RapDec}(R).

This finishes the proof for the case
k
.
∎

2.4.
Reduction of the local smoothing estimate

Let
\sigma=\sigma_{p}=\frac{1}{n}
, when
2\leq p\leq 4
;
\sigma=\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p})
when
4\leq p\leq 4(n-1)
;
\sigma=\frac{2}{p}
when
p\geq 4(n-1)
.
Recall that our goal is to prove that for
2\leq p\leq\infty

(2.43)

\Big\|\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\Big)^{\vee}\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n+1}})}\lesssim R^{-\sigma+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n}})}.

Here we assume
\textup{supp}\widehat{f}\subset B_{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R/2}^{n}
and
(\xi,\xi_{n+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
.
When
p=2
or
\infty
, (
2.43
) is straightforward. By interpolation, it suffices to consider
p\in[4,4(n-1)]
.
We are going to do a sequence of reductions.

By Theorem
2.29
, we have

\widehat{f}(\xi)\widehat{\mu}_{\Gamma}=\widehat{f}(\xi)\sum_{I_{1}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{1},\dots,I_{N}}\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\psi_{I_{k},I_{k+1}}+\phi_{I_{N}}\bigg)+\hat{f}(\xi){\rm RapDec}(R).

By the triangle inequality, it suffices to fix
m\in[1,N-1]
and prove

\Big\|\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\sum_{I_{m}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{m},\dots,I_{N}}\psi_{I_{m},I_{m+1}}\Big)^{\vee}\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n+1}})}\lesssim R^{-\sigma+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n}})}.

The term including
\phi_{I_{N}}
can be handled similarly as
\psi_{I_{N-1},I_{N}}
.
For fixed
I_{m}
, there are
O(1)
many
I_{m+1},\dots,I_{N}
such that
I_{m}<\dots<I_{N}
. Define

\widehat{\mu}_{I_{m}}:=\sum_{I_{m}<\dots<I_{N}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{m},\dots,I_{N}}.

We have

(2.44)

\begin{split}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{m}}(\xi,\xi_{n+1})&=\int_{[0,1]\times[1,2]}e^{-it(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\chi(s,t)\chi_{I_{m}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{[s_{I_{m}}-\Delta_{m},s_{I_{m}}+\Delta_{m}]\times[1,2]}e^{-it(\gamma(s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\chi(s,t)\chi_{I_{m}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

where
\chi_{I_{m}}
is a bump function adapted to
I_{m}
.
Since
\psi_{I_{m},I_{m+1}}
is adapted to

\bigsqcup_{\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{m},\vec{\nu}}P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{m}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{m}}),

we can write

\psi_{I_{m},I_{m+1}}=\sum_{\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{m},\vec{\nu}}\phi_{\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu},s_{I_{m}}},

where
\phi_{\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu},s_{I_{m}}}
is adapted to
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{m}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s_{I_{m}})
.
Since there are
(\log R)^{O(1)}
many choices of
\vec{\delta}
and
O(1)
choices of
\vec{\nu}
. We may fix an admissible
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
with
\vec{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{m}
, and it suffices to prove

(2.45)

\Big\|\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\sum_{I_{m}\in{\bf I}_{m}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{m}}\phi_{\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu},s_{I_{m}}}\Big)^{\vee}\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n+1}})}\lesssim R^{-\sigma+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n}})}.

Denote

\delta:=\Delta(\vec{\delta})=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}.

We have
\delta\in[\Delta_{m},\Delta_{m+1})=[\Delta_{m},R^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{n})\frac{1}{N}}\Delta_{m})
. Therefore,

P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu};\Delta_{m}^{-1}R^{\beta},R^{\beta}](s)
and
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
are the same up to a factor
R^{O(\frac{1}{N})}
.
We may choose
\frac{1}{N}=\epsilon^{10000}
so that the factor
R^{O(\frac{1}{N})}
is dominated by
R^{\epsilon}
. Hence, we may omit the factor
R^{\frac{1}{N}}
, and (
2.45
) becomes

(2.46)

\Big\|\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\sum_{I_{m}\in{\bf I}_{m}}\widehat{\mu}_{I_{m}}\phi_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s_{I_{m}})}\Big)^{\vee}\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n+1}})}\lesssim R^{-\sigma+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n}})},

where
\phi_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s_{I_{m}})}
is a bump function adapted to
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s_{I_{m}})
.

Recall (
2.44
), we use Minkowski’s inequality for variables
s,t
. Then (
2.46
) is bounded by

(2.47)

\int_{[-\Delta_{m},\Delta_{m}]\times[1,2]}\|F(\cdot,\cdot;s,t)\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n+1}})}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

where
F(x,x_{n+1};s,t)
is defined by

\begin{split}\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\sum_{I_{m}\in{\bf I}_{m}}e^{it(\gamma(s_{I_{m}}+s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\chi(s_{I_{m}}+s,t)\chi_{I_{m}}(s_{I_{m}}+s)\phi_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s_{I_{m}})}\Big)^{\vee}(x,x_{n+1}).\end{split}

Hence, by taking
\sup
over
s,t
variables, (
2.46
) boils down to showing

(2.48)

\Big\|\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\sum_{I_{m}\in{\bf I}_{m}}a_{I_{m}}e^{it(\gamma(s_{I_{m}}+s),1)\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\phi_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s_{I_{m}})}\Big)^{\vee}\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\Delta_{m}^{-1}R^{-\sigma+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n}})}

for all
a_{I_{m}}\in\mathbb{C}
with
|a_{I_{m}}|\lesssim 1
,
1\leq t\leq 2
and
|s|\leq\Delta_{m}
.

We further simplify our notation. Recall that
\delta\in[\Delta_{m},\Delta_{m+1})
. By losing a factor
\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\Delta_{m}}\lesssim R^{1/N}
, we may assume the summation
I_{m}\in{\bf I}_{m}
is over
\delta
-separated
I_{m}
. Let
\mathbb{I}_{\delta}
be a set of
\delta
-separated points in
[0,1]
. Then (
2.48
) boils down to proving

(2.49)

\Big\|\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}a_{s}e^{i\mathbf{n}_{s}\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\phi_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big)^{\vee}\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta^{-1}R^{-\sigma+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n}})},

where
|a_{s}|\lesssim 1
,
\mathbf{n}_{s}\in\{t(\gamma(s^{\prime}),1):t\in[1,2],s^{\prime}\in[s-\delta,s+\delta]\}
.
Since we have

\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)e^{i\mathbf{n}_{s}\cdot(\xi,\xi_{n+1})}\phi_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big)^{\vee}=\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\phi_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big)^{\vee}(\cdot-\mathbf{n}_{s}),

(
2.49
) becomes

(2.50)

\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}a_{s}\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\phi_{P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)}\Big)^{\vee}(\cdot-\mathbf{n}_{s})\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta^{-1}R^{-\sigma+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{1}^{n}})}.

Finally, we do a rescaling for (
2.50
). We dilate by
R
in physical space and dilate by
R^{-1}
in frequency space. Denote
p(s):=\mathrm{Dil}_{R^{-1}}(P(s))
which is a
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-plank after
R^{-1}
-dilation. Note that
p_{s}
has dimensions
1\times\rho_{1}\times\dots\times\rho_{n-1}\times R^{-1}
. Also use
\phi_{p(s)}
to denote a bump function adapted to
p(s)
. For
s\in[0,1]
, define
\Gamma(s):=\{t(\gamma(s),1):t\in[1,2]\}
.

The main result is stated as follows:

Theorem 2.30

(Main estimate)
.

Fix an admissible pair
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
at scale
R
. Let
\delta=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}
. Let
\mathbb{I}_{\delta}
be a
\delta
-separated subset of
[0,1]
. For
4\leq p\leq 4(n-1)
and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}a_{s}\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\phi_{p(s)}\Big)^{\vee}(\cdot-R\mathbf{n}_{s})\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta^{-1}R^{\frac{1}{p}}R^{-\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p})+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{R}^{n}})}

for any function
f
satisfying
\widehat{f}\subset B_{1}^{n}\setminus B_{\frac{1}{2}}^{n}
,
|a_{s}|\lesssim 1
, and
\mathbf{n}_{s}\in\Gamma(s)
.

We have shown that Theorem
2.30
implies Theorem
1.1
. In Sections 3-8, we prove Theorem
2.30
.

3.
Kakeya-type estimates for all intermediate scales

Fix
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
that is admissible at scale
R
. Let
\vec{\rho}
be the corresponding
\rho
-sequence. We have defined
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
-planks
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
that are of dimensions
R\times R\rho_{1}\times\dots\times R\rho_{n-1}\times 1
.
Recall that
\rho_{k}=\delta_{1}^{k}\delta_{2}^{k-1}\cdots\delta_{k}
.

Define
Q[\vec{\delta}]
to be
the translated copy of
\textup{Proj}(P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}])
that is centered at the origin. Roughly speaking,
Q[\vec{\delta}]
can be viewed as the following set.

\Big\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}):|a_{i}|\lesssim R\rho_{i-1},1\leq i\leq n-1\Big\}.

Let us fix
\vec{\delta}
. Denote
\delta=\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n-1}
.
Our goal is to do a
high/low decomposition
for
Q[\vec{\delta}]
.
Eventually we will obtain the decomposition

Q[\vec{\delta}]=\bigsqcup_{\sigma}Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma].

Here,
\sigma
ranges over dyadic numbers in
[\delta,1]
.

Intuition.

Before giving the precise definition, let us talk about the intuition.
Let
\sigma
be a dyadic number ranging in
\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1
. We want to study the set

\Theta[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s):=\bigcap_{|s^{\prime}-s|\leq\sigma}Q[\vec{\delta}](s^{\prime}).

Morally speaking,
\Theta[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s)
is a rectangular box centered at the origin.
We further want to compute the size of
\Theta[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s)
. Write
z\in Q[\vec{\delta}](s+\sigma)
of the form

z=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s+\sigma).

Using Taylor’s expansion with Lemma
2.8
, we have
{\bf e}_{i}(s+\sigma)\approx\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}O(\sigma^{|k-i|}){\bf e}_{k}(s)
. Rewrite as follows.

z\approx\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}(\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\sigma^{|k-i|}a_{k}){\bf e}_{i}(s).

If
z
is also in
