Q[\vec{\delta}](s)
, then we expect

\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\sigma^{|k-i|}a_{k}\leq R\rho_{i-1}.

We may assume
a_{i}\geq 0
for simplicity, and obtain the range:

a_{k}\leq R\min_{k\leq i\leq n+1}\{\sigma^{k-i}\rho_{i-1}\}.

We list as follows.

(1)

a_{1}\leq R\min\{\rho_{0},\sigma^{-1}\rho_{1},\sigma^{-2}\rho_{2},\dots\sigma^{-n}\rho_{n}\}
,

(2)

a_{2}\leq R\min\{\rho_{1},\sigma^{-1}\rho_{2},\sigma^{-2}\rho_{3},\dots\sigma^{-(n-1)}\rho_{n}\}
,

(3)

…

(4)

a_{n}\leq R\min\{\rho_{n-1},\sigma^{-1}\rho_{n}\}
,

(5)

a_{n+1}\leq 1
.

Note that the sequence
\{\sigma^{k-i}\rho_{i-1}\}_{i\in[k,n+1]}
is log-concave (this can be verified by using any consecutive three numbers). The range becomes

a_{k}\leq R\min\{\rho_{k-1},\sigma^{k-n-1}\rho_{n}\}.

The critical values of
\sigma
naturally arise by setting
\rho_{k-1}=\sigma^{k-n-1}\rho_{n}=\sigma^{k-n-1}R^{-1}
.
Define
\sigma_{k}
to be such that the equality holds. In other words,

(3.1)

\sigma_{k}:=\Big(\frac{\rho_{n}}{\rho_{k-1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{n+1-k}}=\delta\delta_{k+1}^{-\frac{1}{n+1-k}}\delta_{k+2}^{-\frac{2}{n+1-k}}\cdots\delta_{n-1}^{-\frac{n-1-k}{n+1-k}}.

One can compute
\sigma_{n}=\sigma_{n-1}=\delta
.
Though, we only need to care about
\sigma_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq n-2
. And we note that

\delta\leq\sigma_{n-2}\leq\sigma_{n-3}\leq\dots\leq\sigma_{1}\leq 1.

It is convenient to set
\sigma_{0}=1
. We may also assume all
\sigma_{i}
are dyadic.

Remark 3.1
.

Guth-Wang-Zhang’s high/low decomposition corresponds to
n=2
,
\delta=R^{-1/2}
, in which no critical values
\sigma_{i}
need to be considered.

Definition 3.2
.

For dyadic
\sigma\in[\delta,1]
, define

Q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma]:=\{(a_{i}):|a_{i}|\leq\min\{\sigma^{i-n-1},R\rho_{i-1}\}\,(1\leq i\leq n+1)\}.

In particular,
Q[\vec{\delta};\leq 1]=Q[\vec{\delta}]
. Define

Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma]:=Q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma]\big\backslash Q[\vec{\delta};\leq 2\sigma],

for
\sigma\in(\delta,1]
, and

Q[\vec{\delta};1]:=Q[\vec{\delta};\leq 1],

is a unit cube.

We have the decomposition

Q[\vec{\delta}]=\bigsqcup_{\sigma}Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma].

Remark 3.3
.

Morally speaking,
Q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma](s)
is
\Theta[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s)
. One should think that
Q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma]
gets smaller when
\sigma
gets bigger from the definition of
\Theta[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s)
.

Remark 3.4
.

If
\sigma\in[\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k-1})
, we have

Q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma]:=\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}[-R\rho_{i-1},R\rho_{i-1}]\times\prod_{i=k}^{n+1}[-\sigma^{i-n-1},\sigma^{i-n-1}].

and

Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma]:=\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}[-R\rho_{i-1},R\rho_{i-1}]\times\bigg(\prod_{i=k}^{n}[-\sigma^{i-n-1},\sigma^{i-n-1}]\Big\backslash\prod_{i=k}^{n}[-(2\sigma)^{i-n-1},(2\sigma)^{i-n-1}]\bigg)\times[-1,1].

Our next goal is the following finite overlapping lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Fix
\sigma\in(\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k-1}]
.
Let
\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}
be a
\sigma
-separated subset of
[0,1]
. Then
\{Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s):s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}\}
is
(\log R)^{O(1)}
-overlapping. In other words,

\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}1_{Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim(\log R)^{n}.

Proof.

We partition
Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma]
into
\lesssim(\log R)^{n}
rectangular boxes
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}[\vec{\delta};\sigma]=\{U\}
:

Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma]=\bigsqcup_{U\in\mathcal{U}}U.

Then we just need to show that for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
,
\{U(s):s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}\}
is
O(1)
-overlapping.

We give the definition of
U
.
Recall that
Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma]
is of form

\{(a_{i}):|a_{i}|\leq R\rho_{i-1}(1\leq i\leq k-1),|a_{i}|\lesssim\sigma^{i-n-1}(k\leq i\leq n+1),\exists k\leq i\leq n+1:|a_{i}|\sim\sigma^{i-n-1}\}.

We can partition
Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma]
into rectangular boxes

(3.2)

Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma]=\bigsqcup_{k\leq m\leq n}V_{m,+}\sqcup V_{m,-},

where

(3.3)

\begin{split}V_{m,+}=\{(a_{i}):|a_{i}|\leq R\rho_{i-1}(1\leq i\leq k-1),|a_{i}|\leq(2\sigma)^{i-n-1}(k\leq i\leq m-1),\\
(2\sigma)^{m-n-1}<a_{m}\leq\sigma^{m-n-1},|a_{i}|\leq\sigma^{i-n-1}(m+1\leq i\leq n+1)\},\end{split}

and similarly by changing the sign of
a_{m}
we get
V_{m,-}
.
For convenience, we may think of
V_{m,+}
as

\{(a_{i}):|a_{i}|\lesssim R\rho_{i-1}(1\leq i\leq k-1),|a_{i}|\lesssim\sigma^{i-n-1}(k\leq i\leq n+1),a_{m}\sim\sigma^{m-n-1}\}.

Next, we partition each
V_{m,+}
(and
V_{m,-}
) further into pieces.

We discuss a general setting. Suppose we are given dyadic numbers
r_{1}\geq r_{2}\geq\dots\geq r_{m}>0
and a rectangular box

D=\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}[-r_{i},r_{i}]\times[\frac{1}{2}r_{m},r_{m}].

We claim that we can partition
D
as:

D=\bigsqcup_{\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}D_{\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}.

Here,
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m-1})
are dyadic numbers with
\mu_{i+1}\leq\mu_{i}\leq r_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq m-1
) and we set
\mu_{m}=r_{m}
;
\vec{\iota}=(\iota_{1},\dots,\iota_{m-1})
satisfies
\iota_{i}\in\{-1,0,1\}
, and
\iota_{i}=0
if
\mu_{i}=\mu_{i+1}
;
and

D_{\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}=\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}I_{i}\times[\frac{1}{2}r_{m},r_{m}],

where

(3.4)

I_{i}=\begin{cases}[-\mu_{i},\mu_{i}]&\iota_{i}=0,\\
(\frac{1}{2}\mu_{i},\mu_{i}]&\iota_{i}=1,\\
[-\mu_{i},-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{i})&\iota_{i}=-1.\end{cases}

Actually, the way we obtain these
D_{\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}
is through an algorithm starting from the
(m-1)
th component to the
1
st component. We briefly discuss it. Starting with the
(m-1)
th component, we partition
[-r_{m-1},r_{m-1}]
into dyadic intervals

[-r_{m-1},r_{m-1}]=\bigsqcup_{i=0}^{l}(2^{-i-1}r_{m-1},2^{-i}r_{m-1}]\sqcup[-2^{-i}r_{m-1},-2^{-i-1}r_{m-1})\bigsqcup[-r_{m},r_{m}],

where
2^{l+1}=\frac{r_{m-1}}{r_{m}}
.
If we choose
(2^{-i-1}r_{m-1},2^{-i}r_{m-1}]
to be the
(m-1)
th component, we set
\mu_{m-1}=2^{-i}r_{m-1},\iota_{m-1}=1
; If we choose
[-2^{-i}r_{m-1},-2^{-i-1}r_{m-1})
to be the
(m-1)
th component, we set
\mu_{m-1}=2^{-i}r_{m-1},\iota_{m-1}=-1
; If we choose
[-r_{m},r_{m}]
to be the
(m-1)
th component, we set
\mu_{m-1}=r_{m},\iota_{m-1}=0
. We continue this process until we obtain
\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}
, and hence hence we obtain
D_{\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}
.

Returning back to (
3.2
),
We partition the first
m
components of
V_{m,\pm}
using the the above algorithm:

V_{m,\pm}=\bigsqcup_{\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}V_{m,\pm;\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}.

Hence, we obtain the partition

Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma]=\bigsqcup_{k\leq m\leq n}\bigsqcup_{\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}V_{m,+;\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}\sqcup V_{m,-;\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}=:\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{U}}U.

We fix a
U=V_{m,+;\vec{\mu},\vec{\iota}}
and verify that
\{U(s):s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}\}
is
O(1)
-overlapping. We may assume all
\iota_{i}\geq 0
. It is convenient to set
\mu_{m}=\sigma^{m-n-1},\mu_{m+1}=\sigma^{m-n}
. We use the rough form of
U
as

(3.5)

\{(a_{i}):a_{i}\prec\mu_{i}(1\leq i\leq m-1),a_{i}\sim\sigma^{m-n-1},|a_{i}|\lesssim\sigma^{i-n-1}(m+1\leq i\leq n+1)\},

where
\prec
means either
\sim
or
\lesssim
, and if it means
\lesssim
, then we must have
\mu_{i}=\mu_{i+1}
.

We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
U
be given by (
3.5
).
For fixed
1\leq l\leq m
, if
\frac{\mu_{l+1}}{\mu_{l}}\ll|s-s^{\prime}|\ll\min\{1,\frac{\mu_{2}}{\mu_{1}},\dots,\frac{\mu_{l}}{\mu_{l-1}}\}
(the upper bound is
1
when
l=1
), then

U(s)\bigcap U(s^{\prime})=\emptyset.

Proof.

If
\mu_{l+1}=\mu_{l}
, then there are no
s,s^{\prime}
such that
1\ll|s-s^{\prime}|\ll 1
, so the conclusion automatically holds. Therefore, we assume
\mu_{l}<\mu_{l+1}
, and hence
a_{l}\sim\mu_{l}
.

Write
s^{\prime}=s+\Delta
with
\Delta\ll\min\{1,\frac{\mu_{2}}{\mu_{1}},\dots,\frac{\mu_{l}}{\mu_{l-1}}\}
. Suppose by contradiction that
z\in U(s^{\prime})\bigcap U(s)
. Express
z
as

z=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}a_{i}{\bf e}_{i}(s+\Delta),

where
a_{i}
satisfies the range in (
3.5
).

We compute

(3.6)

\langle z,{\bf e}_{l+1}(s)\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\bigg(a_{i}\frac{\Delta^{l+1-i}}{(l+1-i)!}\kappa_{i}(s)\cdots\kappa_{l}(s)+a_{i}O(\Delta^{l+2-i})\bigg)+O(\mu_{l+1}).

Since
z\in U(s)
, we have
|\langle z,{\bf e}_{l+1}(s)\rangle|\lesssim\mu_{l+1}
. We will derive a contradiction by showing

\Delta\lesssim\frac{\mu_{l+1}}{\mu_{l}}.

Note that
|\kappa_{i}(s)|\sim 1
and
\Delta\ll\min\{1,\frac{\mu_{2}}{\mu_{1}},\dots,\frac{\mu_{l}}{\mu_{l-1}}\}
. We claim that for
1\leq i\leq l-1
, we have

a_{i}\Delta^{l+1-i}\ll a_{l}\Delta.

This is true since

a_{i}\Delta^{l-i}\ll\mu_{i}\prod_{j=i}^{l-1}\frac{\mu_{j+1}}{\mu_{j}}=\mu_{l}\sim a_{l}.

Plugging into (
3.6
), we see
a_{l}\Delta
dominates, and hence using
|\langle z,{\bf e}_{l+1}(s)\rangle|\lesssim\mu_{l+1}
, we get

a_{l}\Delta\lesssim\mu_{l+1},

which implies

\Delta\lesssim\frac{\mu_{l+1}}{a_{l}}\sim\frac{\mu_{l+1}}{\mu_{l}}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Now it remains to show

(3.7)

\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}1_{U(s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim 1.

The proof relies on Lemma
3.6
. We assume the implicit constant in “
\ll
” in Lemma
3.6
is
K
. For each number
0<\beta\leq 1
, let
\mathcal{J}_{\beta}
be a collection of intervals of length
\beta
that form a finitely overlapping cover of
[0,1]
. If
J\subset[0,1]
is an interval, we denote
\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}(J):=\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}\cap J
.

For
2\leq l\leq m-1
, let
\beta_{l}=\min\{1,\frac{\mu_{2}}{\mu_{1}},\dots,\frac{\mu_{l}}{\mu_{l-1}}\}
. (
\beta_{1}=1
.) We have

\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}1_{U(s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim K\sup_{J_{1}\in\mathcal{J}_{K^{-1}\beta_{1}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}(J_{1})}1_{U(s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim K\sup_{J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}_{K\beta_{2}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}(J_{2})}1_{U(s)}\Big\|_{\infty}.

Here, the first inequality is by the triangle inequality; in the second inequality, we used Lemma
3.5
with
l=1
. Similarly, we have

(3.8)

\begin{split}\sup_{J_{l}\in\mathcal{J}_{K\beta_{l}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}(J_{l})}1_{U(s)}\Big\|_{\infty}&\lesssim K^{2}\sup_{J_{l}\in\mathcal{J}_{K^{-1}\beta_{l}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}(J_{l})}1_{U(s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\\
&\lesssim K^{2}\sup_{J_{l+1}\in\mathcal{J}_{K\beta_{l+1}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}(J_{l+1})}1_{U(s)}\Big\|_{\infty}.\end{split}

Iterating and noting
K\lesssim 1
, we get

\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}1_{U(s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sup_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta_{m+1}}}\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}(J)}1_{U(s)}\Big\|_{\infty}\leq 1.

The last inequality is because
\beta_{m+1}\leq\frac{\mu_{m+1}}{\mu_{m}}=\sigma
, and hence
\#\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}(J)\leq 1
for
J\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta_{m+1}}
.
∎

We state a geometric result which is similar to Lemma
2.14
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose
\sigma\in[\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k-1})
and
1\leq m\leq k-1
. Let
Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma]
be defined as above.
If
|s-s^{\prime}|\ll\sigma
, then
Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s)
and
Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s^{\prime})
are comparable.

Proof.

We follow the same procedure as in Lemma
2.14
. Some cases have been verified in the proof of Lemma
2.14
. Let
z\in Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma;m+]
, we just need to verify
|\langle z,{\bf e}_{j}(s)\rangle|\lesssim\sigma^{j-n-1}
for
i\leq k-1,j\geq k
. By Taylor expansion, it is equivalent to verify
R\rho_{i-1}\Delta^{j-i}\lesssim\sigma^{j-n-1}
for any
i\leq k-1,j\geq k,\Delta\ll\sigma
. In other words, we verify
R\rho_{i-1}\lesssim\sigma^{i-n-1}
for
i\leq k-1
. This is true since
\sigma\leq\sigma_{k-1}
.
∎

For
0<\sigma\leq 1
, let
\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}
be a maximal set of
\sigma
-separated points in
[0,1]
. We temporarily fix a
\vec{\delta}
and may assume all components of
\vec{\nu}
are
\geq 0
.
To simplify our notation, we will temporarily drop
\vec{\delta}
and
\vec{\nu}
from the notation in the rest of the section. (We may still need to keep
\vec{\delta}
in the latter sections.) Hence,
P[\vec{\delta}](s)
,
Q[\vec{\delta}]
,
Q[\vec{\delta};\sigma](s)
,
Q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma](s)
are denoted by
P(s)
,
Q(s)
,
Q[\sigma](s)
,
Q[\leq\sigma](s)
. We also use the small letter to denote its
R^{-1}
-dilation;
p(s),q(s),q[\sigma](s),q[\leq\sigma](s)
. For a rectangular box
U
, we use
U^{*}
to denote the dual rectangular box (which is centered at the origin). For each
\sigma
and
s
, we tile
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
by translates
\{U\}
of
q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})^{*}
, for which we denote as
U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}
.

We have the following Kakeya-type estimate.

Proposition 3.8
.

Suppose that
\{g_{s}\}_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}
satisfy
\textup{supp}\widehat{g}_{s}\subset q(s)
. Then we have

(3.9)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}g_{s})^{2}\lesssim\delta^{-\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}|U|(\int\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|g_{s}|w_{U})^{2}.

Recall that
w_{U}\sim\frac{1}{|U|}
in
U
and decays rapidly away from
U
.

Proof.

Recall that we have the partition

(3.10)

q(s)=\bigsqcup_{\sigma}q[\sigma](s),

where
q[\sigma](s)=q[\leq\sigma](s)\big\backslash q[\leq 2\sigma](s)
when
\sigma<1
;
q[1](s)=q[\leq 1](s)
is a
R^{-1}
-cube. Let
\phi_{q[\leq\sigma](s)}
be a bump function adapted to
q[\leq\sigma](s)
. Let
\phi_{q[\sigma](s)}=\phi_{q[\leq\sigma](s)}-\phi_{q[\leq 2\sigma](s)}
, which is a bump function adapted to
q[\sigma](s)
.
\{\phi_{q[\sigma](s)}\}_{\sigma}
is a partition of unity subordinated to (
3.10
). We have

\widehat{g}_{s}=\sum_{\sigma}\widehat{g}_{s}\phi_{q[\sigma](s)}.

Using Plancherel, the LHS of (
3.9
) is

\lesssim(\log R)^{O(1)}\sum_{\sigma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}\widehat{g}_{s}\phi_{q[\sigma](s)})^{2}.

Using Lemma
3.5
, it is bounded by

(3.11)

\displaystyle\lesssim(\log R)^{O(1)}\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}\widehat{g}_{s}\phi_{q[\sigma](s)})^{2}

(3.12)

\displaystyle\leq(\log R)^{O(1)}\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|g_{s}|*|\phi_{q[\sigma](s)}^{\vee}|)^{2}.

It is easy to see
q[\leq 2\sigma](s)
and
q[\leq\sigma](s)
are comparable.
By Lemma
3.7
,
q[\leq\sigma](s)
is comparable to
q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})
if
|s-s^{\prime}|\ll\sigma
. It is harmless to assume
q[\leq\sigma](s)
is comparable to
q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})
if
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma
by losing a constant factor. We see that

|\phi_{q[\sigma](s)}^{\vee}|\lesssim w_{q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})^{*}}.

Here,
w_{q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})^{*}}

\sim\frac{1}{|q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})^{*}|}
on
q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})^{*}
and decays rapidly away from
q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})^{*}
.

Therefore,

(3.13)

(
3.12
)

\displaystyle\lesssim(\log R)^{O(1)}\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|g_{s}|*w_{q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})^{*}})^{2}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\lesssim(\log R)^{O(1)}\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime})^{*}}|U|(\int\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|g_{s}|w_{U})^{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

We will use the following form of the Kakeya-type estimates.

Proposition 3.9
.

For
2\leq q<\infty
,
R\geq 1
, and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\int_{B_{R}}(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}|f_{s}|^{q}*w_{p(s)^{*}})^{2}\lesssim R^{\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}:\\
U\subset B_{R}\end{subarray}}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|f_{s}|^{q}w_{U}\Big)^{2}

for any functions
\{f_{s}\}_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}
satisfying
\textup{supp}\widehat{f}_{s}\subset p(s)
.

Proposition 3.10
.

For any
2\leq q<\infty
,
R\geq 1
, and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\int_{B_{R}}(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{q}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{2}\lesssim R^{\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\|U_{\tau,R}\\
U\subset B_{R}\end{subarray}}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}|f_{\theta}|^{q}w_{U}\Big)^{2}.

We postpone the proofs of the propositions to Section
5.3
, where we prove some interpolation lemmas.

4.
l^{p/2}
function estimates

In this section, we prove the wave envelop estimate for the curve (Theorem
1.3
) and for the cone (Theorem
1.5
) from the
l^{p/2}
-function estimates (Theorem
4.2
and Theorem
4.3
). Before we begin the discussion, let us first give a precise definition of weight functions.

4.1.
Weight functions

Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty)
be a radial, smooth bump function supported in
|\xi|\leq 1/4
and satisfying
|\widecheck{\phi}(x)|>0
for all
|x|\leq 1
. For any
d\in\mathbb{N}
, define
W^{n,d}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty)
by

W^{n,d}(x):=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{100n^{2}jd}}|\widecheck{\phi}|^{2}(2^{-j}x).

Let
B_{0}
be the unit ball centered at the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For any set
U=T(B_{0})
where
T
is an affine transformation
T:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, define

W_{U}^{n,d}(x):=W^{n,d}(T^{-1}(x)).

If
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a convex set, then we write
W_{U}^{n,d}
to mean
W_{\tilde{U}}^{n,d}
, where
\tilde{U}=T(B_{0})
for some affine transformation
T
and
\tilde{U}
is comparable to
U
. This defines
W_{U}^{n,d}
up to a bounded constant, which is sufficient for our arguments.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and let
W^{n,d}
be the weight function. Let
B_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote the unit ball centered at the origin. For any set
U=T(B_{0})
where
T
is an affine transformation
T:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, define

w_{U,d}(x):=|U|^{-1}W^{n,d}(T^{-1}(x)).

When it is clear in the context, we denote by
w_{U}
for
w_{U,d}
.
The function
w_{U}
is essentially supported on the set
U
and the Fourier transform of it is supported on
U^{*}
.

4.2.
l^{p/2}
function estimates without weights

Let us state the
l^{p/2}
function estimate for the nondegenerate curve
\gamma_{n}
.

Theorem 4.2
.

For any
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
, we have

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{\gamma_{n},\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for any Schwartz functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose Fourier supports are in
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
. The constant
C_{\gamma_{n},\epsilon}
depends only on the parameter
\epsilon
and the bounds of the determinant Vandermonde matrix of the curve
\gamma_{n}
.

Theorem
1.3
follows immediately from Theorem
4.2
and Proposition
3.10
. Namely, since the Fourier support of
f_{\theta}
is contained in
(10n)\theta
, we have

(4.1)

|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}=\big||f_{\theta}|^{2}*w_{\theta^{*}}\big|^{\frac{p}{4}}\lesssim|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}}.

So we have

\begin{split}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}&\lesssim_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
&\lesssim_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}||f_{\theta}|^{2}*w_{\theta^{*}}|^{\frac{p}{4}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
&\lesssim_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
&\lesssim_{\epsilon}R^{2\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U,{\frac{p}{2}}}^{curve}f\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}.\end{split}

So to prove Theorem
1.3
, it suffices to prove Theorem
4.2
and Proposition
3.10
.
We next state the
l^{p/2}
function estimate for a cone.

Theorem 4.3
.

For any
4\leq p\leq n^{2}-n-2
,

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{\gamma_{n},\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{{\frac{p}{2}}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for any Schwartz functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
.

Theorem
1.5
follows immediately from Theorem
4.3
and Proposition
4.4
.

Proposition 4.4
.

For any
4\leq p<\infty
,
R\geq 1
, and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{{\frac{p}{2}}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}}^{p}\leq C_{\gamma_{n},\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Xi_{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U.\frac{p}{2}}^{cone}f\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

One may see that
Proposition
4.4
is equivalent to Proposition
3.10
.

4.3.
l^{p/2}
function estimates with weights

We prove that Theorem
4.2
follows from the
l^{p/2}
function estimate with a weight. Let us first state the
l^{p/2}
function estimate with weights (Proposition
4.5
).

Proposition 4.5
.

For any
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
, we have

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{\epsilon,\gamma_{n}}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{{\frac{p}{2}}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for any Schwartz functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose Fourier supports are in
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
.

Let us prove that Proposition
4.5
implies Theorem
4.2
. It suffices to prove

(4.2)

\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{{\frac{p}{2}}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{{\frac{p}{2}}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

We apply Proposition
3.10
to the left hand side. Then it suffices to prove

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}^{curve}f\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\lesssim\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{{\frac{p}{2}}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

To prove this, since there are only
O(\log R)
many choices of
s
, it suffices to check

(4.3)

\sum_{\tau}\sum_{U}|U|\Big(\int|S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}^{curve}f|^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big)^{2}\lesssim\int(\sum_{\theta}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}})^{2}.

By definition, the left hand side is equal to

(4.4)

\sum_{\tau}\sum_{U}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}|f_{\theta}(x)|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}(x)\Big)^{2}.

By Hölder’s inequality, this is bounded by

(4.5)

\begin{split}&\lesssim\sum_{\tau}\sum_{U}\|w_{U}\|_{1}|U|\int\big(\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}|f_{\theta}(x)|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big)^{2}w_{U}(x)\,dx\\
&\lesssim\sum_{\tau}\int(\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}})^{2}\lesssim\int(\sum_{\theta}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}})^{2}.\end{split}

This completes the proof.
Therefore, to prove Theorem
1.3
, it suffices to prove Proposition
4.5
.

We can prove an analogous result for the cone. By following the same argument, one can show that Proposition
4.6
implies Theorem
4.3
. We omit the proof here.

Proposition 4.6
.

For any
4\leq p\leq n^{2}-n-2
,

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{{\frac{p}{2}}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for any Schwartz functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma_{n},R)
.

5.
Set-up for the high/low method

We introduce some definitions and lemmas for the high/low method. Most of the lemmas are already presented and stated in
[
GM23
]
. In their paper, they considered the model curve
\gamma(t)=(t,t^{2},\ldots,t^{n})
. In our paper, we consider a nondegenerate curve
\gamma_{n}
, but the same proof works for the lemmas we are going to state below.

5.1.
Reduction to polynomial curves

For given
0<A<\infty
, we define a collection
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
of polynomials curves

(5.1)

\gamma_{n}(R;s)=\big(\frac{s^{n}}{n!},\frac{s^{n-1}}{(n-1)!},\ldots,\frac{s}{1!}\big)+R^{-\epsilon}s^{n+1}E_{\epsilon}(s)

where
E_{\epsilon}(s)
is a
n
-tuple of polynomials of degree at most
\epsilon^{-1}
and the coefficients of monomials of
E_{\epsilon}(s)
are all bounded by
A
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Fix
\epsilon,A>0
. For any
\gamma_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
,
for any
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
, we have

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{A,\epsilon}R^{200^{n}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for any Schwartz functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose Fourier supports are in
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
. Here the constant
C_{A,\epsilon}
depends only on the parameter
A
and
\epsilon
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Fix
\epsilon,A>0
. For any
\gamma_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
,
for any
4\leq p\leq n^{2}-n-2
, we have

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{A,\epsilon}R^{200^{n}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for any Schwartz functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
. Here the constant
C_{A,\epsilon}
depends only on the parameter
A
and
\epsilon
.

To prove Proposition
4.5
, it suffices to consider polynomial curves in the collection
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
. The reduction to a polynomial function is fairly standard, for example, see Section 2 of
[
GO24
]
or Section 7.2 of
[
GHI19
]
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Proposition
5.1
implies
Proposition
4.5
.

Proposition
5.2
implies Proposition
4.6
.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

The proofs of the two statements are the same. So let us prove the first statement only. Let us fix a smooth nondegenerate curve
\gamma_{n}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Divide the interval
[0,1]
into smaller subintervals with sidelength
R^{-\epsilon}
. Since we are allowed to lose
R^{O(\epsilon)}
in the estimate, after translation, it suffices to consider
\gamma_{n}(s)
on the domain
s\in[0,R^{-\epsilon}]
. We write

(5.2)

\gamma_{n}(s)=\gamma_{n,W}(s)+\Delta_{W}(s)

where
\gamma_{n,W}
is the Taylor expansion of
\gamma_{n}
up to the order
W
. Take
W=10/\epsilon
. Then it is easy to see that

(5.3)

|\partial_{s}^{j}\Delta_{W}(s)|\leq CR^{-2}

for all
0\leq j\leq n
and
s\in[0,R^{-\epsilon}]
. Here,
C
depends on the choice of the curve
\gamma_{n}
. Since the size of
\Delta_{W}
is small, to prove Proposition
4.5
, it suffices to prove for
\gamma_{n,W}(s)
for
s\in[0,R^{-\epsilon}]
. By the nondegeneracy assumption (
1.2
), if
R
is sufficiently large, by a linear transformation with the determinant comparable to one, we may write

(5.4)

\gamma_{n,W}(s)=\big(\frac{s^{n}}{n!},\frac{s^{n-1}}{(n-1)!},\ldots,\frac{s}{1!}\big)+s^{n+1}E_{\epsilon/10}(s)

where
E_{\epsilon/10}(s)
is a polynomial curve of degree at most
10\epsilon^{-1}
defined on
s\in[0,R^{-\epsilon}]
. Define the rescaling map

(5.5)

L(\xi)=(R^{n\epsilon}\xi_{1},\ldots,R^{\epsilon}\xi_{n}).

Define the curve

(5.6)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}(s):=L\big(\gamma_{n,W}(R^{-\epsilon}s)\big).

Then
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}
is a curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined on the domain
[0,1]
, and we have the expression

(5.7)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}(s)=\big(\frac{s^{n}}{n!},\frac{s^{n-1}}{(n-1)!},\ldots,\frac{s}{1!}\big)+R^{-\epsilon}s^{n+1}E_{R,\epsilon/10}(s)

where
E_{R,\epsilon/10}(s)=R^{-n\epsilon}L\big(E_{\epsilon/10}(R^{-\epsilon}s)\big)
. Note that
E_{R,\epsilon/10}(s)
is a polynomial of degree at most
10\epsilon^{-1}
, and every coefficient of a monomial of
E_{R,\epsilon/10}
is small if
R
is sufficiently large. Therefore, by applying scaling to
\gamma_{n,W}
, it is straightforward to see that Proposition
4.5
for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}
implies the desired result. It suffices now note that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
for some
A
.
∎

5.2.
Wave packet decomposition

Let
\epsilon
be a small number such that
\epsilon^{-1}
is an integer.
Introduce scales

(5.8)

1\leq R^{\epsilon}\leq R^{2\epsilon}\leq R^{3\epsilon}\leq\cdots\leq R^{\epsilon^{-1}\epsilon}=R.

For simplicity, we use the notation
R_{k}=R^{k\epsilon}
and
R_{N}=R
with
N=\epsilon^{-1}
. Whenever we use the notation
\theta
, it always means that
\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)
. When we use the notation
\tau_{k}
, it usually means that
\tau_{k}
is an element of
\Theta^{n}(R_{k})
.
For parameters
\alpha,\beta>0
, define

(5.9)

\begin{split}&U_{\alpha}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|f(x)|\geq\alpha\},\\
&U_{\alpha,\beta}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|f(x)|\geq\alpha,\;\frac{\beta}{2}\leq\sum_{\theta}|f_{\theta}|^{2}*w_{\theta}(x)\leq\beta\}.\end{split}

For each
\tau_{k}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{k})
, for
k=1,2,\ldots,N
, let
\mathbb{T}_{\tau_{k}}
be the collection of
T_{\tau_{k}}
, which is a translate of
\tau_{k}^{*}
so that elements of
\mathbb{T}_{\tau_{k}}
form a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Recall that the function
\phi
is defined in Subsection
4.1
. For each
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, let

\psi_{m}(x)=c\int_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{n}}|\widecheck{\phi}|^{2}(x-y-m)dy,

where
c
is chosen so that
\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\psi_{m}(x)=c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\widecheck{\phi}|^{2}=1
. Since
|\widecheck{\phi}|
is a rapidly decaying function, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C_{k}>0
such that

\psi_{m}(x)\leq c\int_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{n}}\frac{C_{k}}{(1+|x-y-m|^{2})^{100kn}}dy\leq\frac{\tilde{C}_{k}}{(1+|x-m|^{2})^{k}}.

Define the partition of unity
\{\psi_{T_{\tau_{k}}}\}
associated to
{\tau_{k}}
to be
\psi_{T_{\tau_{k}}}(x)=\psi_{m}\circ A_{\tau_{k}}
, where
A_{\tau_{k}}
is a linear transformations taking
\tau_{k}^{*}
to
[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{n}
and
A_{\tau_{k}}(T_{\tau_{k}})=m+[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{n}
. The important properties of
\psi_{T_{\tau_{k}}}
are

(1)

rapid decay off of
T_{\tau_{k}}

(2)

Fourier support contained in a translate of
\tau_{k}
to the origin.

We next do a pigeonholing on the wave packets at the last scale
R
.

Proposition 5.4

(Proposition 6.6 of
[
GM23
]
)
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
. Let
{\alpha}>C_{\epsilon}R^{-100n}\max_{\theta}\|f_{\theta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
. There exist subsets
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\theta}\subset\mathbb{T}_{\theta}
and a constant
\mathcal{X}>0
depending on
\alpha
with the following properties:

(5.10)

\displaystyle|U_{\alpha}|\lesssim(\log R)|\{x\in U_{\alpha}:\,\,{{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\sum_{\theta\in{\Theta^{n}(R)}}\sum_{T\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\theta}}\psi_{T}(x)f_{\theta}(x)|\,\,\}|,

(5.11)

\displaystyle R^{\epsilon}T\cap U_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for all}\quad\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R),\quad T\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\theta}

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\lesssim\|\sum_{T\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\theta}}\psi_{T}f_{\theta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{3\epsilon}\mathcal{X}\qquad\text{for all}\quad\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)

(5.13)

\displaystyle\|\psi_{T}f_{\theta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{X}\qquad\text{for all}\quad\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R),\quad T\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\theta}.

We define the function

(5.14)

f^{N}(x):=\sum_{\tau_{N}}f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}(x):=\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}\sum_{T\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\theta}}\psi_{T}(x)f_{\theta}(x).

By replacing
\alpha
by
\alpha/\mathcal{X}
, we may assume that
\mathcal{X}=1
. In the language of wave packets, this says that all the wave packets at the last scale has the height either comparable to one or zero. Throughout the paper,
f^{N}
will play a role of
f
frequently. We modify the definitions of
U_{\alpha,\beta}
in (
5.9
), and use

(5.15)

U_{\alpha,\beta}:=\{x\in U_{\alpha}:\frac{\beta}{2}\leq\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{2}*w_{\theta}(x)\leq\beta\}.

This is the definition we use for the rest of the paper.

5.3.
Interpolation

The advantage of Proposition
5.4
is to enable us to interpolate the
l^{q/2}
function estimates.
Here, we prove some elementary interpolation lemmas. The interpolating argument is somewhat standard and appears in several places, for example, Lemma 3.4 of
[
BD15
]
or the proof of Proposition 4.4 of
[
Mal24
]
. Recall that Theorem
1.3
was reduced to Proposition
5.1
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Proposition
4.5
for
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
follows from that for
p=4
and
n^{2}+n-2
.

Here is one more technical interpolation lemma, which is used in the proof of Proposition
6.4
for the case
n=3
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
n=3
.
For
8\leq p\leq 10
,

(5.16)

\sup_{\tau_{N}}\|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}\|_{\infty}^{p-8}\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{4}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}\lesssim\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}.

Lastly, we prove the Kakeya-type estimate (Proposition
3.9
) here. Let us recall the statement.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
2\leq q<\infty
. Suppose
\{f_{s}\}_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}
satisfies
\textup{supp}\widehat{f}_{s}\subset p(s)
. Then

\int_{B_{R}}(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}|f_{s}|^{q}*w_{p(s)^{*}})^{2}\lessapprox\sum_{\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\parallel(q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}:\\
U\subset B_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|U|}(\int\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|f_{s}|^{q}w_{U})^{2}.

Proposition
3.10
can be proved identically by following the proof of Proposition
5.7
. So we omit the proof here.

Proof of Proposition
5.6
.

Recall that
\|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}\|_{\infty}\sim 1
. We apply the Kakeya-type estimate (Proposition
3.10
) to the left hand side. It suffices to prove that

\sum_{s}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U,4}f^{N}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{8}\lesssim\sum_{s}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}f^{N}\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

This is because

(5.17)

\sum_{s}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}f^{N}\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\lesssim\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}.

Once we have this, the desired bound follows from Hölder’s inequality and embedding. The above inequality follows from

(5.18)

\|S_{U,4}f^{N}\|_{4}^{8}\lesssim\|S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}f^{N}\|_{\frac{p}{2}}^{p}.

By definition, this is equivalent to

(5.19)

\int\sum_{\tau_{N}\subset\tau}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}(x)|^{4}w_{U}(x)\,dx\lesssim\int\sum_{\tau_{N}\subset\tau}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}(x)|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}(x)\,dx.

This follows from

(5.20)

\int|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}(x)|^{4}w_{U}(x)\,dx\sim\int|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}(x)|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}(x)\,dx

for all
\tau_{N}
. This is true since both sides are essentially
\sim|T|\cdot\#\{T\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\theta}:T\subset 2U\}
as we have assumed the height
\mathcal{X}=1
. This is a standard application of a wave packet decomposition, so we omit the details.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

We need to prove

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{\gamma_{n},\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{{\frac{p}{2}}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
. . By pigeonholing, it suffices to prove

(5.21)

\big(\alpha^{p}|U_{\alpha}|\big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C_{\gamma_{n},\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{{\frac{p}{2}}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for all
\alpha>0
. Let us fix
p
and
\alpha
. Now,
by Proposition
5.4
, we may replace
f
by
\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}\sum_{T\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\theta}}\psi_{T}(x)f_{\theta}(x)
. By abusing the notation, let us use the same notation
f
for the new function. Define
\theta
to be the number satisfying

(5.22)

\frac{1}{p}=\frac{\theta}{4}+\frac{1-\theta}{n^{2}+n-2}.

By Hölder’s inequality and the assumption that Proposition
4.5
is true for
p=4
and
n^{2}+n-2
, we have

(5.23)

\begin{split}(\alpha^{p}|U_{\alpha}|)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{4}^{\theta}\|f\|_{n^{2}+n-2}^{1-\theta}\lesssim R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{4})\theta}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{n^{2}+n-2})(1-\theta)}X^{\theta}Y^{1-\theta}\end{split}

where

(5.24)

\begin{split}&X:=\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{4}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}\\
&Y:=\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S_{U,\frac{n^{2}+n-2}{2}}f\|_{L^{\frac{n^{2}+n-2}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{n^{2}+n-2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{n^{2}+n-2}}\end{split}

Since
R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{4})\theta}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{n^{2}+n-2})(1-\theta)}=R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}
, it suffices to check

(5.25)

X^{p\theta}Y^{p(1-\theta)}\lesssim\sum_{s}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}\|S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}f\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

Once we have this, (
5.21
) follows by embedding.
As we already saw in the proof of Proposition
5.6
(see (
5.20
)), we have

(5.26)

\|S_{U,2}f\|_{2}^{2}\sim\|S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}f\|_{\frac{p}{2}}^{\frac{p}{2}}\sim\|S_{U,\frac{n^{2}+n-2}{2}}f\|_{\frac{n^{2}+n-2}{2}}^{\frac{n^{2}+n-2}{2}}.

Therefore,
X^{4}\sim Y^{n^{2}+n-2}
, and furthermore,

(5.27)

X^{p\theta}Y^{p(1-\theta)}\sim\sum_{s}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}\|S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}f\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.7
.

Use a similar argument as in (
5.20
), and assume that each wave packet has height either one or zero. So we have

(5.28)

\int_{B_{R}}(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}|f_{s}|^{q}*w_{p(s)^{*}})^{2}\lesssim\int_{B_{R}}(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}|f_{s}|^{2}*w_{p(s)^{*}})^{2}.

Take
g_{s}:=|f_{s}|^{2}*w_{p(s)^{*}}
. By Proposition
3.8
, this is bounded by

(5.29)

\lesssim\delta^{-\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}\big(|f_{s}|^{2}*w_{p(s)^{*}}\big)w_{U}\Big)^{2}.

Since
w_{p(s)^{*}}*w_{U}\lesssim w_{U}
, this is bounded by

(5.30)

\lesssim\delta^{-\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|f_{s}|^{2}w_{U}\Big)^{2}.

As we have shown, since each wave packet has height either one or zero, by (
5.20
), this is bounded by

(5.31)

\lesssim\delta^{-\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|f_{s}|^{q}w_{U}\Big)^{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

5.4.
Pruning and high/low lemma

In this subsection, we state several definitions and lemmas for the high/low method. This is the setup in
[
GMW24
,
Mal24
]
.
Recall that we have defined
f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}
(see (
5.14
)) and introduced
\tau_{k}
(see the beginning of Subsection
5.2
). We next define
f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}
in an inductive way. Define
f_{\tau_{N-1}}^{N}:=\sum_{\tau_{N}\subset\tau_{N-1}}f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}
. Let us fix
\alpha,\beta>0
. Let
C,K
be sufficiently large constants, which will be determined later.

Definition 5.8

(pruning with respect to
\tau_{k}
)
.

For
1\leq k\leq N-1
, define

(5.32)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{T}_{\tau_{k}}^{g}:=\Big\{T_{\tau_{k}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\tau_{k}}:\;\|\psi_{T_{\tau_{k}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}\|_{\infty}\leq K^{3}C^{N-k+1}\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\Big\}\\
&f_{\tau_{k}}^{k}:=\sum_{T_{\tau_{k}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\tau_{k}}^{g}}\psi_{T_{\tau_{k}}}f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1},\;\;\;\;\;f_{\tau_{k-1}}^{k}:=\sum_{\tau_{k}\subset\tau_{k-1}}f_{\tau_{k}}^{k}.\end{split}

For each
k
, define
f^{k}:=\sum_{\tau_{k}}f_{\tau_{k}}^{k}
.

Recall that by pigeonholing argument (see (
5.14
)), each wave packet has height either comparable to one or zero. So we have the following lemma, which is Lemma 4.1 of
[
GM23
]
.

Lemma 5.9

(Lemma 4.1 of
[
GM23
]
)
.

Here are properties of
f^{k}
.

(1)

|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k}(x)|\leq|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}(x)|\lesssim\#\{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R):\theta\subset\tau_{k}\}

(2)

\|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k}\|_{\infty}\leq K^{3}C^{N-k+1}\frac{\beta}{\alpha}
.

(3)

For
R
sufficiently large depending on
\epsilon
,
\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{f_{\tau_{k}}^{k}})\subset 3\tau_{k}
.

Lemma 5.10

(Locally constant property, Lemma 4.2 of
[
GM23
]
)
.

For each
\tau_{k}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{k})
and
T_{\tau_{k}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\tau_{k}}
,

\displaystyle\|f_{\tau_{k}}\|_{L^{\infty}(T_{\tau_{k}})}^{2}\lesssim|f_{\tau_{k}}|^{2}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}(x)\qquad\text{for any}\quad x\in T_{\tau_{k}}.

Also, for any
R_{k}^{1/n}
-ball
B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}
,

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{\tau_{k}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{k})}|f_{\tau_{k}}|^{2}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R_{k}^{1/n}})}\lesssim\sum_{\tau_{k}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{k})}|f_{\tau_{k}}|^{2}*w_{B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}}(x)\qquad\text{for any}\quad x\in B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}.

Definition 5.11
.

Let
\eta:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty)
be a radial, smooth bump function satisfying
\eta(x)=1
on
B_{1/2}
and
\textup{supp}\eta\subset B_{1}
. Then for each
s>0
, let

\eta_{\leq s}(\xi):=\eta(s^{-1}\xi).

We will sometimes abuse notation by denoting
h*\widecheck{\eta}_{>s}:=h-h*\widecheck{\eta}_{\leq s}
, where
h
is some Schwartz function. Also define
\eta_{s}(x):=\eta_{\leq s}-\eta_{\leq s/2}
.

Let
N_{0}
be a large integer such that
N_{0}\leq N-1
, which will be determined later. Let us briefly explain why we introduce this number
N_{0}
. When we apply the high/low method, we do dichotomy according to whether
k
is smaller than
N_{0}
or not. When
k
is smaller than
N_{0}
, it is the “low case” and when
k
is larger than
N_{0}
, it is the “high case”. We refer to Subsection
6.1
for this dichotomy.

Definition 5.12
.

For
N_{0}\leq k\leq N-1
, let

g_{k}:=\sum_{\tau_{k}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{k})}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{2}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}},\;\;\;g_{k}^{\ell}:=g_{k}*\widecheck{\eta}_{\leq R_{k+1}^{-1/n}},\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;g_{k}^{h}:=g_{k}-g_{k}^{\ell}.

In the following definition,
C\gg 1
is the same constant that goes into the pruning definition of
f^{k}
.

Definition 5.13
.

Define the high set by

\Omega_{N-1}:=\{x\in U_{\alpha,\beta}:C\beta\leq g_{N-1}(x)\}.

For each
k=N_{0},\ldots,N-2
, let

\Omega_{k}=\{x\in U_{\alpha,\beta}\setminus\cup_{l=k+1}^{N-1}\Omega_{l}:C^{N-k}\beta\leq g_{k}(x)\}.

Define the low set to be

L:=U_{\alpha,\beta}\setminus[\cup_{k=N_{0}}^{N-1}\Omega_{k}].

Lemma 5.14

(High-dominance on
\Omega_{k}
, Corollary 4.5 of
[
GM23
]
)
.

For
R
large enough depending on
\epsilon
,
g_{k}(x)\leq 2|g_{k}^{h}(x)|
for all
x\in\Omega_{k}
.

Lemma 5.15

(Pruning lemma, Lemma 4.6 of
[
GM23
]
)
.

Let
0\leq s\leq 1
.
5
5

5

In Lemma 4.6 of
[
GM23
]
, they stated the range of
s
:
s>R^{-\epsilon/n}
. But by the same argument, one can prove the same statement without a restriction on the range of
s
.
For any
\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s)
, for any
N_{0}\leq k\leq N-1
,

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{\tau_{k}\subset\tau}f_{\tau_{k}}(x)-\sum_{\tau_{k}\subset\tau}f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}(x)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{C^{1/2}K^{3}}\qquad\text{for all $x\in\Omega_{k}$}

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{\tau_{N_{0}}\subset\tau}f_{\tau_{N_{0}}}(x)-\sum_{\tau_{N_{0}}\subset\tau}f_{\tau_{N_{0}}}^{{N_{0}}}(x)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{C^{1/2}K^{3}}\qquad\text{ for all $x\in L$}.

This finishes the high/low set up.

6.
Proof of Proposition
5.1

In this section, we prove Proposition
5.1
. Recall that Theorem
1.3
is reduced to Proposition
5.1
. To prove Proposition
5.1
,
we use an induction on the dimension
n
. The base case is
n=2
, and this case is already proved by
[
Fef73
]
(see also
[
C8́2
]
). Throughout the section, let us fix
A
and
\epsilon
(see the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
in Subsection
5.1
). Assume that Proposition
5.1
is proved for
\leq n-1
. Recall that
\{\tau_{N}\}=\{\theta\}
and (
5.14
).

Definition 6.1
.

Let
S_{p}(1,R)
be the smallest constant such that

(6.1)

\big(\alpha^{p}|U_{\alpha}|\big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq S_{p}(1,R)\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{N}\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

for all curves
\gamma_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
, all
\alpha>0
, and all Schwartz functions
f
whose Fourier supports are in
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
.

Let us give one remark on the definition. Since
|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|\leq|f_{\theta}|
, one may see that

(6.2)

\|f\|_{L^{p}}\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}\big(1+S_{p}(1,R)\big)\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

This inequaltiy is more directly related to Proposition
5.1
. However,
by some technical reasons, we define the constant
S_{p}(1,R)
by using the inequality (
6.1
).

S_{p}(1,R)
is the constant we lose when we “decouple” the function from scale
1
to scale
R
.
Recall that we have introduced intermediate scales
R_{k}
(see the beginning of Subsection
5.2
). Our proof relies on a multiscale analysis. In the following, we also define the constant from the scale
R_{k}
to the last scale
R
.

Definition 6.2
.

For
k>0
, let
S_{p}(R_{k},R)
to be the smallest constant such that

(6.3)

\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{k}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{k})}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq S_{p}(R_{k},R)\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{N}\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for all curves
\gamma_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
, and all Schwartz functions
f
whose Fourier supports are in
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
.

By definition, Proposition
5.1
follows from

(6.4)

S_{p}(1,R)\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{200^{n}\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}

for
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
. To prove the estimate, we use a broad-narrow analysis originated from
[
BG11
]
, where they applied the broad-narrow analysis to a hypersurface. Later,
[
HL14
]
applied the broad-narrow analysis to a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We say
\tau^{1},\ldots,\tau^{n}\in\Theta^{n}(K)
are
K
-transverse if
d(\tau^{j_{1}},\tau^{j_{2}})>K^{-\frac{1}{n}}
for any
j_{1},j_{2}
. Recall (
5.15
).
For a
K
-transverse tuple
\{\tau^{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\Theta^{n}(K)
, define the broad set

(6.5)

{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}:=\big\{x\in U_{\alpha,\beta}:\alpha\leq K\mathop{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\displaystyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=11.00012pt}\cr$\displaystyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\textstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=11.00012pt}\cr$\textstyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=8.00009pt}\cr$\scriptstyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=6.00006pt}\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\prod$\cr}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}|f_{\tau^{j}}(x)|,\;\;\max_{\tau^{j}}|f_{\tau^{j}}(x)|\leq\alpha\big\}.

Let
S_{{\rm{Br}},p,K}(1,R)
be the smallest constant such that

(6.6)

\alpha^{p}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}|\leq S_{{\rm{Br}},p,K}(1,R)^{p}\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{N}\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}

for all
\alpha,\beta
, any choice of a
K
-transverse tuple, any functions
f
whose Fourier supports are in
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
and any curves
\gamma_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
.

By a standard broad-narrow analysis, one can show that bounding
S_{p}(1,R)
is reduced to bounding
S_{\mathrm{Br},p,K}(1,R)
.

Proposition 6.3
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
K>0
depending on
\epsilon
so that

(6.7)

S_{p}(1,R)\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}\sup_{1\leq X\leq R}S_{{\rm{Br}},p,K}(1,X)+C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}

for all
R\geq 1
.

To bound
S_{\mathrm{Br},p,K}(1,R)
, we use a multiscale argument. Here is the key estimate. By some technical reasons (see (
6.26
) and discussion there), we need to distinguish
n=3
with
n\geq 4
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
n=3
. For
8\leq p\leq 10
, we have

(6.8)

\begin{split}S_{{\rm{Br}},p,K}(1,R)&\lesssim R^{10^{n}N_{0}\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\\
&+R^{1000\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\sum_{j=N_{0}}^{N}\Big(\frac{(R_{j})^{\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{8})}}{R^{\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{8})}}\Big)^{\frac{8}{p}}S_{8}(R_{j},R)^{\frac{8}{p}}.\end{split}

Let
n\geq 4
. For
p=n^{2}+n-2
, we have

(6.9)

S_{{\rm{Br}},p,K}(1,R)\lesssim R^{10^{n}N_{0}\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}+R^{100^{n}\epsilon}\sum_{j=N_{0}}^{N}(R_{j})^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}S_{p}(R_{j},R).

For
p=4
, we have

(6.10)

S_{p}(1,R)\lesssim R^{10^{n}N_{0}\epsilon}+R^{100^{n}\epsilon}.

Note that
N_{0}\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{1/2}
. So this is still a very small number.
By the above theorem, bounding
S_{\mathrm{Br},p,K}(1,R)
is reduced to bounding
S_{p}(R_{j},R)
. We are going to related
S_{p}(R_{j},R)
and
S_{p}(1,X)
.

Proposition 6.5
.

(6.11)

S_{p}(R_{k},R)\lesssim R^{\epsilon}\Big(\sup_{1\leq X\leq R^{\epsilon}}S_{p}(1,X)\big(\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{X}\big)^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big)^{N-k}.

On the left hand side of (
6.3
), we have the
L^{p}
norm of the
l^{p/2}
expression. On the other hand, on the left hand side of (
6.1
), we have the
L^{p}
norm of
f
. To relate these two, we use Proposition
3.9
. After applying the proposition, on the “high case”, we use rescaling and use the definition of
S_{p}(1,R)
. In the “low case”, we use the decoupling theorem for a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
by
[
BDG16
]
. This gives the proof of Proposition
6.5
.

Let us state the proposition for a cone. By a bootstrapping argument, originated from
[
PS07
]
, one can prove the following proposition.

Proposition 6.6
.

Assume that Proposition
5.1
is true for
n-1
.
Then for
4\leq p\leq n^{2}-n-2
and
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
,

\|g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{p,\epsilon^{\prime}}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})+\epsilon^{\prime}}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n}(R)}|g_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for all curves
\gamma_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
, and all Schwartz functions
g
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma_{n};R)
.

For the rest of the section, we prove Proposition
6.3
,
6.4
and
6.5
. Then assuming Proposition
6.6
, we deduce Proposition
4.5
. Proposition
6.6
will be proved in Section
7
.

6.1.
Proof of Proposition

6.4

Let us begin the proof. One ingredient is the multilinear restriction estimate. The proof of the estimate is well-known, for example, see the proof of Theorem 5.2 of
[
Mal24
]
or Lemma 2.5 of
[
HL14
]
.

Proposition 6.7

(Multilinear restriction estimate)
.

Let
\{\tau^{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\Theta^{n}(K)
be a transverse tuple. Then for any
M\geq K
, we have

(6.12)

\int_{B_{M}}\mathop{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\displaystyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=11.00012pt}\cr$\displaystyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\textstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=11.00012pt}\cr$\textstyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=8.00009pt}\cr$\scriptstyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=6.00006pt}\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\prod$\cr}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}|f_{\tau^{j}}(x)|^{2n}\,dx\leq C_{A,K}\mathop{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\displaystyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=11.00012pt}\cr$\displaystyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\textstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=11.00012pt}\cr$\textstyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=8.00009pt}\cr$\scriptstyle\prod$\cr}}}}{{\vphantom{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\prod$}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr\smash{\vrule height=1.0pt,depth=6.00006pt}\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\prod$\cr}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f_{\tau^{j}}(x)|^{2}W_{B_{M}}(x)\,dx\Big)^{n}

for all Schwartz functions
f
whose Fourier supports are contained in the
M^{-1}
-neighborhood of a nondegenerate curve
\gamma_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
.

We write

(6.13)

\alpha^{p}|\mathrm{Br}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}|\lesssim\alpha^{p}|\mathrm{Br}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap L|+\sum_{k=N_{0}}^{N}\alpha^{p}|\mathrm{Br}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}|.

Let us first consider the term
\alpha^{p}|\mathrm{Br}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap L|
. By Lemma
5.15
, the definition of
L
, and Hölder’s inequality, we have

(6.14)

\begin{split}\alpha^{p}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap L|\lesssim\int_{L}|f_{\tau_{N_{0}}}^{N_{0}}(x)|^{p}&\lesssim R^{10^{n}N_{0}\epsilon}\int_{L}\Big(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{2}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}}\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{10^{n}N_{0}\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}}\Big)^{2}.\end{split}

Hence, it suffices to consider the second term. Let
N_{0}\leq k\leq N-1
. For simplicity, let us use the notation
q:=p/2
. We cover
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by finitely overlapping balls
B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}
, and obtain

(6.15)

|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}|\lesssim\sum_{B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}\cap B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}|.

Fix
B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}
.
By Lemma
5.15
, the multilinear restriction estimate and Hölder’s inequality, we have

(6.16)

\begin{split}\alpha^{2n}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}\cap B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}|&\lesssim\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f^{k+1}_{\tau_{k}}(y)|^{2})W_{B_{R_{k}}^{1/n}(y)}\mathrm{d}y\big)^{n}\\
&\lesssim|B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}||g_{k}(x)|^{n}\end{split}

for any
x\in{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}\cap B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}
. Take
(\tilde{p},\tilde{q}):=(n^{2}-n-2,\frac{n^{2}-n-2}{2})
so that

(6.17)

\frac{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{q}}=\frac{p}{q}=2.

By the inequalities
|g_{k}(x)|\gtrsim\beta
on
x\in\Omega_{k}
and the high-dominance on
\Omega_{k}
(Lemma
5.14
) and the locally constant property (Lemma
5.10
), the RHS of (
6.16
) is bounded by

(6.18)

\lesssim\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}|B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}||g_{k}^{h}(x)|^{\tilde{p}}\lesssim\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g_{k}^{h}(y)|^{\tilde{p}}W_{B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}}(y)\,dy.

By summing over
B_{R_{k}^{1/n}}
, we obtain

(6.19)

\alpha^{2n}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}|\lesssim\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g_{k}^{h}(y)|^{\tilde{p}}\,dy.

We next analyze the Fourier support of
g_{k}^{h}
. Write

(6.20)

\widehat{g_{k}^{h}}=(1-\eta_{\leq R_{k+1}^{-1/n}})\sum_{\tau_{k}}\Big(\widehat{f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}}*\widehat{\overline{f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}}}\Big)\widehat{w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}}=:\sum_{\tau_{k}}\widehat{G_{\tau_{k}}}.

Write

(6.21)

\widehat{g_{k}^{h}}=\sum_{R^{-\epsilon/n}\lesssim s\lesssim 1:\,\mathrm{dyadic}}\sum_{\tau_{k}}\widehat{G_{\tau_{k}}}\eta_{sR_{k}^{-1/n}}.

Since there are
O(\log R_{k})
many
s
’, we have

(6.22)

\alpha^{2n}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}|\lesssim\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}R_{k}^{O(\epsilon)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\sum_{\tau_{k}}G_{\tau_{k}}*(\eta_{sR_{k}^{-1/n}})^{\vee}|^{\tilde{p}}

for some
s
.
The Fourier support of
G_{\tau_{k}}*(\eta_{sR_{k}^{-1/n}})^{\vee}
is contained in

\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n}^{(j)}(t):\;t\in[t_{\tau_{k}},t_{\tau_{k}}+R_{k}^{-\frac{1}{n}}],\;|\lambda_{i}|\leq 2R_{k}^{-\frac{i}{n}}\;\mathrm{for\;all}\;2\leq i\leq n,\;\;|\lambda_{1}|\sim sR_{k}^{-\frac{1}{n}}\Big\}.

Let us do an isotrophic rescaling so that the length of
\lambda_{1}
becomes one.
Define
H_{\tau_{k}}
to be a smooth function so that
\widehat{H_{\tau_{k}}}(\xi)=\widehat{G}_{\tau_{k}}(2sR_{k}^{-\frac{1}{n}}\xi)\eta_{sR_{k}^{-1/n}}(2sR_{k}^{-\frac{1}{n}}\xi)
.
Then the Fourier support of
H_{\tau_{k}}
is contained in

\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n}^{(j)}(t):t\in[t_{k},t_{k}+R_{k}^{-\frac{1}{n}}],\;\;|\lambda_{i}|\leq s^{-1}R_{k}^{-\frac{i-1}{n}}\;\mathrm{for\;all}\;2\leq i\leq n,\;\;|\lambda_{1}|\sim 1\Big\}.

Since we have assumed Proposition
5.1
holds for
n-1
as induction hypothesis, by
applying Proposition
6.6
, noting that
s^{-1}=O(R^{\epsilon})
, and applying Hölder’s inequality, we obtain

(6.23)

\Big\|\sum_{\tau_{k}}H_{\tau_{k}}\Big\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}}^{\tilde{p}}\lesssim R^{9^{n}\epsilon}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\tilde{p}})}\Big\|\big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|H_{\tau_{k}}|^{\frac{\tilde{p}}{2}}*w_{(\tau_{k}^{\prime})^{*}}\big)^{\frac{2}{\tilde{p}}}\Big\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}}^{\tilde{p}}

where
\tau_{k}^{\prime}
is defined by
(2s)^{-1}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}}
-dilation of the Fourier support of
G_{\tau_{k}}*(\eta_{sR_{k}^{-1/n}})^{\vee}
.
After rescaling back, the right hand side of (
6.22
) is bounded by

(6.24)

\begin{split}&\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}\big(R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\tilde{p}})}\big)^{\tilde{p}}\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|G_{\tau_{k}}*(\eta_{sR_{k}^{-1/n}})^{\vee}|^{\frac{\tilde{p}}{2}}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}\\
&\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}\big(R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\tilde{p}})}\big)^{\tilde{p}}\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|G_{\tau_{k}}|^{\frac{\tilde{p}}{2}}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}\\
&\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}\big(R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\tilde{p}})}\big)^{\tilde{p}}\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{\tilde{p}}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}.\end{split}

To summarize, we have proved

(6.25)

\alpha^{2n}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}|\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}\big(R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{\tilde{p}}{2}-2)}\big)\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{{\tilde{p}}}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}.

By routine calculations, we have

(6.26)

\frac{p}{2}=q\leq\tilde{p}=n^{2}-n-2\;\;\;\mathrm{if\;and\;only\;if\;}\;\;p\leq 2n^{2}-2n-4.

Recall that we are considering the range
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
. So we have
q\leq\tilde{p}
for
n\geq 4
. On the other hand, it may happen that
q\geq\tilde{p}
for
n=3
. So we distinguish two ranges of
n
.

6.1.1.
The case that
n=3

Let us state
(
6.25
)
. Recall that
(\tilde{p},\tilde{q})=(4,2)
.

(6.27)

\alpha^{6}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}|\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}\beta^{-1}\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{4}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}.

To prove the proposition,
we need to show that

(6.28)

\begin{split}\alpha^{p-6}&\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{4}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}\\
&\lesssim\beta R^{\frac{p}{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})+\epsilon}\Big(\frac{R_{k}}{R}\Big)^{{\frac{8}{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{8})}}S_{8}(R_{k},R)^{8}\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{q}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}.\end{split}

By Lemma
5.15
and the definition of
\Omega_{k}
, for
x\in\Omega_{k}
, we have

(6.29)

\alpha^{2}\lesssim|\sum_{\tau_{k}}f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}(x)|^{2}\lesssim R_{k}^{\frac{1}{3}}\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}(x)|^{2}\lesssim R_{k}^{\frac{1}{3}}g_{k}(x)\lesssim R_{k}^{\frac{1}{3}}R^{\epsilon}\beta.

By the inequality, (
6.28
) follows from

(6.30)

\begin{split}\alpha^{p-8}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{3}}&\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{4}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{p}{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\Big(\frac{R_{k}}{R}\Big)^{\frac{2}{3}}S_{8}(R_{k},R)^{8}\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{q}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}.\end{split}

By (
5.9
) and (
6.29
), we have
\alpha^{2}\lesssim R_{k}^{\frac{1}{3}}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{3}}\sup_{\tau_{N}}\|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}\|^{2}_{\infty}
. So, (
6.30
) follows from

\sup_{\tau_{N}}\|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}\|_{\infty}^{p-8}\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{4}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}\lesssim(R_{k})^{-\frac{p}{6}+\frac{5}{3}}S_{8}(R_{k},R)^{8}\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{q}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}.

Note that the power of
R_{k}
is non-negative (since
8\leq p\leq 10
), we just need to prove

\sup_{\tau_{N}}\|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}\|_{\infty}^{p-8}\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{4}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}\lesssim S_{8}(R_{k},R)^{8}\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{q}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}.

By Definition
6.2
, we have

(6.31)

\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{4}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}\leq S_{8}(R_{k},R)^{8}\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{4}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}.

Now, the interpolation lemma (Proposition
5.6
) gives the desired result. This completes the proof.

6.1.2.
The case that
n\geq 4

Our goal is to prove

(6.32)

\alpha^{p}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}|\lesssim R^{100^{n}\epsilon}(R_{k})^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})p}S_{p}(R_{k},R)^{p}\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}.

Let us continue from (
6.25
).
Since
q\leq\tilde{p}
, we have

\begin{split}\alpha^{2n}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}\cap\Omega_{k}|\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{\tilde{p}}{2}-2)}\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}\sup_{\tau_{k}}\|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}\|_{\infty}^{(\tilde{p}-q)2}\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{q}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}.\end{split}

Hence, it suffices to prove

(6.33)

\begin{split}&\alpha^{p-2n}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{\tilde{p}}{2}-2)}\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}\sup_{\tau_{k}}\|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}\|_{\infty}^{(\tilde{p}-q)2}\int\Big(\sum_{\tau_{k}}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{q}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}}\Big)^{2}\\
&\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}(R_{k})^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})p}S_{p}(R_{k},R)^{p}\int(\sum_{\tau_{N}}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{2}.\end{split}

By Definition
6.2
and
p/q=2
,
it suffices to verify

(6.34)

\alpha^{p-2n}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}({\frac{\tilde{p}}{2}}-2)}\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}\sup_{\tau_{k}}\|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}\|_{\infty}^{2\tilde{p}-p}\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{p}{2}-2)}.

By the inequality
\|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\beta\alpha^{-1}
(see Definition
5.9
), it becomes

(6.35)

\alpha^{p-2n}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}({\frac{\tilde{p}}{2}}-2)}\beta^{-\tilde{p}+n}(\beta\alpha^{-1})^{2\tilde{p}-p}\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{p}{2}-2)}.

After calculation, this is equivalent to

(6.36)

\alpha^{2p-2n-2\tilde{p}}\beta^{\tilde{p}+n-p}\lesssim R^{10^{n}\epsilon}(R_{k})^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{p-\tilde{p}}{2})}.

By following the same proof in (
6.29
),
we have
\alpha\lesssim R^{\epsilon}R_{k}^{\frac{1}{2n}}\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}
. By using this, it becomes
p-\tilde{p}\leq 2n
. This is true because
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
.
This completes the proof.

6.2.
Proof of Proposition
6.5

To simplify the notation, we introduce

(6.37)

S_{p}^{*}(1,R):=\sup_{1\leq X\leq R}S_{p}(1,X)\big(\frac{R}{X}\big)^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}.

To prove the proposition, we need to show

(6.38)

\begin{split}&\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{k}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{k})}|f_{\tau_{k}}^{k+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{k}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
&\lesssim\big(R^{2\epsilon^{3}}S_{p}^{*}(1,R^{\epsilon})\big)^{N-k}\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{N}\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.\end{split}

Recall that
R/R_{k}=R^{\epsilon(N-k)}
. To prove the above inequality, it suffices to prove the inequality below for all
k\leq j\leq N
.

(6.39)

\begin{split}&\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{j}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{j})}|f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{j}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
&\lesssim\big(R^{2\epsilon^{3}}S_{p}^{*}(1,{R^{\epsilon}})\big)\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{j+1}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{j+1})}|f_{\tau_{j+1}}^{j+2}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{j+1}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.\end{split}

Let us fix
j
. Recall that
|f_{\tau_{j+1}}^{j+1}|\lesssim|f_{\tau_{j+1}}^{j+2}|
. Since we are using the inductive argument, we prove a slightly general inequality; for every
1\leq r\leq R^{\epsilon}
,

(6.40)

\begin{split}&\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{j}\in\Theta^{n}(rR_{j})}|f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{j}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
&\lesssim\big(\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{r}\big)^{\epsilon^{2}}R^{2\epsilon^{3}}S_{p}^{*}(1,\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{r})\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{j+1}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{j+1})}|f_{\tau_{j+1}}^{j+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{j+1}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.\end{split}

The desired result follows by taking
r=1
. We use a backward induction on
r
. The term
(R^{\epsilon}/r)
is artificially added so that we can close the backward induction on
r
.

As we mentioned before,
we use a backward induction on
r
.
The estimate is trivially true for the range
r\in[R^{\epsilon-\epsilon^{3}},R^{\epsilon}]
. Suppose that (
6.40
) is true for
r\in[R^{\epsilon-(X-1)\epsilon^{5}},R^{\epsilon}]
(for some
X\in\mathbb{N}
). We will show the estimate for
r\in[R^{\epsilon-X\epsilon^{5}},R^{\epsilon}]
. Apply Proposition
3.9
to the left hand side of (
6.40
). Then it is bounded by

(6.41)

\sum_{(rR_{j})^{-1}\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(\sigma^{-1})}\sum_{U\parallel U_{\tau}}|U|\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{\tau_{j}:\tau_{j}\subset\tau}|f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1}(x)|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}(x)\,dx\Big)^{2}

Here
U_{\tau}
is a union of the dual boxes of
\tau_{j}
, centered at the origin, for all
\tau_{j}\subset\tau
.
We write (
6.41
) as follows.

(6.42)

\begin{split}&\sum_{(rR_{j})^{-1}\leq\sigma\leq R^{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}}(rR_{j})^{-1}}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(\sigma^{-1})}\sum_{U\parallel U_{\tau}}|U|\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{\tau_{j}:\tau_{j}\subset\tau}|f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}\Big)^{2}\\
&+\sum_{R^{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}}(rR_{j})^{-1}\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(\sigma^{-1})}\sum_{U\parallel U_{\tau}}|U|\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{\tau_{j}:\tau_{j}\subset\tau}|f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}\Big)^{2}.\end{split}

We need to bound these two terms.

Let us first consider the first term. By Hölder’s inequality, it is bounded by

(6.43)

R^{C\epsilon^{5}}\sum_{\tau_{j}}\int|f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1}|^{p}.

Fix
\tau_{j}
. We claim that

(6.44)

\begin{split}\int|f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1}|^{p}\lesssim S_{p}^{*}\big(1,\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{r}\big)^{p}\int\big(\sum_{\tau_{j+1}\subset\tau_{j}}|f_{\tau_{j+1}}^{j+2}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{j+1}^{*}}\big)^{2}.\end{split}

To prove the claim,
we do rescaling and apply Definition
6.1
. Since we are going to use the same rescaling argument several times, let us give the proof in details. By (
1.7
) and (
1.8
), we can write
\tau_{j}
as follows.

(6.45)

\Big\{\gamma_{n}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n}^{(j)}(t):t\in[t_{\tau_{j}},t_{\tau_{j}}+(rR_{j})^{-\frac{1}{n}}],\;\;\;|\lambda_{j}|\leq(rR_{j})^{-\frac{j}{n}}\Big\}.

Recall that
\gamma_{n}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
(see (
5.1
)). Take a linear transformation
L
and
\vec{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
L\big(\gamma_{n}(t+t_{\tau_{j}})-\vec{v}\big)
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{n}(10nA,R,\epsilon)
, the size of
\vec{v}
is smaller than ten, and the determinant of
L
is comparable to one. By dividing the domain of the curve into smaller intervals, by abusing the notation we may assume that
L\big(\gamma_{n}(t+t_{\tau_{j}})-\vec{v}\big)
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
. Define the curve
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}(t):=L\big(\gamma_{n}(t+t_{\tau_{j}})-\vec{v}\big)\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
. Note that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}^{(j)}(t)=L\big(\gamma_{n}^{(j)}(t+t_{\tau_{j}})\big)
. So we have
L(\eqref{0623.648}-\vec{v})
is equal to

(6.46)

\Big\{\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}^{(j)}(t):t\in[0,(rR_{j})^{-\frac{1}{n}}],\;\;\;|\lambda_{j}|\leq(rR_{j})^{-\frac{j}{n}}\Big\}.

Note that the range of
\lambda_{j}
does not change.
Hence, to prove (
6.44
), by applying the linear transformation to the both sides of (
6.44
), we may assume that
\tau_{j}
contains the origin. Let us continue to use the notation
\widetilde{\gamma_{n}}
. We next do scaling. Define

(6.47)

\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{n}(t):=L_{1}\Big(\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}\big((rR_{j})^{-\frac{1}{n}}t\big)\Big)

where

(6.48)

L_{1}(\xi):=\big((rR_{j})^{\frac{n}{n}}\xi_{1},(rR_{j})^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\xi_{2},\ldots,(rR_{j})^{\frac{1}{n}}\xi_{n}\big).

Then this curve is defined on
[0,1]
, and it belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
. Note that the image of (
6.46
) under the map
L_{1}
is equal to

(6.49)

\Big\{\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{n}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{n}^{(j)}(t):t\in[0,1],\;\;\;|\lambda_{j}|\leq 1\Big\}.

Moreover, the image of
\tau_{j+1}
under the map
L_{1}
becomes an element of
\Theta^{n}(r^{-1}R_{j}^{-1}R_{j+1})
associated to the curve
\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma_{n}}}
. So we can apply the definition of
S_{p}^{*}(1,r^{-1}R^{\epsilon})
(see Definition
6.1
and below). After rescaling back, we obtain the claim.

Let us next bound the second term of (
6.42
). We claim that

(6.50)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}\lesssim R^{\epsilon^{9}}(R^{\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{n}})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})\frac{p}{2}}\sum_{\tau_{j}^{\prime}\in\Theta^{n}(rR_{j}R^{\epsilon^{5}}):\tau_{j}^{\prime}\subset\tau_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|(f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1})_{\tau_{j}^{\prime}}|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}

for every
\tau_{j}\in\Theta^{n}(rR_{j})
.

Let us prove the claim.
By using the same linear transformation (see the discussion below
(
6.45
)
), define the curve
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}(t):=L\big(\gamma_{n}(t-t_{\tau_{j}})-\vec{v}\big)
. Then, as before, by applying the linear transformation to both sides of (
6.50
), we may assume that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,R,\epsilon)
. It suffices to prove (
6.50
) for the case that
\tau_{j}
contains the origin.
Note that
U\|U_{\tau}
, and
\tau
also contains the origin. By the definition in Section
3
,
U
is comparable to

(6.51)

10rR_{j}\Big([-1,1]\times[-\sigma^{\frac{1}{n}},\sigma^{\frac{1}{n}}]\times\cdots\times[-\sigma^{\frac{n-1}{n}},\sigma^{\frac{n-1}{n}}]\Big).

The next step is to do rescaling.
We use the rescaling map
L_{1}
in (
6.48
), and define the curve
\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{n}(t)
by (
6.47
). Note after applying the linear transformations,
\tau_{j}^{\prime}
becomes an element of
\Theta^{n}(R^{\epsilon^{5}})
. By definition, the dimension of an element of
\Theta^{n}(R^{\epsilon^{5}})
is as follows,

(6.52)

R^{\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{n}}\times R^{\frac{2\epsilon^{5}}{n}}\times\cdots\times R^{\frac{n\epsilon^{5}}{n}}.

On the other hand, the image of (
6.51
) under the map
(L_{1})^{-1}
is as follows.

(6.53)

10\Big([-1,1]\times[-(\sigma rR_{j})^{\frac{1}{n}},(\sigma rR_{j})^{\frac{1}{n}}]\times\cdots\times[-(\sigma rR_{j})^{\frac{n-1}{n}},(\sigma rR_{j})^{\frac{n-1}{n}}]\Big).

By the assumption that
\sigma\geq R^{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}}(rR_{j})^{-1}
. Hence, the above set contains

(6.54)

10\Big([-1,1]\times[-R^{\frac{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}}{n}},R^{\frac{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}}{n}}]^{n-1}\Big).

To apply the decoupling for a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
by
[
BDG16
]
, let us check the range. Since
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
, we have
p/2\leq(n-1)^{2}+(n-1)
. Hence, after freezing the first variable, by the decoupling, we have the following inequality: for
4\leq p\leq n^{2}+n-2
,

(6.55)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{L_{1}^{-1}(U)}&\lesssim R^{\epsilon^{9}}\Big(\sum_{\tilde{\tau}\in\Theta^{n-1}(R^{{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}}/{n}})}\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g_{\tilde{\tau}}|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{L_{1}^{-1}(U)}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}\cdot 2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\epsilon^{9}}R^{\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})\frac{p}{2}}\sum_{\tilde{\tau}\in\Theta^{n-1}(R^{{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}}/{n}})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g_{\tilde{\tau}}|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{L_{1}^{-1}(U)}\end{split}

for all Schwartz functions
g
whose Fourier supports are in
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\gamma_{n};R^{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}/n})
.
Here
g_{\tilde{\tau}}
is a Fourier restriction of
g
for the last
(n-1)
-variables to the set
\tilde{\tau}
. The dimension of
\tilde{\tau}
is as follows.

(6.56)

R^{\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{n}}\times R^{\frac{2\epsilon^{5}}{n}}\times\cdots\times R^{\frac{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}}{n}}.

Recall (
6.52
).
Hence, by applying the decoupling and rescaling back, we prove the claim (
6.50
).

Let us now finish the proof. By (
6.50
), the second term of
(
6.42
)
is bounded by

R^{\epsilon^{9}}R^{\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}){p}}\sum_{R^{(n-1)\epsilon^{5}}(rR_{j})^{-1}\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(\sigma^{-1})}\sum_{U\parallel U_{\tau}}|U|\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\sum_{\tau_{j}^{\prime}:\tau_{j}^{\prime}\subset\tau}|f_{\tau_{j}}^{j+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}\Big)^{2}.

By Hölder’s inequality and embedding, this is bounded by

(6.57)

R^{2\epsilon^{9}}R^{\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}){p}}\Big\|\big(\sum_{\tau_{j}^{\prime}\in\Theta^{n}(rR_{j}R^{\epsilon^{5}})}|f_{\tau_{j}^{\prime}}^{j+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

By the uncertainty principle (see (
4.1
)) and the induction hypothesis on
r
, this is bounded by

R^{2\epsilon^{9}}\big(\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{rR^{\epsilon^{5}}}\big)^{p\epsilon^{2}}R^{2p\epsilon^{3}}R^{\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}){p}}S_{p}^{*}(1,\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{rR^{\epsilon^{5}}})^{p}\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{j+1}\in\Theta^{n}(R_{j+1})}|f_{\tau_{j+1}}^{j+1}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{j+1}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}}^{p}.

Note that

(6.58)

R^{2\epsilon^{9}}\big(\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{rR^{\epsilon^{5}}}\big)^{\epsilon^{2}}R^{2\epsilon^{3}}R^{\epsilon^{\prime}}R^{\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}S_{p}^{*}(1,\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{rR^{\epsilon^{5}}})\lesssim\big(\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{r}\big)^{\epsilon^{2}}R^{2\epsilon^{3}}S_{p}^{*}(1,\frac{R^{\epsilon}}{r}).

So we can close the induction on
r
and this finishes the proof.

6.3.
Proof of Proposition
6.3

Let us fix
\alpha>0
. By pigeonholing, we can find
\beta>0
so that

(6.59)

\alpha^{p}|U_{\alpha}|\lesssim(\log R)\alpha^{p}|U_{\alpha,\beta}|+R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})p}\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{N}\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

To do a broad-narrow analysis,
fix
x\in U_{\alpha,\beta}
. Let us introduce sufficiently large
K
, which will be determined later. Define a collection of significant caps.

(6.60)

\mathcal{C}:=\{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(K):|(f^{N})_{\tau}(x)|\geq K^{-100n}|f^{N}(x)|\}.

We say we are in the broad case if
|\mathcal{C}|>K^{\epsilon}
. Othewise, we say we are in the narrow case. If we are in the broad case, then we can find
n
many
\tau_{j}\in\mathcal{C}
such that any two of them are not adjacent. Hence, we have

(6.61)

|U_{\alpha,\beta}|\lesssim K^{100n}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}|.

So we have

(6.62)

\begin{split}\alpha^{p}|U_{\alpha,\beta}|&\lesssim\alpha^{p}K^{100n}|{\rm{Br}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{K}|\\
&\lesssim K^{100n}S_{{\rm{Br}},p,K}(1,R)^{p}\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{N}\in\Theta(R)}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.\end{split}

If we are in the narrow case, then for some
\widetilde{\alpha}
, we have

(6.63)

\alpha^{p}|U_{\alpha}|\lesssim\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(K)}\widetilde{\alpha}^{p}|\{x:|(f^{N})_{\tau}|>\widetilde{\alpha}\}|.

We next fix
\tau
and repeat the rescaling argument in the proof of (
6.40
). By using a linaer transformation (see the discussion below (
6.45
)), we may assume that
\tau
contains the origin. Next, by using scaling (see (
6.47
) and the discussion there) and the definition of
S_{p}(1,R/K)
, we have

\widetilde{\alpha}^{p}\big|\{x:(f^{N})_{\tau}(x)>\widetilde{\alpha}\}\big|\lesssim S_{p}(1,R/K)^{p}\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{N}\in\Theta^{n}(R):\tau_{N}\subset\tau}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

Hence, by embedding, we have

\begin{split}\alpha^{p}|U_{\alpha}|&\lesssim S_{p}(1,R/K)^{p}\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{N}\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.\end{split}

To summarize, we have

\big(\alpha^{p}|U_{\alpha}|\big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim\big(S_{p}(1,\frac{R}{K})+K^{100n}S_{Br,p,K}(1,R)\big)\Big\|(\sum_{\tau_{N}\in\Theta^{n}(R)}|f_{\tau_{N}}^{N}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau_{N}^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

This implies

(6.64)

S_{p}(1,R)\leq C_{p}S_{p}(1,R/K)+C_{p}K^{100n}S_{Br,p,K}(1,R)

for any
R\geq 1
.
After applying the inequality repeatedly, we obtain

(6.65)

S_{p}(1,R)\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}\sup_{1\leq X\leq R}S_{Br,p,K}(1,X)+R^{\epsilon}

provided that
K
is sufficiently large.
This gives the desired result.

6.4.
Proof of Proposition
5.1
assuming Proposition
6.6

Let us give a proof. By replacing
S_{p}(1,R)
by
\sup_{1\leq X\leq R}S_{p}(1,X)
, we may assume that
S_{p}(1,R)
is non-decreasing. For convenience, we introduce the notations

(6.66)

\begin{split}&\widetilde{S}_{p}(1,R):=R^{-\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}S_{p}(1,R)\\
&\widetilde{S_{p}}(R_{k},R):=(\frac{R}{R_{k}})^{-\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}S_{p}(R_{k},R).\end{split}

We need to show that

(6.67)

\begin{split}\widetilde{S}_{p}(1,R)\lesssim R^{\epsilon}.\end{split}

As in Proposition
6.4
, we will consider two cases:
n=3
and
n\geq 4
.
Let us first consider
n\geq 4
.
With the new notations, Proposition
6.3
and
6.4
give the following inequality.

(6.68)

\widetilde{S_{p}}(1,R)\lesssim R^{10^{n}N_{0}\epsilon}+R^{100^{n}\epsilon}\sum_{k=N_{0}}^{N}\widetilde{S_{p}}(R_{k},R).

Recall that
R^{\epsilon(N-k)}=RR_{k}^{-1}
.
Proposition
6.5
becomes

(6.69)

\begin{split}\widetilde{S_{p}}(R_{k},R)&=(\frac{R}{R_{k}})^{-\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}S_{p}(R_{k},R)\\
&\lesssim R^{\epsilon}(\frac{R}{R_{k}})^{-\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\big(\frac{R}{R_{k}}\big)^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\sup_{1\leq X\leq R^{\epsilon}}\widetilde{S_{p}}(1,X)^{N-k}\\
&\lesssim R^{\epsilon}\sup_{1\leq X\leq R^{\epsilon}}\widetilde{S_{p}}(1,X)^{N-k}.\end{split}

Combining these two, we obtain

(6.70)

\widetilde{S_{p}}(1,R)\lesssim R^{10^{n}N_{0}\epsilon}+R^{101^{n}\epsilon}\sup_{1\leq X\leq R^{\epsilon}}\widetilde{S_{p}}(1,X)^{N-N_{0}}.

Take
\eta
so that

(6.71)

\limsup_{R\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\widetilde{S_{p}}(1,R)}{R^{\eta}}>0,\;\;\;\limsup_{R\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\widetilde{S_{p}}(1,R)}{R^{\eta+\epsilon^{\prime}}}=0

for every
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
.
Then we have

(6.72)

\eta\leq\max\big(10^{n}N_{0}\epsilon,100^{n}\epsilon+\eta\epsilon(N-N_{0})\big).

We take
N_{0}=\epsilon^{-1/2}
.
If the first term dominates, then we are already done. Suppose that the second term dominates.
Since
N\epsilon=1
, this implies

(6.73)

\eta\epsilon N_{0}\leq 100^{n}\epsilon.

This gives
\eta\leq 100^{n}/N_{0}
. It suffices to recall that
N_{0}=\epsilon^{-1/2}
.

Let us next consider
n=3
. With the new notations, Proposition
6.3
and
6.4
give the following inequality.

(6.74)

\widetilde{S_{p}}(1,R)\lesssim R^{100N_{0}\epsilon}+R^{100^{3}\epsilon}\sum_{k=N_{0}}^{N}\widetilde{S_{8}}(R_{k},R)

for any
4\leq p\leq 10
. By Proposition
6.5
, we have

(6.75)

\begin{split}\widetilde{S_{8}}(R_{k},R)\lesssim R^{\epsilon}\sup_{1\leq X\leq R^{\epsilon}}\widetilde{S_{8}}(1,X)^{N-k}.\end{split}

Combining these two, we obtain

(6.76)
