\widetilde{S_{p}}(1,R)\lesssim R^{100N_{0}\epsilon}+R^{101^{3}\epsilon}\sup_{1\leq X\leq R^{\epsilon}}\widetilde{S_{8}}(1,X)^{N-N_{0}}.

By the above inequality, it suffices to prove

(6.77)

\widetilde{S}_{8}(1,R)\lesssim R^{100\epsilon^{1/2}}.

The rest of the proof is identical to that for the case
n\geq 4
. We omit the details.

7.
Proof of Proposition
6.6

We prove a general statement of Proposition
6.6
for the use in Section
8
. Recall that
p_{n}=n^{2}+n-2
. Let
\gamma_{n+k}
be an element of
\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R,\epsilon)
. For
1\leq k\leq n-1
, define the
k
-flat cone
\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma_{n+k};R)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+k}
to be

(7.1)

\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(t):t\in[0,1],\;\lambda_{k}\geq\frac{1}{2},\;|\lambda_{i}|\leq\min(1,R^{\frac{k-i}{n}}),\;1\leq i\leq n+k\Big\}.

Write
\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma_{n+k};R)\subset\bigcup_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k}(R)}\theta
where
\Xi_{n,k}(R)
is the collection of
\theta
of the form

\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(t_{\theta}):t\in[t_{\theta},t_{\theta}+R^{-\frac{1}{n}}],\;\lambda_{k}\geq\frac{1}{2},\;|\lambda_{i}|\leq\min(1,R^{\frac{k-i}{n}}),\;1\leq i\leq n+k\Big\}

where
t_{\theta}\in R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\mathbb{Z}
. Note that Proposition
6.6
is a special case of Proposition
7.1
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
1\leq k\leq n-1
.
Assume that Proposition
5.1
is true for
n
.
Then for
4\leq p\leq p_{n}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, we have

\Big\|\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k}(R)}f_{\theta}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}\leq C_{A,p,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}(R^{\frac{1}{n}})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})+\epsilon^{\prime}}\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}

for all curves
\gamma_{n+k}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R,\epsilon)
, and all Schwartz functions
g
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma_{n+k};R)
.

Our proof relies on a bootstrapping argument by
[
PS07
]
. To do that, let us first define a truncated
k
-flat cone.
For
K\geq 100^{n+k}
,
1\leq k\leq n-1
, and
\vec{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})
with
|h_{i}|\leq 1
and
|h_{k}|\geq\frac{1}{2}
, define the truncated
k
-flat cone
\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma_{n+k},K,\vec{h},R)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+k}
to be

(7.2)

\begin{split}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(t):\;t\in[0,1],\;\;&h_{j}\leq\lambda_{j}\leq h_{j}+\frac{1}{K},\;1\leq j\leq k,\\
&|\lambda_{j}|\leq R^{\frac{k-j}{n}},\;k+1\leq j\leq n+k\Big\}.\end{split}

Write
\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma_{n+k},K,\vec{h};R)\subset\bigcup_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R)}\theta
where
\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R)
is the collection of
\theta
of the form

(7.3)

\begin{split}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(t):\;t\in[t_{\theta},t_{\theta}+R^{-\frac{1}{n}}],\;\;&h_{j}\leq\lambda_{j}\leq h_{j}+\frac{1}{K},\;1\leq j\leq k,\\
&|\lambda_{j}|\leq R^{\frac{k-j}{n}},\;k+1\leq j\leq n+k\Big\}.\end{split}

where
t_{\theta}\in R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\mathbb{Z}
.
Define
D_{p,K}(R)
to be the smallest constant such that

\Big\|\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R)}f_{\theta}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}\leq D_{p,K}(R)\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}.

for all curves
\gamma_{n+k}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R,\epsilon)
, for all
\vec{h}
with
|h_{i}|\leq 1
and
|h_{k}|\geq\frac{1}{2}
, and all Schwartz functions
f
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma_{n+k},K,\vec{h};R)
. To prove Proposition
7.1
, it suffices to prove that for
4\leq p\leq p_{n}

(7.4)

D_{p,K}(R)\lesssim(R^{\frac{1}{n}})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+\epsilon^{\prime}}.

We use an iterative argument. To do that, let us define
D_{p,K}(R_{1},R)
to be the smallest constant such that

\begin{split}\Big\|(\sum_{\tau\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R_{1})}&\big|\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R):\theta\subset\tau}f_{\theta}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}\\
&\leq D_{p,K}(R_{1},R)\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}\end{split}

for all curves
\gamma_{n+k}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R,\epsilon)
, for all
\vec{h}
with
|h_{i}|\leq 1
and
|h_{k}|\geq\frac{1}{2}
, and all Schwartz functions
f
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma_{n+k},K,\vec{h};R)
. Note that

(7.5)

D_{p,K}(R)\lesssim K^{O(1)}D_{p,K}(K,R),\;\;\;D_{p,K}(K^{-\epsilon}R,R)\lesssim K^{O(1)}.

Proposition 7.2

(Iterative formula)
.

Let
2\leq p\leq p_{n}
. For every
R\geq K
and
R\geq R_{1}K^{\frac{1}{n}}
, we have

(7.6)

\begin{split}D_{p,K}(R_{1},R)&\lesssim(K^{\frac{\epsilon}{n}})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}}D_{p,K}(K^{{\epsilon}}R_{1},R)\\
&+(K^{\frac{1}{n}})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+\epsilon}D_{p,K}(KR_{1},R).\end{split}

The desired bound (
7.4
) follows by applying (
7.5
) and Proposition
7.2
repeatedly. So it suffices to prove the above proposition. The proof of Proposition
7.2
is analogous to that of Proposition
6.5
. To do that, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.3
.

Assume that Proposition
5.1
is true for
n
. For every
R\geq K^{\frac{100}{\epsilon}}
,
4\leq p\leq p_{n}
, and
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, we have

\Big\|\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R)}f_{\theta}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}\leq C_{p,\epsilon^{\prime}}(K^{\frac{1}{n}})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+\epsilon^{\prime}}\Big\|\big(\sum_{\tau\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(K)}\big|\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}f_{\theta}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau^{*}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}.

for all curves
\gamma_{n+k}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R,\epsilon)
, for all
\vec{h}
with
|h_{i}|\leq 1
and
|h_{k}|\geq\frac{1}{2}
, and all Schwartz functions
f
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma_{n+k},K,\vec{h};R)
.

Lemma
7.3
can be viewed as the base case in the Pramanik-Seeger’s bootstrapping argument. Since we truncated the
k
flat directions to length
K^{-1}
, these directions becomes harmless. The problem is reduced to that for a nondegenerate curve
\gamma_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In this section, we use rescaling frequently. So let us introduce a rescaling map here. For any
1\leq J\leq n
and
X>0
, we define the linear transformation
L_{J,X}:\mathbb{R}^{n+k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n+k}
by

(7.7)

L_{J,X}(\xi):=\Big(X^{n+k-J}\xi_{1},\ldots,X\xi_{n+k-J},\xi_{n+k+1-J},\ldots,X^{-(J-1)}\xi_{n+k}\Big).

Let us begin the proof of Lemma
7.3
.

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

The first step is to decompose the frequency by the triangle inequality.

(7.8)

\Big\|\sum_{\theta\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R)}f_{\theta}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}\lesssim\Big(\sum_{\tau_{0}\in\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(100^{n(n+k)})}\Big\|\sum_{\theta\subset\tau_{0}}f_{\theta}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Let us fix
\tau_{0}
. By translation (see the discussion around
6.45
), we may assume that
\tau_{0}
contains the origin.
By definition (see (
7.2
)), the intersection of the
k
-flat cone with
\tau_{0}
is as follows.

(7.9)

\begin{split}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(t):\;t\in[0,100^{-(n+k)}],\;\;&h_{j}\leq\lambda_{j}\leq h_{j}+\frac{1}{K},\;1\leq j\leq k,\\
&|\lambda_{j}|\leq R^{\frac{k-j}{n}},\;k+1\leq j\leq n+k\Big\}.\end{split}

This set is equal to

\begin{split}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{k}h_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}\lambda_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(t):t\in[0,&100^{-(n+k)}],\;0\leq\lambda_{j}\leq\frac{1}{K},\;1\leq j\leq k,\\
&|\lambda_{j}|\leq R^{\frac{k-j}{n}},\;k+1\leq j\leq n+k\Big\}.\end{split}

Since
\gamma_{n+k}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R,\epsilon)
and
R\geq K^{\frac{100}{\epsilon}}
, we can ignore the perturbation term in the definition of
\gamma_{n}
(see (
5.1
)), and pretend that
\gamma_{n+k}(s)=(\frac{s^{n+k}}{(n+k)!},\frac{s^{n+k-1}}{(n+k-1)!},\ldots,\frac{s}{1!})
. Then the above set is contained in

(7.10)

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{2K^{-1}}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{k}h_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(t):t\in[0,100^{-(n+k)}]\Big\}.\end{split}

Recall that
\frac{1}{2}\leq h_{k}\leq 1
.
We next do rescaling using the map
L_{k,100}(\xi)
(see (
7.7
)). Define the curve
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}(t):=L_{k,100^{n+k}}\big({\gamma}_{n+k}\big(t/100^{n+k}\big)\big)
for
t\in[0,1]
.
Note that

(7.11)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(j)}(t)=100^{-(n+k)j}L_{k,100^{n+k}}\big({\gamma}_{n+k}^{(j)}\big(t/100^{n+k}\big)\big).

If we pretend that
\gamma_{n+k}(s)=(\frac{s^{n+k}}{(n+k)!},\frac{s^{n+k-1}}{(n+k-1)!},\ldots,\frac{s}{1!})
, then we have

(7.12)

100^{(n+k)k}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(k)}(t)=\big(\frac{t^{n}}{n!},\ldots,\frac{t}{1},1,0,\ldots,0\big).

Then the image of (
7.10
) under the rescaling map
L_{k,100}
is given by

(7.13)

\mathcal{N}_{2K^{-1}}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{k}100^{(n+k)j}h_{j}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(j)}(t):t\in[0,1]\Big\}.

We now see the reason why we applied the inequality (
7.8
). Define
\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{n}(t)
to be the projection of
\sum_{j=1}^{k}100^{(n+k)j}h_{j}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(j)}(t)
to the first
n
coordinates. We write

(7.14)

\sum_{j=1}^{k}100^{(n+k)j}h_{j}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(j)}(t)=100^{(n+k)k}h_{k}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(k)}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}100^{(n+k)j}h_{j}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(j)}(t).

Recall that
|h_{j}|\lesssim 1
and
|h_{k}|\sim 1
.
The first term on the right hand side is “essentially” equal to

(\frac{t^{n}}{n!},\ldots,\frac{t}{1!},1,0,\ldots,0).

The second term on the right hand side of (
7.14
) can be viewed as a small perturbation of the first term. Hence, one may see that
\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma_{n}}}
is a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
After getting rid of the last
k
many coordinates, (
7.13
) is contained in

(7.15)

\mathcal{N}_{CK^{-1}}\Big\{\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{n}(t):0\leq t\leq 1\Big\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}.

By following the same argument, the Fourier support of
\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}f_{\theta}
is contained in

(7.16)

\mathcal{N}_{CK^{-1}}\Big\{\widetilde{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{n}(t):t\in[t_{\tau},t_{\tau}+K^{-\frac{1}{n}}]\Big\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}.

Therefore, after freezing the last
k
variables, we can apply the result for a nondegenerate curve (Proposition
5.1
), and obtain the
desired result.
∎

Let us next prove Proposition
7.2
. The proof is very similar to that for Proposition
6.5
. Compared to Proposition
6.5
, the main difference is that we are dealing with the
k
-flat cone here. This makes the rescaling more involved.

Proof of Proposition
7.2
.

Let us first state our goal. To simplify the notation, we use the notation
\Xi(R)
for
\Xi_{n,k,K,\vec{h}}(R)
. By the definition of
D_{p,K}
, what we need to prove is as follows.

(7.17)

\begin{split}&\Big\|(\sum_{\tau\in\Xi(R_{1})}\big|\sum_{\theta\in\Xi(R):\theta\subset\tau}f_{\theta}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\tau^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}\\
&\lesssim\Big((K^{\frac{\epsilon}{n}})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+2\epsilon^{5}}D_{p,K}(K^{{\epsilon}}R_{1},R)\\
&\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;+(K^{\frac{1}{n}})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+\epsilon}D_{p,K}(KR_{1},R)\Big)\Big\|(\sum_{\theta\in\Xi(R)}|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}})^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+k})}\end{split}

for all curves
\gamma_{n+k}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R,\epsilon)
, for all
\vec{h}
with
|h_{i}|\leq 1
and
|h_{k}|\geq\frac{1}{2}
, and all Schwartz functions
f
whose Fourier supports are in
\Gamma_{n,k}(\gamma_{n+k},K,\vec{h};R)
.

To use Proposition
3.9
, let us introduce some notations. Let
q=\frac{p}{2}
. Define

(7.18)

\vec{\delta}:=(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{k-1},R_{1}^{-\frac{1}{n}},\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{n-2}),\;\;\vec{\delta^{\prime\prime}}:=(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{k-1},R^{-\frac{1}{n}},\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{n-2}).

Denote

\vec{\nu}_{\pm}:=(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{k-1},\pm 1,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{n-2}).

Note that for given
\theta\in\Xi(R)
there exists
s^{\prime\prime}
so that
f_{\theta}=f_{p[\vec{\delta}^{\prime\prime},\vec{\nu}_{+}](s^{\prime\prime})}+f_{p[\vec{\delta}^{\prime\prime},\vec{\nu}_{-}](s^{\prime\prime})}
.
By the triangle inequality, we just consider one branch
f_{\theta}=f_{p[\vec{\delta}^{\prime\prime},\vec{\nu}_{+}](s^{\prime\prime})}
. We may omit the
\vec{\nu}
-notation and write
f_{\theta}=f_{p[\vec{\delta}^{\prime\prime}](s^{\prime\prime})}
.
So we may say that
\Xi(R)
and
\{p[\vec{\delta}^{\prime\prime}](s^{\prime\prime})\}_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{R^{-1/n}}}
are the same.
For simplification, use the notation

(7.19)

g_{s}:=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{R^{-1/n}}:|s-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq R_{1}^{-1/n}}f_{p[\vec{\delta^{\prime\prime}}](s^{\prime\prime})}.

By Proposition
3.9
and dilation, we have

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+k}}\Big(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{R_{1}^{-1/n}}}|g_{s}|^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big)^{2}\lessapprox\sum_{R_{1}^{-1/n}\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}\frac{1}{|U|}\Big(\int_{U}\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|g_{s}|^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big)^{2}.\end{split}

We consider two cases depending on the size of
\sigma
:
\sigma\leq K^{\epsilon}R_{1}^{-1/n}
or
\sigma\geq K^{\epsilon}R_{1}^{-1/n}
.
In other words,
we write the right hand side as two terms, and bound each term individually.

(7.20)

\begin{split}&\sum_{R_{1}^{-1/n}\leq\sigma\leq K^{\epsilon}R_{1}^{-1/n}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}\frac{1}{|U|}\Big(\int_{U}\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{R_{1}^{-1/n}}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|g_{s}|^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big)^{2}\\
&+\sum_{K^{\epsilon}R_{1}^{-1/n}\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}\frac{1}{|U|}\Big(\int_{U}\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{R_{1}^{-1/n}}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|g_{s}|^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big)^{2}.\end{split}

In the language of the high/low method, the former term corresponds to the high term and the latter term corresponds to the low term. As we mentioned before, we may say that
\{\theta\}_{\theta\in\Xi(R)}
and
\{p[\vec{\delta^{\prime\prime}}](s^{\prime\prime})\}_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{R^{-1/n}}}
are the same. Hence, it suffices to bound (
7.20
) by the right hand side of (
7.17
).

Let us bound the first term of (
7.20
).
Suppose that
\sigma\leq K^{\epsilon}R_{1}^{-1/n}
. For given
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}
, the number of
s\in\mathbb{I}_{R_{1}^{-1/n}}
such that
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma
is bounded by
K^{\epsilon}
. So by Hölder’s inequality, the first term of (
7.20
) is bounded by

(7.21)

\begin{split}&\lesssim K^{O(\epsilon)}\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{R_{1}^{-1/n}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+k}}|g_{s}|^{p}\\
&\lesssim K^{O(\epsilon)}\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{R_{1}^{-1/n}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+k}}\Big|\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{R^{-1/n}}:|s-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq R_{1}^{-1/n}}f_{p[\vec{\delta^{\prime\prime}}](s^{\prime\prime})}\Big|^{p}\\
&\lesssim K^{O(\epsilon)}\sum_{\tau\in\Xi(R_{1})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+k}}\Big|\sum_{\theta\in\Xi(R):\theta\subset\tau}f_{\theta}\Big|^{p}.\end{split}

We next do rescaling and apply Lemma
7.3
. Let us first fix
\tau\in\Xi(R_{1})
. By using the linear transformation in the discussion below (
6.45
), we may assume that
\tau
contains the origin. Hence, by definition,

(7.22)

\begin{split}\tau=\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}a_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(0):\;&h_{j}\leq a_{j}\leq h_{j}+\frac{1}{K},\;1\leq j\leq k,\;\;\\
&0\leq a_{j}\leq(R_{1})^{\frac{k-j}{n}},\;\;k+1\leq j\leq n+k\Big\}.\end{split}

Also,
\theta\subset\tau
is expressed by

(7.23)

\begin{split}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}a_{j}\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(s_{\theta}):\;&h_{j}\leq a_{j}\leq h_{j}+\frac{1}{K},\;1\leq j\leq k,\;\;\\
&0\leq a_{j}\leq(R)^{\frac{k-j}{n}},\;\;k+1\leq j\leq n+k\Big\}\end{split}

where
|s_{\theta}|\leq R_{1}^{-1/n}
.
We use the rescaling map
L_{k,R_{1}^{1/n}}
(see (
7.7
) for the definition).
Since
\gamma_{n+k}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R,\epsilon)
, we have the expression

(7.24)

\gamma_{n+k}(s)=\big(\frac{s^{n+k}}{(n+k)!},\frac{s^{n+k-1}}{(n+k-1)!},\ldots,\frac{s}{1!}\big)+R^{-\epsilon}s^{n+k+1}E_{\epsilon}(s)

where
E_{\epsilon}(s)
is a polynomial of degree at most
\epsilon^{-1}
and coefficients are bounded by
A
.
Let us see how
\theta\subset\tau
changes under the rescaling. Define

(7.25)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}(s):=R_{1}^{k/n}L_{k,R_{1}^{1/n}}\big(\gamma_{n+k}(R_{1}^{-1/n}s)\big).

Note that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R,\epsilon)
. We have

(7.26)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(j)}(s)=R_{1}^{-j/n}R_{1}^{k/n}L_{k,R_{1}^{1/n}}\big(\gamma_{n+k}^{(j)}(R_{1}^{-1/n}s)\big).

Let
\widetilde{s_{\theta}}=R_{1}^{1/n}s_{\theta}
and
\tilde{h}_{j}=R^{-\frac{k-j}{n}}h_{j}
.
The image of
\theta
under the map
L_{k,R_{1}^{1/n}}
is

(7.27)

\begin{split}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}a_{j}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(j)}(\widetilde{s}_{\theta}):\;&\widetilde{h}_{j}\leq a_{j}\leq\widetilde{h}_{j}+\frac{R_{1}^{-\frac{k-j}{n}}}{K},\;1\leq j\leq k,\;\;\\
&0\leq a_{j}\leq(R_{1}^{-1}R)^{\frac{k-j}{n}},\;\;k+1\leq j\leq n+k\Big\}.\end{split}

So we can apply Lemma
7.3
. After rescaling back, (
7.21
) is bounded by

(7.28)

\begin{split}&\lesssim K^{O(\epsilon)}K^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})p}\sum_{\tau}\Big\|\big(\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\Xi(KR_{1}):\tau^{\prime}\subset\tau}\big|\sum_{\theta\subset\tau^{\prime}}f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{(\tau^{\prime})^{*}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}^{p}\\
&\lesssim K^{O(\epsilon)}K^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})p}\Big\|\big(\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\Xi(KR_{1})}\big|\sum_{\theta\subset\tau^{\prime}}f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{(\tau^{\prime})^{*}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}^{p}.\end{split}

After applying the definition of
D_{p,K}(KR_{1},R)
to the right hand side, this is further bounded by

(7.29)

K^{O(\epsilon)}K^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})p}D_{p,K}(KR_{1},R)\Big\|\big(\sum_{\theta}\big|f_{\theta}|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}^{p}.

This is the second term on the right hand side of (
7.17
). This takes care of the first term of (
7.20
).

Let us bound the second term of (
7.20
). We claim that for every
\tau\in\Xi(R_{1})
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+k}}\Big|\sum_{\theta\in\Xi(R):\theta\subset\tau}f_{\theta}\Big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}\lesssim(K^{\frac{\epsilon}{n}})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})\frac{p}{2}+\epsilon^{5}}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\Xi(K^{\epsilon}R_{1}):\tau^{\prime}\subset\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+k}}\Big|\sum_{\theta\subset\tau^{\prime}}f_{\theta}\Big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}

for any
U\|(q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}
. Here, we recall that
\vec{\delta}
is defined in
(
7.18
)
,
K^{\epsilon}R_{1}^{-1/n}\leq\sigma\leq 1
, and
s^{\prime}
corresponds to
\tau
.

Once we have the claim, by the claim, the second line of (
7.20
) is bounded by

(7.30)

(K^{\frac{\epsilon}{n}})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})p+2\epsilon^{5}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+k}}\Big(\sum_{\tau^{\prime}}\big|\sum_{\theta\subset\tau^{\prime}}f_{\theta}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{(\tau^{\prime})^{*}}\Big)^{2}.

Apply the definition of
D_{p,K}
, and the above inequality is bounded by

(7.31)

(K^{\frac{\epsilon}{n}})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})p+2\epsilon^{5}}D_{p,K}(K^{\epsilon}R_{1},R)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+k}}\Big(\sum_{\theta}\big|f_{\theta}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}*w_{\theta^{*}}\Big)^{2}.

This is the first term on the right hand side of (
7.17
).

Let us prove the claim. Fix
\tau\in\Xi(R_{1})
. As we did before, by using the linear transformation (see the discussion around (
6.45
)), we may assume that
\tau
contains the origin (in other words,
s^{\prime}=0
). Let us compute the size of
U
. Recall that

q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma]=\{(a_{i}):|a_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{R_{1}}\min\{\sigma^{i-n-k},R_{1}\rho_{i-1}\}(1\leq i\leq n+k)\}.

Hence, by definition, we have

(7.32)

q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma](0)=\{(a_{i}):|a_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{R_{1}}\min\{\sigma^{1-i},R_{1}\rho_{n+k-i}\}(1\leq i\leq n+k)\}.

The coordinate is reversed since
{\bf e}_{i}(0)=(\underbrace{0,\cdots,0}_{n+k-i},1,\underbrace{0,\cdots,0}_{i-1})
.

Note that

(7.33)

\sigma^{1-i}\leq R_{1}\rho_{n+k-i}=R_{1}(R_{1}^{-\frac{1}{n}})^{n-i+1}\;\;\mathrm{for}\;\;1\leq i\leq n+1.

We don’t use any information on the last
k
coordinates of
q[\vec{\delta};\leq\sigma](0)
. We write
U
as follows.

(7.34)

[-R_{1},R_{1}]\times[-R_{1}\sigma,R_{1}\sigma]\times\cdots\times[-R_{1}\sigma^{n-1},R_{1}\sigma^{n-1}]\times B

where
B
is a set in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
The next step is applying rescaling in
(
7.7
)
.
After applying the inverse map of
L:=L_{k,R_{1}^{1/n}}
, we have

(7.35)

L^{-1}(U)=[-1,1]\times[-R_{1}^{\frac{1}{n}}\sigma,R_{1}^{\frac{1}{n}}\sigma]\times\cdots\times[-R_{1}^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\sigma^{n-1},R_{1}^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\sigma^{n-1}]\times\widetilde{B}

for some rectangular box
\widetilde{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
. We don’t use any information on
\widetilde{B}
. Since
\sigma\geq K^{\epsilon}R_{1}^{-1/n}
, this set is large enough for our purpose. Note that we used the same rescaling map to deal with the first term of (
7.20
).
On the other hand, the image of
\theta\in\Xi(R)
satisfying
\theta\subset\tau
under the map
L
becomes
(
7.27
)
. Hence,
\cup_{\theta\subset\tau}L(\theta)
is contained in

(7.36)

\begin{split}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+k}a_{j}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(j)}(s):\;&0\leq s\leq 1,\;\widetilde{h}_{j}\leq a_{j}\leq\widetilde{h}_{j}+\frac{R_{1}^{-\frac{k-j}{n}}}{K},\;1\leq j\leq k,\;\;\\
&0\leq a_{j}\leq(R_{1}^{-1}R)^{\frac{k-j}{n}},\;\;k+1\leq j\leq n+k\Big\}.\end{split}

Here
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+k}(A,R_{1},\epsilon)
,
|\widetilde{h}_{j}|\lesssim R^{-\frac{k-j}{n}}
and
|\widetilde{h}_{k}|\sim 1
. So the contribution from
a_{1},\ldots,a_{j-1}
is minor. Take
\gamma_{n}(s)
to be the first
n
coordinates of the curve
\widetilde{h}_{k}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+k}^{(k)}(s)
. Then
\gamma_{n}
becomes a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Also, the set
L(\theta)
is contained in

(7.37)

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{K^{-1}}\Big\{\gamma_{n}(R_{1}^{-\frac{1}{n}}s_{\theta})+&\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\gamma_{n}^{(j)}(R_{1}^{-\frac{1}{n}}s_{\theta}):\;\\
&|a_{j}|\leq(R_{1}^{-1}R)^{-\frac{j}{n}},\;\;1\leq j\leq n\Big\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}.\end{split}

By applying the change of variables, the claim is reduced to proving

(7.38)

\int\Big|\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}f_{L(\theta)}\Big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{L^{-1}(U)}\lesssim K^{\frac{\epsilon}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})\frac{p}{2}+\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{n}}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\subset\tau}\int\Big|\sum_{\theta\subset\tau^{\prime}}f_{L(\theta)}\Big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{L^{-1}(U)}.

Since the first coordinate of
L^{-1}(U)
is small (recall (
7.35
)), we use only the coordinates from the second to the
n
th. Note that
L^{-1}(U)
contains

(7.39)

[-1,1]\times[-K^{\epsilon},K^{\epsilon}]^{n-1}\times\widetilde{B}.

We freeze the first coordinate and the coordinates from the
(n+1)
th to the
(n+k)
th.
To apply the decoupling for a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
by
[
BDG16
]
, we need to check that
\frac{p_{n}}{2}\leq(n-1)^{2}+(n-1)
. Since
p_{n}=n^{2}+n-2
, the inequality is true for any
n\geq 2
. Hence, we obtain (
7.38
), and this finishes the proof.

8.
Proof of Theorem
2.30

In this section, we prove Theorem
2.30
. Let us recall the theorem.

Theorem 8.1
.

Fix an admissible pair
(\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu})
at scale
R
. Let
\delta=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{i}
. Let
\mathbb{I}_{\delta}
be a
\delta
-separated subset of
[0,1]
. For
4\leq p\leq 4(n-1)
, we have

(8.1)

\Big\|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}a_{s}\Big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\phi_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)}\Big)^{\vee}(\cdot-R\mathbf{n}_{s})\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta^{-1}R^{\frac{1}{p}}R^{-(\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p}))+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{R}^{n}})}

for any function
f
satisfying
\widehat{f}\subset B_{1}^{n}\setminus B_{\frac{1}{2}}^{n}
,
|a_{s}|\lesssim 1
, and
\mathbf{n}_{s}\in\Gamma(s)
. Here, we simply denote
p[\vec{\delta}](s)=p[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
.

To simplify the notation, define the operator

(8.2)

Tf(x,t):=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}a_{s}\big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\phi_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)}\big)^{\vee}\big((x,t)-R\mathbf{n}_{s}\big).

We also use the notation

(8.3)

T_{s}f(x,t):=a_{s}\big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\phi_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)}\big)^{\vee}\big((x,t)-R\mathbf{n}_{s}\big).

By the triangle inequality, we may assume that all components of
\vec{\nu}
have the same sign. Recall that
p[\vec{\delta}](s)
is the
R^{-1}
-dilation of the set
P[\vec{\delta},\vec{\nu}](s)
, and this rectangular box is defined in (
2.8
). So
p[\vec{\delta}](s)
has the dimension

(8.4)

1\times\rho_{1}\times\cdots\times\rho_{n-1}\times R^{-1},

where
\rho_{j}=\delta_{1}^{j}\delta_{2}^{j-1}\cdots\delta_{j}
. With the new notations, our goal is to prove

(8.5)

\|Tf\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta^{-1}R^{\frac{1}{p}}R^{-\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p})+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{R}^{n}})}

for any
\delta
and
4\leq p\leq 4(n-1)
. We first state three propositions, and derive Theorem
8.1
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
p_{c}:=4(n-1)
. For any
\epsilon>0
, we have

(8.6)

\|Tf\|_{L^{p_{c}}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta^{-1}R^{-\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p_{c}})+\epsilon}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}\|T_{s}f\|_{\infty}^{\frac{p_{c}-4}{p_{c}}}\Big\|\big(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}|T_{s}f|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}^{\frac{4}{p_{c}}}.

Proposition 8.3
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, we have

(8.7)

\|Tf\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta^{-1}R^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{n}+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n}})}.

Proposition 8.4
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, we have

(8.8)

\Big\|\big(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}|T_{s}f|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}^{4}\lesssim R^{1+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n}})}^{4}.

Let us assume these propositions and finish the proof. By interpolation, it suffices to prove for
p=4
and
p=4(n-1)
.
By the uncertainty principle, we may assume that
f
is constant on a unit cube. By losing a power of
O(\log R)
and pigeonholing argument, we may assume that
|f|\sim X
on the support of
f
for some constant
X
. By Proposition
8.2
,

(8.9)

\|Tf\|_{L^{p_{c}}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}^{p_{c}}\lesssim\delta^{-p_{c}}R^{-\frac{p_{c}}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p_{c}})+p_{c}\epsilon}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}\|T_{s}f\|_{\infty}^{{p_{c}-4}}\Big\|\big(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}|T_{s}f|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}^{4}.

By Proposition
8.4
and the inequality
\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}\|T_{s}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|f\|_{\infty}
,
we have

(8.10)

\|Tf\|_{L^{p_{c}}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}^{p_{c}}\lesssim\delta^{-p_{c}}R^{-\frac{p_{c}}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p_{c}})+p_{c}\epsilon}R\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p_{c}-4}\|f\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n}})}^{4}.

Since
|f|\sim X
on the support of
f
, we have

(8.11)

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p_{c}-4}\|f\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}}^{n})}^{4}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{p_{c}}(W_{B_{R}^{n}})}^{p_{c}}.

This completes the proof. It remains to prove Proposition
8.2
,
8.3
, and
8.4
.

8.1.
Proof of Proposition
8.2
and
8.3

The proofs of two proposition are essentially the same.
We use an inductive argument. Let us first fix
\vec{\delta}=(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n-1})
. For
1\leq J\leq n-1
, define

(8.12)

\vec{\delta}_{(J)}:=(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{J},1,\ldots,1).

\vec{\nu}_{(J)}:=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J},0,\ldots,0).

Let
r_{J}:=\delta_{1}^{-n}\cdots\delta_{J}^{-(n+1-J)}
. Let
P[\vec{\delta}_{(J)},\vec{\nu}_{(J)}]
be the
(\vec{\delta}_{(J)},\vec{\nu}_{(J)})
-plank at scale
r_{J}
.
Define

(8.13)

p[\vec{\delta}_{(J)}](s):=r_{J}^{-1}P[\vec{\delta}_{(J)},\vec{\nu}_{(J)}](s).

We have the nested sequence:

p[\vec{\delta}](s)\subset p[\vec{\delta}_{(n-2)}](s)\subset\dots\subset p[\vec{\delta}_{(1)}](s).

To prove the propositions, we use the following inequality.

Proposition 8.5
.

For every
1\leq J\leq n-2
,
4\leq p\leq p_{n-J}
and any
\epsilon>0
, we have

(8.14)

\begin{split}\Big\|\big(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{J}}}\big|&\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{J+1}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{J}}}F_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s^{\prime})}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\\
&\lesssim(\delta_{J+1})^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+\epsilon)}\Big\|\big(\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{J+1}}}|F_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s^{\prime})}|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\end{split}

for any functions
F_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s^{\prime})}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with
\widehat{F}_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s^{\prime})}\subset p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s^{\prime})
.

Let us assume Proposition
8.4
and
8.5
, and finish the proof of Proposition
8.2
and
8.3
. Let us first prove Proposition
8.3
, which is easier. By Theorem
4.3
with a standard localization argument, we have

(8.15)

\|Tf\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta_{1}^{-\epsilon}\Big\|\Big(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}}}\big|\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{1}}T_{s^{\prime}}f\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}.

By Proposition
8.5
and Proposition
8.4
, we have

(8.16)

\|Tf\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta^{-\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{4}+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n}})}.

To prove Proposition
8.3
,
it suffices to prove

(8.17)

1\lesssim\delta^{-1}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}.

Recall that
R=\delta_{1}^{-n}\cdots\delta_{n-1}^{-2}
and
\delta=\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n-1}
. After rearranging the terms and raising the
n
th power, the above inequality is equivalent to

(8.18)

(\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n-1})^{n}\lesssim\delta_{1}^{n}\cdots\delta_{n-1}^{2}.

This is true. So we proved Proposition
8.3
.

Let us next prove Proposition
8.2
. For simplicity, we use the notation
p_{c}=4(n-1)
. Recall that
p_{n}=n^{2}+n-2
. Let
m
be the largest integer such that

(8.19)

p_{n+1-m}=(n+1-m)^{2}+(n+1-m)-2\geq p_{c}.

For simplicity, we use the following notation.

(8.20)

\begin{split}&T_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(m)}](s)}f(x):=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{m}}T_{s^{\prime}}f(x),\qquad s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{m}}\\
&T_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)}f(x):=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta}T_{s^{\prime}}f(x),\qquad s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}.\end{split}

By Proposition
4.6
and Proposition
8.5
, we have

(8.21)

\begin{split}\|Tf\|_{p_{c}}^{p_{c}}\lesssim R^{O(\epsilon)}(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}^{-1})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{c}})p_{c}}\Big\|(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{m}}}|T_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(m)}](s)}f|^{\frac{p_{c}}{2}})^{\frac{2}{p_{c}}}\Big\|_{p_{c}}^{p_{c}}.\end{split}

By the definition of
m
(see (
8.19
)), we cannot apply Proposition
8.5
. Hence, we take
L^{\infty}
and bound (
8.21
) by

(8.22)

\begin{split}\lesssim R^{O(\epsilon)}(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}^{-1})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{c}})p_{c}}&\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{m}}}\|T_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(m)}](s)}f\|_{\infty}^{p_{c}-p_{n-m}}\\
&\times\Big\|(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{m}}}|T_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(m)}](s)}f|^{\frac{p_{n-m}}{2}})^{\frac{2}{p_{n-m}}}\Big\|_{p_{n-m}}^{p_{n-m}}.\end{split}

We can apply Proposition
8.5
and repeat this process. Then this is bounded by

\begin{split}&\lesssim\delta^{-O(\epsilon)}(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}^{-1})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{c}})p_{c}}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{m}}}\|T_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(m)}](s)}f\|_{\infty}^{p_{c}-p_{n-m}}\\
&\times\Big(\prod_{j=m+1}^{n-2}(\delta_{j}^{-1})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{n+1-j}})p_{n+1-j}}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{j}}}\|T_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(j)}](s)}f\|_{\infty}^{p_{n+1-j}-p_{n-j}}\Big)\Big\|(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta}}|T_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)}f|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{4}^{4}.\end{split}

We apply Proposition
8.4
and rearrange the terms. This is further bounded by

(8.23)

\begin{split}&\lesssim R^{O(\epsilon)}(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}^{-1})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{c}})p_{c}}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{m}}}\|T_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(m)}](s)}f\|_{\infty}^{p_{c}-p_{n-m}}\\
&\times\Big(\prod_{j=m+1}^{n-2}(\delta_{j}^{-1})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{n+1-j}})p_{n+1-j}}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{j}}}\|T_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(j)}](s)}f\|_{\infty}^{p_{n+1-j}-p_{n-j}}\Big)R\|f\|_{4}^{4}.\end{split}

Lastly, we use the following trivial estimate.

(8.24)

\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{j}}}\|T_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(j)}](s)}f\|_{\infty}\lesssim\big(\prod_{k=j+1}^{n}\delta_{k}^{-1}\big)\sup_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n-1}}}\|T_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)}f\|_{\infty}.

Then (
8.23
) is bounded by

(8.25)

\begin{split}&\lesssim R^{O(\epsilon)}(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}^{-1})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{c}})p_{c}}\big(\prod_{k=m+1}^{n-1}\delta_{k}^{-1}\big)^{p_{c}-p_{n-m}}\\
&\times\Big(\prod_{j=m+1}^{n-2}\big((\delta_{j}^{-1})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{n+1-j}})p_{n+1-j}}\big(\prod_{k=j+1}^{n-1}\delta_{k}^{-1}\big)^{p_{n+1-j}-p_{n-j}}\big)\Big)R\|f\|_{4}^{4}.\end{split}

Hence, to prove Proposition
8.2
, by recalling
R=\delta_{1}^{-n}\cdots\delta_{n-1}^{-2}
, it suffices to check

(8.26)

\begin{split}&\big(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}^{-1}\big)^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{c}})p_{c}}\big(\prod_{k=m+1}^{n-1}\delta_{k}^{-1}\big)^{p_{c}-p_{n-m}}\\
&\times\Big(\prod_{j=m+1}^{n-2}\big((\delta_{j}^{-1})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{n+1-j}})p_{n+1-j}}(\prod_{k=j+1}^{n-1}\delta_{k}^{-1})^{p_{n+1-j}-p_{n-j}}\big)\Big)\\
&\lesssim\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\delta_{j}^{-p_{c}}\Big)(\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\delta_{j}^{n-j+1})^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p_{c}})p_{c}}.\end{split}

Since
p_{c}=4(n-1)
, by direct computations, one may see that

(8.27)

\begin{split}\textup{RHS}=\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\delta_{j}^{-2n-2j+6}.\end{split}

We need to compute the left hand side of (
8.26
). By computations, the exponent of
\delta_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq m
is equal to
-2n+4
. Let us next consider the case that
m+1\leq j\leq n-1
. By computations, the exponent of
\delta_{j}
is as follows.

(8.28)

\begin{split}-(p_{c}-p_{n-m})-\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p_{n+1-j}}\Big)p_{n+1-j}-(p_{n-m}-p_{n+1-j}).\end{split}

This number is equal to

(8.29)

-p_{c}+\frac{p_{n+1-j}}{2}+2.

So the left hand side of (
8.26
) is equal to

(8.30)

\big(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}^{-2n+4}\big)\big(\prod_{j=m+1}^{n-1}\delta^{-p_{c}+\frac{p_{n+1-j}}{2}+2}\big).

So by comparing the right hand side and left hand side of (
8.26
), what we need to check becomes as follows. For
m+1\leq j\leq n-1
,

(8.31)

-p_{c}+\frac{p_{n+1-j}}{2}+2\geq-2n-2j+6.

Recall that
p_{c}=4n-4
and
p_{n+1-j}=(n+1-j)^{2}+(n+1-j)-2
.
By calculations, one can see that this is the case. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

The local smoothing estimate
(
8.1
)
is not true for
p>4(n-1)
by the counterexample of
[
BH25
]
.
Let us explain why our proof does not give (
8.1
) for
p>4(n-1)
. Suppose that
p>4(n-1)
. If we follow our argument, (
8.1
) is reduced to proving (
8.26
) where
p_{c}
is now replaced by
p
. If (
8.26
) were true for
p
, then by comparing the exponent of
\delta_{n-1}
, we must have

(8.32)

-p+\frac{p_{2}}{2}+2\geq-p+\frac{2}{n}(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{p})p.

Since
p_{2}=4
, by computations, this is equivalent to
p\leq 4(n-1)
. So our proof cannot give (
8.1
) for
p>4(n-1)
.

8.2.
Proof of Proposition
8.5

Next, we prove Proposition
8.5
. The proof is very similar to that for Proposition
6.5
except that our rescaling is more involved.

Proof of Proposition
8.5
.

Let
4\leq p\leq p_{n-J}
. Fix
\epsilon>0
.
Since our argument relies on iteration, we need to introduce intermediate scales.

(8.33)

\begin{split}&\vec{\delta}_{(J)}^{\prime}:=(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{J},\delta_{J+1}^{\prime},\ldots,1),\\
&\vec{\delta}_{(J)}^{\prime\prime}:=(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{J},\delta_{J+1}^{\prime\prime},\ldots,1).\end{split}

Here
\delta_{J+1}\leq\delta_{J+1}^{\prime}\leq 1
and
\delta_{J+1}^{\prime\prime}:=\delta_{J+1}^{\prime}\delta_{J+1}^{\epsilon}
.
We also introduce

(8.34)

\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}:=\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{J}\delta_{J+1}^{\prime},\;\;\;\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime\prime}:=\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{J}\delta_{J+1}^{\prime\prime}.

We claim that

(8.35)

\begin{split}&\Big\|\big(\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}}}\big|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{(J+1)}}:|s^{\prime}-s|\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}}F_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s)}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}}\\
&\lesssim(\delta_{J+1}^{\epsilon})^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+\epsilon)}\Big\|\big(\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime\prime}}}\big|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{(J+1)}}:|s-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime\prime}}F_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s)}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}}\\
&+(\delta_{J+1}^{\prime-1}\delta_{J+1})^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+\epsilon)}\Big\|\big(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{(J+1)}}}|F_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s)}|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}}.\end{split}

Once we have the claim, by applying this inequality repeatedly, we get the desired result. So, let us prove the claim. For simplicity, let us use the notation
p(s):=p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s)
.
Apply Proposition
3.9
to the left hand side of (
8.35
). Then the left hand side is bounded by

(8.36)

\sum_{\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime\prime}))^{*}}|U|\Big(\int_{U}\sum_{s^{\prime}:|s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq\sigma}\big|\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta^{\prime}_{(J+1)}}F_{p(s)}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}\Big)^{2}.

We consider two cases depending on the size of
\sigma
:

(8.37)

\sigma\leq{\delta}_{(J+1)}^{\prime}\delta_{J+1}^{-\epsilon}\;\;\;\mathrm{or}\;\;\;\sigma\geq{\delta}_{(J+1)}^{\prime}\delta_{J+1}^{-\epsilon}.

So we write the right hand side of (
8.36
) as follows.

(8.38)

\begin{split}&\sum_{\sigma\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}\delta_{J+1}^{-\epsilon}}\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{s^{\prime}:|s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq\sigma}\big|\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta^{\prime}_{(J+1)}}F_{p(s)}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}\Big)^{2}\\
&+\sum_{\sigma\geq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}\delta_{J+1}^{-\epsilon}}\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{s^{\prime}:|s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq\sigma}\big|\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta^{\prime}_{(J+1)}}F_{p(s)}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}\Big)^{2}.\end{split}

To deal with the first term, we use Proposition
7.1
. To deal with the second term, we use a decoupling inequality for a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n-J-1}
.

Let us first bound the first case. After applying Hölder’s inequality, the first term is bounded by

(8.39)

\delta_{J+1}^{-O(\epsilon)}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{{\delta}_{(J+1)}^{\prime}}}\Big\|\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}}F_{p(s)}\Big\|_{L^{p}}^{p}.

Let us fix
s^{\prime}
. By using the linear transformation argument (see the discussion around (
6.45
)), we may assume that
s^{\prime}=0
. We need to deal with

(8.40)

\Big\|\sum_{|s|\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}}F_{p(s)}\Big\|_{L^{p}}^{p}.

We next apply the rescaling. Define the rescaling map
\widetilde{L}_{J,X}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
by

(8.41)

\widetilde{L}_{J,X}(\xi):=\Big(X^{n+1-J}\xi_{1},\ldots,X\xi_{n+1-J},\xi_{n+2-J},\ldots,X^{-(J-1)}\xi_{n+1}\Big).

Here is the rescaling map we use.

(8.42)

L(\xi):=L_{{J+1},(\delta_{J+1}^{\prime})^{-1}}\circ L_{J,{\delta_{J}^{-1}}}\circ\cdots\circ L_{1,{\delta_{1}^{-1}}}(\xi).

By definition,

\begin{split}L_{1,\delta_{1}^{-1}}(\xi)&:=(\delta_{1}^{-n}\xi_{1},\delta_{1}^{-(n-1)}\xi_{2},\ldots,\delta_{1}^{-1}\xi_{n},\xi_{n+1}),\\
L_{2,\delta_{2}^{-1}}(\xi)&:=(\delta_{2}^{-(n-1)}\xi_{1},\ldots,\delta_{2}^{-1}\xi_{n-1},\xi_{n},\delta_{2}\xi_{n+1}),\\
&\vdots\\
L_{J,\delta_{J}^{-1}}(\xi)&:=(\delta_{J}^{-(n+1-J)}\xi_{1},\ldots,\delta_{J}^{-1}\xi_{n+1-J},\xi_{n+2-J},\delta_{J}\xi_{n+3-J},\ldots,\delta_{J}^{J-1}\xi_{n+1}),\\
L_{J+1,(\delta_{J+1}^{\prime})^{-1}}(\xi)&:=((\delta_{J+1}^{\prime})^{-(n-J)}\xi_{1},\ldots,(\delta_{J+1}^{\prime})^{-1}\xi_{n-J},\xi_{n+1-J},\ldots,(\delta_{J+1}^{\prime})^{J}\xi_{n+1}).\end{split}

One may see that
p(s)=p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s)
is contained the following set.

(8.43)

\begin{split}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}a_{j}\gamma_{n+1}^{(j)}(s):\;&|a_{J+1}|\sim\widetilde{\rho}_{J},\;\;|a_{j}|\lesssim\widetilde{\rho}_{j-1},\;\;j\neq J+1\Big\}.\end{split}

Here, the number
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}
is defined by

(8.44)

\widetilde{\rho}_{j}=\begin{cases}1,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\,j=0\\
\delta_{1}^{j}\cdots\delta_{j},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;1\leq j\leq J+1\\
\delta_{1}^{j}\cdots\delta_{J}^{j-J+1}(\delta_{J+1})^{j-J},\;\;\;J+2\leq j\leq n\end{cases}

Recall the definition of
\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}
in (
8.34
). Define

(8.45)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+1}(s):=\big(\delta_{1}\delta_{2}^{2}\cdots\delta_{J}^{J}(\delta_{J+1}^{\prime})^{J+1}\big)^{-1}L\big(\gamma_{n+1}(\delta^{\prime}_{(J+1)}s)\big).

For
j\geq J+1
we have

(8.46)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+1}^{(j)}(s)=\widetilde{\rho}_{j-1}(\delta_{J+1}\delta_{J+1}^{\prime-1})^{J-(j-1)}L\big(\gamma_{n+1}^{(j)}(\delta^{\prime}_{(J+1)}s)\big).

Then
the set
L(p(s))
is contained in

(8.47)

\begin{split}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}a_{j}&\widetilde{\gamma}_{n+1}^{(j)}\big((\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime})^{-1}s\big):\;\\
&|a_{J+1}|\sim 1,\;\;|a_{j}|\lesssim((\delta_{J+1}^{\prime})^{-1}\delta_{J+1})^{j-1-J},\;J+2\leq j\leq n+1\Big\}.\end{split}

Note that this is a form of
\Xi_{n-J,J+1}((\delta_{J+1}^{\prime-1}\delta_{J+1})^{n-J})
.
So we can apply the
l^{p/2}
function estimate (Proposition
7.1
) with
(n,k)
replaced by
(n-J,J+1)
. Hence, the first term of (
8.38
) is bounded by

(8.48)

\delta_{J+1}^{-O(\epsilon)}(\delta_{J+1}^{\prime-1}\delta_{J+1})^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+\epsilon)}\Big\|\big(\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{(J+1)}}}|F_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s)}|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}}.

This gives the desired result because this is the second term of (
8.35
) on the right hand side.

Let us next bound the second term of (
8.38
). We use
\widetilde{R}:=\delta_{1}^{-n}\cdots\delta_{J+1}^{-n+J}
. We need to deal with

(8.49)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Big|\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}}F_{p(s)}\Big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}.

The proof of this part is essentially parallel to that for dealing with the second term of (
7.20
).
As before, by using the linear transformation argument (see the discussion around (
6.45
)), we may assume that
s^{\prime}=0
, and we use the same rescaling map
L
. Since
U\|(q[\leq\sigma](0))^{*}
, the set
U
has the dimension

(8.50)

[-\widetilde{R},\widetilde{R}]\times[-\widetilde{R}\sigma,\widetilde{R}\sigma]\times\cdots\times[-\widetilde{R}\sigma^{n-J},\widetilde{R}\sigma^{n-J}]\times B

for some rectangular box
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{J}
(see the discussion around (
7.32
)).
Apply
L
to the frequency variables and
L^{-1}
to the physical variables. The domain
U
becomes
L^{-1}(U)
, which is comparable to

[-1,1]\times[-(\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime-1}\sigma),(\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime-1}\sigma)]\times\cdots\times[-(\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime-1}\sigma)^{n-J},(\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime-1}\sigma)^{n-J}]\times\widetilde{B}

for some rectangular box
\widetilde{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{J+1}
(compare this with (
7.35
)). By the assumption that
\sigma\geq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}\delta_{J+1}^{-\epsilon}
, the set contains

(8.51)

[-1,1]\times[-\delta_{J+1}^{-\epsilon},\delta_{J+1}^{-\epsilon}]^{n-J-1}\times\tilde{B}.

Freeze the first coordinate and the coordinates from the
(n-J+1)
th to the
(n+1)
th, and apply the decoupling inequality for a nondegenerate curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n-J-1}
by
[
BDG16
]
. To apply the theorem, we need to check that

(8.52)

\frac{p}{2}\leq(n-J-1)(n-J).

Recall that we have the condition
p\leq p_{n-J}=(n-J)^{2}+(n-J)-2
. So (
8.52
) is true. By the decoupling theorem, we have

(8.53)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Big|\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}}F_{p(s)}\Big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}\\
&\lesssim(\delta_{J+1}^{-\epsilon})^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})\frac{p}{2}+\epsilon}\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}_{(J+1)}}:|s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Big|\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime}}F_{p(s)}\Big|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}.\end{split}

By embedding, the second term of (
8.38
) is bounded by

(8.54)

(\delta_{J+1}^{\epsilon})^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}+\epsilon)}\Big\|\big(\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime\prime}}}\big|\sum_{s\in\mathbb{I}_{\delta_{(J+1)}}:|s-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq\delta_{(J+1)}^{\prime\prime}}F_{p[\vec{\delta}_{(J+1)}](s)}\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}}\Big\|_{L^{p}}.

This is the second term of (
8.35
) on the right hand side.
∎

8.3.
Proof of Proposition
8.4

The proof is a simple application of Proposition
3.9
. By Proposition
3.9
with
q=2
, we have

(8.55)

\begin{split}&\Big\|\big(\sum_{s}|T_{s}f|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}^{4}\\
&\lesssim\delta^{-\epsilon}\sum_{\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|T_{s}f|^{2}w_{U}\Big)^{2}.\end{split}

Recall that

(8.56)

T_{s}f(x,t):=a_{s}\big(\widehat{f}(\xi)\phi_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)}\big)^{\vee}\big((x,t)-R\mathbf{n}_{s}\big)

where
|a_{s}|\lesssim 1
.
By the property of
U
, (
8.55
) is bounded by

(8.57)

\delta^{-\epsilon}\sum_{\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}|U|\Big(\int\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)})^{\vee}(x,t)|^{2}w_{U}(x,t)\Big)^{2}.

For given
U
, we tile the frequency space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
by using a translation of the dual set of
U
(which is denoted by
U^{*}
).
By the
L^{2}
-orthogonality, we have

(8.58)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)})^{\vee}|^{2}w_{U}&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}\sum_{V\|U^{*}}|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)}\chi_{V})^{\vee}|^{2}w_{U}\\
&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\sum_{V\|U^{*}}\sum_{s:|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta}](s)}\chi_{V})^{\vee}|^{2}w_{U}.\end{split}

Let us fix
\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1
. By Lemma
2.17
, there exists
\vec{\delta^{\prime}}=(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\delta_{n-1}^{\prime})
so that

(1)

\sigma=\delta_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\delta_{n-1}^{\prime}

(2)

100p[\vec{\delta^{\prime}}](s^{\prime})
contains the convex hull of
\cup_{|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\sigma}p[\vec{\delta}](s)
.

By Lemma
2.16
,
\{p[\vec{\delta}](s)\}_{s}
is
O(1)
-overlapping.
Hence, (
8.58
) is bounded by

(8.59)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\sum_{V\|U^{*}}\Big|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta^{\prime}}](s^{\prime})}\chi_{V})^{\vee}\Big|^{2}w_{U}.

By these inequalities, (
8.57
) is bounded by

(8.60)

\delta^{-\epsilon}\sum_{\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}\int\Big(\sum_{V\|U^{*}}\big|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta^{\prime}}](s^{\prime})}\chi_{V})^{\vee}\big|^{2}\Big)^{2}W_{U}.

This is further bounded by

(8.61)

\begin{split}&\lesssim\delta^{-\epsilon}\sum_{\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\sum_{U\parallel(q[\leq\sigma](s^{\prime}))^{*}}\int|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta^{\prime}}](s^{\prime})})^{\vee}|^{4}W_{U}\\
&\lesssim\delta^{-\epsilon}\sum_{\delta\leq\sigma\leq 1}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\int|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta^{\prime}}](s^{\prime})})^{\vee}|^{4}W_{B_{R}}\\
&\lesssim\delta^{-2\epsilon}\sup_{\sigma}\big(\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\int|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta^{\prime}}](s^{\prime})})^{\vee}|^{4}W_{B_{R}}\big).\end{split}

The first inequality follows from
the Rubio de Francia’s inequality
[
RdF85
]
.
Claim that for every
2\leq p\leq\infty
we have

(8.62)

\big(\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}\int|(\hat{f}\phi_{p[\vec{\delta^{\prime}}](s^{\prime})})^{\vee}|^{p}W_{B_{R}}\big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim\big(\int|f|^{p}W_{B_{R}}\big)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

By interpolation, it suffices to prove for
p=2
and
p=\infty
. To prove the claim for
p=2
, it suffices to prove that
\{p[\vec{\delta}^{\prime}](s^{\prime})\}_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{\sigma}}
are
O(1)
-overlapping. This is proved in Lemma
2.16
. On the other hand, since
\|\widehat{\phi}_{p[\vec{\delta}^{\prime}](s^{\prime})}\|_{\infty}\lesssim 1
it is straightforward to prove the claim for
p=\infty
. Hence, by interpolation, we obtain (
8.62
).
To summarize, by (
8.55
), (
8.61
) and (
8.62
), we have proved

(8.63)

\Big\|\big(\sum_{s}|T_{s}f|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}\lesssim\delta^{-2\epsilon}\big(\int|f(x)|^{4}W_{B_{R}}(x,t)\,dxdt\big)^{\frac{1}{4}}.

Since
f
is independent of
t
, this implies

(8.64)

\Big\|\big(\sum_{s}|T_{s}f|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n+1}})}^{4}\lesssim R^{1+O(\epsilon)}\|f\|_{L^{4}(W_{B_{R}^{n}})}^{4}.

This completes the proof.

9.
Proof of Theorem
1.4

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.4
.

The theorem is obvious when
p=2
and
p=\infty
. By interpolation, it suffices to prove the estimate for
p=4
and
p=n^{2}+n-2
. For the rest of the section, let us assume that
p=4
or
p=n^{2}+n-2
.
By a localization, we may assume that
f
is supported on the ball
B_{CR}
for some constant
C>0
. It suffices to prove

(9.1)

\|\mathrm{BR}_{R}(f)\|_{L^{p}(B_{R})}\leq\big(1+R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}+R^{\frac{1}{n}}R^{-\frac{n+1}{2p}-\frac{1}{np}}\big)\|f\|_{L^{p}}

Here,
B_{CR}
and
B_{R}
have the same center. By the uncertainty principle, we may further assume that
f
is constant on a unit ball.
By normalization, we may assume that
\|f\|_{p}=1
. Write

(9.2)

f(x)=\sum_{j}f(x)\chi_{|f(x)|\sim 2^{j}}.

Since
f
is constant at scale
1
and supported on
B_{CR}
, there are
O(\log R)
many
j
’s. Denote by
f_{j}
the function
f(x)\chi_{|f(x)|\sim 2^{j}}
. By the wave envelope estimate (Theorem
1.3
),

\|\mathrm{BR}_{R}(f_{j})\|_{L^{p}(B_{R})}\leq C_{\epsilon}R^{\epsilon}R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S^{curve}_{U,{\frac{p}{2}}}f_{j}\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}}^{p}.

Recall that

(9.3)

S_{U,\frac{p}{2}}^{curve}f(x)=\Big(\sum_{\theta\subset\tau}|f_{\theta}(x)|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{U}(x)\Big)^{\frac{2}{p}}.

We need to prove

(9.4)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R^{-\frac{1}{n}}\leq s\leq 1:\\
dyadic\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S^{curve}_{U,{\frac{p}{2}}}f_{j}\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

Since there are
\log(R)
many
s^{\prime}
, it suffices to prove

(9.5)

\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S^{curve}_{U,{\frac{p}{2}}}f_{j}\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

By direct calculations, we have

(9.6)

\begin{split}|U|\|S^{curve}_{U,{\frac{p}{2}}}f_{j}\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}&\lesssim|U|\|S^{curve}_{U,2}f_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{4}\sup_{\theta}\|(f_{j})_{\theta}\|_{\infty}^{p-4}\\
&\lesssim\|S^{curve}_{U,2}f_{j}\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}^{p-4}.\end{split}

Hence,

(9.7)

\begin{split}\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S^{curve}_{U,{\frac{p}{2}}}f_{j}\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}^{p-4}\int(\sum_{\theta}|(f_{j})_{\theta}|^{2})^{2}.\end{split}

To proceed the proof, we use the following inequality

(9.8)

\int(\sum_{\theta}|(f_{j})_{\theta}|^{2})^{2}\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{4}^{4}.

This inequality can be proved by following the proof of Proposition
8.4
. Since the proof is identical, we do not produce it here.
Since
f_{j}(x)=f(x)\chi_{|f(x)|\sim 2^{j}}
, we have
\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}^{p-4}\|f_{j}\|_{4}^{4}\sim\|f_{j}\|_{p}^{p}
. Combining all these inequalities gives

(9.9)

\sum_{\tau\in\Theta^{n}(s^{-n})}\sum_{U\|U_{\tau,R}}|U|\|S^{curve}_{U,{\frac{p}{2}}}f_{j}\|_{L^{\frac{p}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{p}^{p}.

So we proved (
9.4
), and this completes the proof.

10.
Sharpness of Theorem
1.4

In this section, we give a heuristic idea of the sharpness of the Bochner-Riesz type estimate (Theorem
1.4
). The idea goes back to a counterexample of a ball multiplier operator by
[
Fef71
]
.

Let
C_{p}(R)
be the smallests constant such that

(10.1)

\|\mathrm{BR}_{R}(f)\|_{L^{p}}\leq C_{p}(R)\|f\|_{L^{p}}

for all functions
f
. We need to show that

(10.2)

C_{p}(R)\gtrsim 1+R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}+R^{\frac{1}{n}}R^{-\frac{n+1}{2p}-\frac{1}{np}}.

We will construct two examples to prove the lower bound (
10.2
).

The first example can be constructed as follows. Let
f=\sum_{\theta}f_{T_{\theta}}
where
f_{T_{\theta}}
is a wave packet with height one, and
T_{\theta}
has dimension
R^{1/n}\times R^{2/n}\times\cdots\times R
. Here,
\theta
determines the direction of
T_{\theta}
in a canonical way. The volume of the plank is
R^{\frac{n+1}{2}}
. The number of planks is
R^{\frac{1}{n}}
. We take
T_{\theta}
so that a translation of
T_{\theta}
by
R
units along the longest direction intersects the origin.
So we have

(10.3)

\|f\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim R^{\frac{n+1}{2p}+\frac{1}{np}}.

On the other hand, heuristically,
\mathrm{BR}_{R}(f_{T_{\theta}})
is a wave packet with height comparable to one and essentially supported on
10T_{\theta}
.
By considering the outer part and inner part, we have

(10.4)

\begin{split}\|\mathrm{BR}_{R}f\|_{L^{p}}\gtrsim R^{\frac{1}{n}}+R^{\frac{n+1}{2p}+\frac{1}{np}}.\end{split}

So we have

(10.5)

C_{p}(R)\gtrsim 1+R^{\frac{1}{n}}R^{-\frac{n+1}{2p}-\frac{1}{np}}.

This gives the first and third terms on the right hand side of (
10.2
).

The second example can be constructed as follows. Let
\{a_{\theta}\}
be random signs.
Let
f=\sum_{\theta}a_{\theta}\sum_{T_{\theta}}f_{T_{\theta}}
where
f_{T_{\theta}}
is a wave packet with height one. We stack these planks so that for given
\theta
the union of the planks
\bigcup T_{\theta}
has dimension
R^{(n-1)/n}\times R^{(n-1)/n}\times R^{(n-1)/n}\times\cdots\times R^{(n-1)/n}\times R
. Here we take planks so that a translation of
\cup T_{\theta}
by
R
units along the longest direction intersects the origin. By computations, for given
\theta
,

(10.6)

\Big|\bigcup T_{\theta}\Big|\sim R^{\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{n}+1}\sim R^{n-1+1/n}.

Recall that the number of directions is comparable to
R^{1/n}
.
This gives the bound

(10.7)

\|f\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim R^{(n-1+\frac{2}{n})\frac{1}{p}}.

On the other hand, heuristically,
\mathrm{BR}_{R}(\sum_{T_{\theta}}f_{T_{\theta}})
is a wave packet with height comparable to one and essentially supported on
\bigcup 10T_{\theta}
.
By using the square root cancellation bound on
B_{R^{(n-1)/n}}
, we have

(10.8)

\begin{split}\|\mathrm{BR}_{R}f\|_{L^{p}}\gtrsim R^{\frac{n-1}{p}+\frac{1}{2n}}.\end{split}

This gives the lower bound

(10.9)

C_{p}(R)\gtrsim R^{\frac{n-1}{p}+\frac{1}{2n}}R^{-(n-1+\frac{2}{n})\frac{1}{p}}\sim R^{\frac{1}{n}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{p})}.

This gives the second bound on the right hand side of (
10.2
). This explains the lower bound of (
10.2
).

Appendix A

Recall the following result from
[
BGHS25
, Appendix D]
.

Lemma A.1
.

For
a\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
supported in an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(I)
, define the oscillatory integral

\mathcal{I}[\phi,a]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\phi(s)}a(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Let
r\geq 1
. Suppose that for each
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exist constants
C_{j}\geq 1
such that the following conditions hold on
\textup{supp}\ a
:

(1)

|\phi^{\prime}(s)|>0
,

(2)

|\phi^{(j)}(s)|\leq C_{j}r^{-(j-1)}|\phi^{\prime}(s)|^{j}
for all
j\geq 2
,

(3)

|a^{(j)}(s)|\leq C_{j}r^{-j}|\phi^{\prime}(s)|^{j}
for all
j\geq 0
.

Then for all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there exists some constant
C(N)
such that

|\mathcal{I}[\phi,a]|\leq C(N)\cdot|\textup{supp}\ a|\cdot r^{-N}.
